When proofs fail, it identifies whether the issue is in derivation, proof structure, or theorem formulation and routes accordingly.

Result: Evaluated on nine subproblems from five COLT 2026 open problems: two runs produced internally finalized theorem candidates matching source brief scope; seven yielded restricted-method results, special cases, or conditional theorems. The system successfully distinguishes between source-scope matches, relaxations, conditional results, and blocked attempts.

Conclusion: VALG demonstrates that autonomous agentic workflows can be effectively organized for ML theory research, maintaining mathematical rigor while adapting to obstacles. The system shows promise for accelerating theoretical ML research through automated theorem formulation and proof development.

Abstract: Machine learning theory studies learning procedures through mathematical setups in which the data model, training protocol, oracle access, loss, metric, and randomness define the phenomenon that a theorem is meant to explain. Solving an open problem therefore requires the problem formulation, theorem target, and proof mechanism to be developed in concert. Researchers formulate hypotheses, test them through preliminary theoretical or empirical analysis, and refine both assumptions and proofs. We investigate whether this process can be organized as an autonomous agentic workflow for ML theory research.
  We develop VALG, an agentic system that combines multi-level Verification, Adaptive formulation of Learning-theory problems, and Graph-structured proof development. Within each source-relative theorem branch, VALG maintains a fixed mathematical specification, checks the theorem-level composition of a typed proof-dependency graph, and constructs and reviews local proofs in dependency order. When a proof attempt fails, VALG identifies whether the obstruction lies in a derivation, the proof structure, or the theorem formulation and routes the next attempt accordingly. Formulation-level obstructions initiate an explicitly related variant or relaxation, preserving the mathematical relation between the resulting theorem and the source problem.
  We evaluate VALG on nine subproblems from five COLT 2026 open problems. Two runs produce internally finalized theorem candidates that match the scope of their source briefs; the remaining seven yield restricted-method results, special cases, or conditional theorems. These case studies show how VALG keeps source-scope matches, relaxations, conditional results, and blocked attempts mathematically distinct. VALG is open source at https://github.com/DechenZhang/VALG-ML-Theory-Agent.

</details>


### [52] [Uniform Herding: Exemplar Replay with Representation Refresh](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13061)
*Krishna Subedi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Uniform Herding improves class-incremental learning by refreshing exemplars in active memory with bounded candidate pools, outperforming iCaRL in accuracy and forgetting metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In class-incremental learning, feature representations change over time, making it challenging to preserve earlier classes with a bounded active exemplar set. Replay methods must maintain earlier class information while working within fixed memory constraints.

Method: Proposes Uniform Herding which allocates the active exemplar set across observed classes and uses a bounded candidate pool to refresh chosen exemplars in the current representation. The method maintains balanced exemplar selection while adapting to changing feature representations.

Result: On CIFAR-100 with ten class-incremental tasks using ResNet-18, Uniform Herding achieves 44.00±0.51% final average accuracy and 17.22±0.43% forgetting, outperforming iCaRL's 42.33±1.20% and 24.87±1.11%. Ablation studies show accuracy decreases when replacing NME or herding, and forgetting increases without distillation.

Conclusion: Uniform Herding effectively manages exemplar refreshment for class-incremental learning within bounded memory constraints. The approach demonstrates superior performance over iCaRL, though results are protocol-specific and the comparison doesn't isolate refresh effects from other protocol differences.

Abstract: As the feature representation changes, replay must preserve the earlier classes. However, only a bounded active exemplar set can be replayed. We propose Uniform Herding, which allocates the current active set across observed classes and uses a bounded candidate pool to refresh their chosen exemplars in the current representation. On CIFAR-100 with ten class-incremental tasks, a ResNet-18 backbone, active budget $M=2{,}000$, retrieval budget $b=64$, and three seeds, Uniform Herding obtains $44.00\pm0.51\%$ final average accuracy and $17.22\pm0.43\%$ forgetting, compared with $42.33\pm1.20\%$ and $24.87\pm1.11\%$ for iCaRL. Within the Uniform Herding protocol, final accuracy decreased when NME or herding was replaced with the tested alternatives, while forgetting increased when distillation was removed. Changing the retrieval budget has a smaller effect across the tested range than changing the active budget. The comparison with iCaRL is end-to-end. It does not isolate the effect of refresh from the other protocol differences. These results are limited to the tested protocol.

</details>


### [53] [Explanatory Engagement Under Rare Anomalous Failure: Asymptotic Rarity in Model Behavior (or: The Asymptotic AI)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13063)
*Sam Mao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Models show different explanatory engagement patterns when tool-call failures become rarer, with elicitation structure (how/when they're prompted to explain) significantly moderating whether predicted "engagement collapse" occurs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work only asks if models notice anomalies, but this paper asks a narrower question: as failures become asymptotically rarer in a workflow with low, controllable failure rates, does the model's explanatory engagement (length, specificity, confidence) change? They want to understand if there's a predicted "rise and collapse" pattern in engagement as failures become extremely rare.

Method: Built a local zero-cost harness testing three open-weight models (qwen3:8b, llama3.1:8b, mistral:7b) on repeated tool-call tasks with controlled failure probabilities swept across eight rates from 0.2 to 0.0001. Used five elicitation conditions: immediate prompting, grouped runs, passive unprompted, etc. Also ran a companion guaranteed-failure run to backfill rates where random sampling gave zero real failures.

Result: Pooled results initially seemed to show flat, monotonic decrease in explanation length, but splitting by condition revealed different patterns. Under immediate forced elicitation, length rose then plateaued (peaked at 28.4 words at p=0.05, settled at 17.4-19.0 words at rarest rates), with confidence rising unevenly. No collapse appeared in grouped runs. Passive unprompted revealed model-specific self-monitoring: llama3.1 volunteered structured confidence reports unprompted, sometimes eroding its own confidence over trials.

Conclusion: Elicitation structure is a first-class moderator of collapse observability. Models differ in whether they recognize an anomaly (distinct from engagement once recognized). The predicted "engagement collapse" pattern depends heavily on how and when models are prompted to explain failures. Limitation: discrete rate points can't capture behavior between them.

Abstract: Prior work on LLM behavior under anomalous conditions asks whether a model notices anomalies. We ask a narrower question: once a model sits in a workflow with a low, controllable failure rate, does its explanatory engagement - length, specificity, self-reported confidence - change as failure grows asymptotically rarer? We built a local, zero-cost harness on three open-weight models (qwen3:8b, llama3.1:8b, mistral:7b) running a repeated tool-call task where one call fails at probability p, swept across eight rates from 0.2 to 0.0001, under five elicitation conditions from immediate prompting to none. We hypothesized a rise in engagement as failures grew rarer, then a collapse near a detectability threshold. Pooled across conditions this appeared false: length fell in a flat, monotonic pattern. Splitting by condition overturned that. Under immediate_forced, where the model must explain every failure instantly, the predicted rise is confirmed but followed by a plateau, not a collapse: length peaks at 28.4 words at p=0.05, settles to 17.4-19.0 words at the rarest rates, and confidence rises unevenly from about 53% to the 70s-90s. Under grouped_runs, explanation batched to run-end, no collapse appears. Under passive_unprompted, aggregate magnitude is a floor artifact, but a recovered logging gap revealed real, model-specific self-monitoring: llama3.1:8b volunteers structured confidence reports unprompted, sometimes eroding its own confidence as trials accumulate; the other two do so only once, as boilerplate. Elicitation structure is a first-class moderator of collapse observability. A companion guaranteed-failure run (72 cells, backfilling rates where random sampling gave zero real failures) shows models differ in whether they recognize an anomaly, distinct from engagement once recognized. Limitation: discrete rate points cannot capture behavior between them, a direction for future work.

</details>


### [54] [Behavioral Reprogramming of Open-Weights Models: Cognitive Plasticity and Alignment Bounds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13069)
*Lucia Malíčková*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Researchers successfully reprogrammed LLMs to become proactive, question-asking assistants through precise fine-tuning techniques, achieving optimal results with LoRA rank 16 and 2-3 training epochs, with good cross-lingual transfer capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLMs are too passive and sycophantic; the researchers want to challenge this default paradigm by reprogramming models to become proactive, Socratic conversational agents that ask questions frequently.

Method: Used massive parallel HPC experiments (405 jobs) with hyperparameter sweeps to identify optimal PEFT settings. Found LoRA rank r=16 as architectural threshold, optimal training window of e∈[2,3] epochs. Applied Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) and conducted cross-lingual stress testing.

Result: Achieved minimum validation loss of 0.919, lower perplexity (1.414) with 14B parameter scaling. Successfully decoupled assertive behavior from syntax via DPO. Cross-lingual testing showed robust alignment in related languages but degradation in morphologically distant targets.

Conclusion: Established an empirical framework for compute-efficient, cross-lingual behavioral modification of LLMs, demonstrating that proactive conversational behaviors can be reliably engineered through precise fine-tuning within specific architectural boundaries.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are predominantly aligned to function as passive, sycophantic assistants. We challenge this default paradigm by empirically evaluating the cognitive plasticity of open-weight architectures when subjected to rigorous behavioral reprogramming. Our objective is to induce a proactive, Socratic conversational framework, characterized by high-frequency question generation under strictly constrained high-performance computing (HPC) conditions. Through a massively parallelized hyperparameter sweep comprising 405 HPC jobs, we define precise mathematical bounds for parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT). We identify an architectural threshold at LoRA rank $r=16$ and demonstrate via extensive epoch ablation that generalization capacity strictly reaches its optimal convergence within an optimized training window of $e \in [2, 3]$ depending on dataset density (minimum validation loss of 0.919). Furthermore, scaling model capacity to 14B parameters yielded a lower localized evaluation perplexity (1.414). Subsequent Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) successfully decoupled the underlying assertive behavior from localized syntax, while rigorous cross-lingual stress testing reveals both the capabilities and the structural boundaries of zero-shot persona transfer, demonstrating robust alignment in closely related linguistic families alongside identifiable degradation pathways in morphologically distant targets. These findings establish a rigorous empirical framework for compute-efficient, cross-lingual behavioral modification.

</details>


### [55] [EEG-PRIME: Prototype-Aligned Representation Learning with Multi-Level Conditioning for EEG Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13072)
*Shuailei Zhang,Muyun Jiang,Wei Zhang,Jinbo Chen,Zhiwei Guo,Yong Li,Yi Ding,Cuntai Guan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EEG-PRIME is a two-stage EEG foundation model combining masked pretraining with prototype-aligned instruction tuning to enable cross-dataset, cross-subject, and cross-task EEG decoding with zero-shot transfer capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EEG decoding models suffer from poor generalization across datasets and subjects due to domain shifts caused by differences in acquisition protocols and individual neurophysiology. Existing methods struggle with cross-dataset transfer and multi-task learning in EEG-based applications.

Method: Two-stage approach: 1) Masked pretraining where an EEG encoder learns transferable representations through masked reconstruction with frequency-cutoff spectral augmentation. 2) Instruction tuning incorporating task-semantic, dataset-specific, and subject-invariant conditioning using Layer-wise Query Modulation and frozen text embeddings as prototypes for cosine-similarity prediction.

Result: Experiments on sixteen datasets across five tasks (motor imagery, emotion recognition, ADHD detection, covert speech, mental workload) show consistent improvements over state-of-the-art baselines and prior EEG foundation models. On two held-out datasets, achieves balanced accuracy comparable to within-session calibration models without target-domain optimization, calibration, or linear probing.

Conclusion: EEG-PRIME demonstrates promising zero-shot transfer capability and addresses the critical challenge of domain generalization in EEG decoding, enabling instruction-aware and subject-invariant decoding across diverse BCI paradigms without extensive target-domain adaptation.

Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) decoding models often generalize poorly across datasets and subjects due to domain shifts in acquisition protocols and individual neurophysiology. We propose EEG-PRIME, a two-stage EEG foundation model for cross-dataset multi-task decoding. EEG-PRIME combines masked pretraining with prototype-aligned instruction tuning to enable instruction-aware and subject-invariant decoding across diverse BCI paradigms. During pretraining, an EEG encoder learns transferable representations through masked reconstruction with frequency-cutoff spectral augmentation. During instruction tuning, EEG-PRIME incorporates task-semantic, dataset-specific, and subject-invariant conditioning. The resulting conditioning signal modulates the Q-Former through Layer-wise Query Modulation, while frozen text embeddings of class labels serve as prototypes for cosine-similarity-based prediction across heterogeneous label spaces. Experiments on sixteen datasets covering motor imagery, emotion recognition, ADHD detection, covert speech, and mental workload show consistent improvements over state-of-the-art baselines and prior EEG foundation models under cross-subject settings. On two additional held-out datasets, EEG-PRIME achieves balanced accuracy comparable to within-session calibration models without target-domain optimization, calibration, or linear probing, demonstrating promising zero-shot transfer capability.

</details>


### [56] [SPADE: Speculative Decoding for Precise and Low Cost Distributed Edge Cloud Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13076)
*Divya Jyoti Bajpai,Kishan Kumar Upadhyay,Manjesh Kumar Hanawal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Edge-cloud collaborative speculative decoding framework that reduces cloud LLM calls by 76% with zero accuracy loss by having edge draft models generate tokens and cloud verifiers validate them.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs have high computational demands making deployment challenging. Direct edge deployment of smaller models sacrifices accuracy, while cloud deployment of big models is expensive per-token. Need a solution that maintains big model accuracy while reducing cloud costs.

Method: Distributed inference framework integrating speculative decoding across edge and cloud. Compact draft model on edge generates candidate tokens rapidly. Large verifier model on cloud validates tokens in parallel. Accepted tokens are retained, rejections trigger verifier correction. Plug-and-play design shifts bulk of computation to edge.

Result: Reduces cloud model calls by 76% with zero accuracy loss compared to full model. Experimental results across multiple NLP tasks using SpecBench and CNN/Dailymail datasets show practical path to scalable, cost-efficient deployment.

Conclusion: The framework demonstrates practical path toward scalable, cost-efficient, and accurate deployment of LLMs in real-world environments by distributing inference across edge and cloud while preserving big model accuracy without retraining requirements.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable success in natural language understanding and generation, but their deployment is constrained by high computational demands. Deploying smaller LLMs directly on the edge can circumvent this, but with degraded accuracy. Deploying smaller cloud-based big LLMs preserves performance, but at the cost of expensive per-token computation. We present a distributed inference framework, \our{}, that integrates speculative decoding (SD) across edge and cloud. A compact draft model deployed on the edge generates candidate tokens rapidly, and a large verifier model on the cloud validates these tokens in parallel. Accepted tokens are retained, while only rejections trigger verifier correction, substantially reducing the number of cloud queries. Our plug-and-play design shifts the bulk of computation to the edge, significantly lowers inference time and cloud cost, and preserves the accuracy of the big model without any retraining requirement. Our approach demonstrates a practical path toward scalable, cost-efficient, and accurate deployment of LLMs in real-world environments. Experimental results across multiple Natural Language Processing tasks using SpecBench and CNN/Dailymail datasets demonstrate that \our{} reduces the cloud model calls by $76\%$ with zero loss in accuracy as compared to the full model.

</details>


### [57] [Multi-Layer Context Camouflaging: A Semantic Superposition and Contextual Lamination Framework for Malpractice-Resilient Online Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13100)
*Gupta Lovi Raj,Kaur Kamalpreet,Dama Sri Ram,Parani Prajithaa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A mathematical framework called Multi-Layer Context Camouflaging Theory (MCCT) protects online assessment content by superimposing semantic camouflage that's only recoverable by legitimate users, preventing unauthorized extraction while maintaining accessibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current online assessment security systems (browser lockdown, webcam monitoring, behavioral analytics) are vulnerable to content extraction attacks through screenshots, screen sharing, OCR, and automated scraping, creating a need for content-level protection.

Method: Extends MSCCM/MARS with MCCT framework using semantic superposition where authentic content and synthetic camouflage are rendered as a unified display. Models adversarial extraction through extraction-channel operator with six constructs: Context Inversion Operator, Contextual Lamination Operator, Separation Channel, Human Readability Functional, Computational Ambiguity Functional, and Context Camouflage Tensor. Uses conditional entropy for computational ambiguity quantification.

Result: Develops a closed-form expression quantifying uncertainty during unauthorized extraction while guaranteeing legitimate recovery through exact filtering identity. Establishes theoretical properties for ambiguity, camouflage density, semantic preservation, multi-observation leakage, and temporal multiplexing. Includes rendering algorithm and pre-registered evaluation protocol.

Conclusion: MCCT provides a mathematically rigorous foundation for behaviorally adaptive, accessibility-aware, and computationally resilient digital assessment by securing rendered content at the semantic level while preserving readability for legitimate users.

Abstract: Contemporary online assessment systems rely primarily on browser lockdown, webcam monitoring, and behavioural analytics, yet remain vulnerable to attacks that extract the assessment content itself through screenshots, screen sharing, optical character recognition, and automated scraping. This paper extends the Multi-dimensional Spatio-Temporal Context Camouflaging Model (MSCCM) within the MARS (Multi-modal Assessment Resilience Suite) by introducing the Multi-Layer Context Camouflaging Theory (MCCT), a mathematical framework that protects rendered assessment content through semantic superposition. Authentic assessment content and synthetically generated camouflage are represented as a unified rendering while remaining recoverable only by legitimate candidates. The framework models the adversarial extraction process through an explicit extraction-channel operator and develops six coupled constructs: the Context Inversion Operator, Contextual Lamination Operator, Separation Channel, Human Readability Functional, Computational Ambiguity Functional, and Context Camouflage Tensor. Computational ambiguity is formulated using conditional entropy, yielding a closed-form expression that quantifies uncertainty during unauthorized extraction, while legitimate recovery is guaranteed through an exact filtering identity. We further establish theoretical properties governing ambiguity, camouflage density, semantic preservation, multi-observation leakage, and temporal multiplexing, and present a rendering algorithm with computational complexity and a pre-registered evaluation protocol. MCCT provides a mathematically rigorous foundation for behaviorally adaptive, accessibility-aware, and computationally resilient digital assessment by securing rendered assessment content while preserving readability for legitimate users.

</details>


### [58] [Robust Dempster-Shafer Evidence Fusion with Chaos-Conflict Measurement and Historical-Experience Weighting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13108)
*Huiyu Li,Weibo Liu,Xinru Xu,Dongchen Gao,Meng Zhang,Junhua Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A unified evidence fusion framework combining chaos-conflict measurement with historical reliability weighting, outperforming existing methods on 16 benchmark datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Dempster-Shafer theory approaches have two key limitations: incomplete evaluation of evidence (separate treatment of inter-evidence conflict and intra-evidence uncertainty), and lack of long-term reliability assessment using only instantaneous comparisons without historical context.

Method: 1) Chaos-conflict measurement jointly quantifies cross-evidence conflict and intra-evidence non-specificity with five proven properties. 2) Historical experience driven weighting using spectral clustering to partition decision space and regret theory to compute context-specific reliability from past outcomes. 3) Hybrid combination rule adaptively balancing uncertainty preservation vs. weighted consensus. 4) Belief-interval decision strategy preserving epistemic uncertainty.

Result: Achieved average F1 score of 85.78% and mean AUC of 93.30% on 16 real-world benchmark datasets, outperforming eight DST-based baselines and three gradient boosting methods. Ablation analysis confirmed contribution of each proposed component.

Conclusion: The proposed unified evidence reasoning framework effectively addresses limitations of existing methods, offering an adaptive approach for multi-source decision making by integrating comprehensive evidence assessment with historical reliability learning.

Abstract: Multi-source evidence fusion under Dempster-Shafer theory faces two persistent challenges: existing conflict measures assess inter-evidence inconsistency and intra-evidence uncertainty independently, yielding incomplete evaluations, and current fusion methods evaluate evidence sources exclusively through instantaneous comparisns without exploiting their long-term reliability across diverse decision contexts. This paper proposes a unified evidence reasoning framework that addresses both limitations. Specifically, a chaos-conflict measurement is introduced to jointly quantify cross-evidence conflict and intra-evidence non-specificity, with five formally proven properties ensuring consistent assessment. A historical experience driven weighting scheme partitions the decision space via spectral clustering and applies regret theory to compute context-specific reliability profiles from past fusion outcomes. These mechanisms feed into a hybrid combination rule that adaptively balances uncertainty preservation against weighted consensus, controlled by the global conflict level, followed by a belief-interval decision strategy that enables robust classification without discarding epistemic uncertainty. Experiments on 16 real-world benchmark datasets demonstrate that the proposed framework achieves an average F1 score of 85.78 and a mean AUC of 93.30, outperforming eight DST-based baselines and three gradient boosting methods. Ablation analysis confirms the contribution of each component we proposed. The framework offers an effective approach for adaptive evidence fusion in multi-source decision making.

</details>


### [59] [SkillEvo: Self-Renewing Evolution Gradients from Multi-Turn Interaction Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13120)
*Qianxi Yan,Chunrong Chen,Jiuzhou Zhao,Min Zhang,Yongzhou Xu,Xiaochuan Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillEvo introduces a multi-turn evolution system where feedback from simulated user interactions and active governance repair drives continuous skill improvement, overcoming the limitations of single-turn evaluation approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current agent skill development lacks closed-loop improvement mechanisms. Single-turn evaluation creates an asymmetry where evolution stalls after fixing immediate issues, leaving multi-turn defects invisible. Governance systems using scalar scores can only reject degraded candidates but cannot localize or repair structural problems.

Method: SkillEvo combines two components: 1) Multi-turn user simulation that generates trustworthy feedback by exposing defects layer by layer through follow-up questions, creating continuous evolution gradients; 2) An independent governance layer that actively repairs factual degradation and structural bloat rather than just rejecting candidates.

Result: Across six cloud service categories, 9 production Skills, and 98 skill-reference files, SkillEvo outperformed self-reflection-based evolution by 23.0 points and single-turn-QA-driven evolution by 15.4 points.

Conclusion: The key constraint for sustained skill evolution is not editing capability or iteration count, but whether evaluation feedback provides trustworthy evolution gradients. SkillEvo addresses this through multi-turn feedback generation and active governance repair, enabling continuous improvement beyond single-turn limitations.

Abstract: Agent Skills are today either hand-authored or produced in a single LLM generation pass, and consequently possess no closed loop through which they might improve from the interaction failures they actually cause. Recent work does close this loop, but derives its feedback from single-turn question-answering evaluation. The consequence is a sharp asymmetry: once the first round has patched the gaps that a single exchange can reveal, the evolution gradient decays, the defects that surface only across multiple turns remain invisible, and evolution stalls. Governance in these systems is likewise driven by an end-to-end verification score, a scalar gate that can reject a degraded candidate but can neither localize nor repair its structural cause. We argue that the binding constraint on sustained skill evolution is neither editing capability nor the number of iterations, but whether the evaluation feedback keeps supplying trustworthy evolution gradients. We introduce SkillEvo, in which trustworthy feedback generates the gradient and controllable governance constrains its direction. The first component recasts multi-turn user simulation from an evaluation endpoint into a feedback generator: follow-up questions expose defects layer by layer, so that every round of revision both consumes feedback and produces new feedback. The second replaces the passive rejection of a scalar gate with an independent governance layer that actively repairs factual degradation and structural bloat, preventing the gradient from drifting as degradation accumulates. Across six categories of cloud services, 9 production Skills, and 98 skill-reference files, SkillEvo surpasses self-reflection-based evolution by 23.0 points and single- turn-QA-driven evolution by 15.4 points.

</details>


### [60] [Numeracy in Large Language Models: Fundamental Limitations and Paths to Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13129)
*Aoxin Ni*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper examines the gap between LLMs' mathematical reasoning capabilities and their basic numerical understanding, proposing the Numerical Grounding Framework to decompose numeracy into representational and procedural components, with practical recommendations for improving numerical reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models perform well on mathematical reasoning benchmarks but remain unreliable on elementary numerical tasks (magnitude comparison, large-integer arithmetic, fractions, scientific notation), creating a need to understand and address this fundamental numeracy gap.

Method: The authors propose the Numerical Grounding Framework (NGF) which decomposes numeracy into two components: Representational Grounding (RG) for mapping numeral forms to values and magnitudes, and Procedural Grounding (PG) for executing arithmetic operations. They use NGF to organize existing research, analyze structural explanations, and conduct coordinated evaluations of frontier models across multiple benchmarks.

Result: The survey identifies that architectural interventions like digit-aware tokenization and Abacus Embeddings can help models trained from scratch, but for pretrained models, supervised fine-tuning, reasoning scaffolds, and external tools are more practical solutions. The framework successfully organizes diagnostic benchmarks, failure modes, and mitigation strategies across different model families.

Conclusion: The paper concludes with deployment recommendations and research directions for improving numerical reliability in foundation models, emphasizing the distinction between basic numerical understanding and high-level mathematical reasoning as separate capabilities that require different approaches for improvement.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) achieve strong results on mathematical reasoning benchmarks yet remain unreliable on elementary numerical tasks, including magnitude comparison, large-integer arithmetic, fractions, and scientific notation. This survey examines basic numerical understanding as a capability distinct from high-level mathematical reasoning. We propose the Numerical Grounding Framework (NGF), which decomposes numeracy into Representational Grounding (RG), mapping numeral forms to value, magnitude, and equivalent representations, and Procedural Grounding (PG), executing arithmetic operations in accordance with their mathematical definitions. Using NGF, we organize recent diagnostic benchmarks, failure modes, structural explanations, and mitigation strategies. We review evidence concerning tokenization, positional encoding, embedding geometry, and pretraining-data distribution. We also apply NGF in a coordinated evaluation of three frontier model families across Number Cookbook, NumericBench, and GSM-Symbolic, comparing atomic, contextual, and reasoning-assisted numeracy. Architectural interventions such as digit-aware tokenization and Abacus Embeddings can improve models trained from scratch but are generally unavailable to users of pretrained systems, for whom supervised fine-tuning, reasoning scaffolds, and external tools are more practical. We conclude with deployment recommendations and research directions for more reliable numerical behavior in foundation models.

</details>


### [61] [Rethinking Normalization Placement for LLMs: Post-Norm under Curriculum Depth Growing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13156)
*Sheng Ren,Yadong Wang,Naiqiang Tan,Jiangang Kong,Jun Fang,Rui Liu,Jun Wang,Kai Chen,Lipeng Liang,Xiang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Pre-norm is standard in Transformers, but post-norm performs better when using curriculum depth growth in knowledge distillation, with the advantage emerging after new blocks are appended.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates whether the standard preference for pre-norm normalization persists when depth is introduced through curriculum learning. Since curriculum depth growth means each new block receives boundary representations from previously trained blocks, normalization placement becomes relevant for forward conditioning.

Method: Conducted controlled distillation experiments with a Qwen3-8B teacher and nine-layer student. Compared pre-norm vs post-norm under two training regimes: joint training (all blocks trained together) and curriculum growth (blocks appended sequentially). Used various controls including post-joint training matched by active-layer tokens, single-block experiments, and freeze controls. Performed boundary diagnostics to analyze residual scales and structural properties.

Result: Under joint training, pre-norm and post-norm perform similarly (difference of 0.0004 validation CE). However, under curriculum growth, post-norm outperforms pre-norm by 0.0328 validation CE - an order of magnitude larger difference. The advantage emerges during curriculum progression: post-norm takes the lead once blocks are appended. Post-joint control remained worse than post-grow, ruling out compute differences. Diagnostics show post-norm has stable residual scales while pre-norm suffers from structural-token scale drift.

Conclusion: Normalization placement and training curriculum should be treated as coupled design choices in distillation settings. Post-norm works better with curriculum depth growth due to better boundary-scale conditioning when new blocks are appended, addressing issues of scale drift in pre-norm.

Abstract: Pre-norm is the standard normalization placement in modern Transformers because it facilitates joint optimization of full-depth models. We ask whether this preference persists when depth is introduced through a curriculum. In curriculum depth growth, each appended block receives the boundary representation produced by a trained prefix, making normalization placement relevant to forward conditioning. We therefore test whether placement and training curriculum interact. In a controlled distillation study with a Qwen3-8B teacher and a nine-layer student, pre-norm and post-norm are indistinguishable under joint training, differing by $0.0004$ validation CE, while post-norm improves over pre-norm by $0.0328$ under curriculum growth, an order of magnitude larger. A post-joint control matched by student active-layer tokens remains worse than post-grow, which rules out compute as the sole explanation. The ranking crosses over during the curriculum: post-norm takes the lead once blocks are appended. Single-block and freeze controls localize the ranking change to block appending rather than shallow-block quality or retraining. Boundary diagnostics associate post-norm with stable residual scales and pre-norm with structural-token scale drift; on a fixed batch, the final pre-grow block is also nearly identity-mapped. Together with the phase-wise crossover, these observations are consistent with boundary-scale conditioning after new blocks are appended. The results motivate treating normalization placement and training curriculum as coupled design choices in this distillation setting.

</details>


### [62] [SkillShapley: Boundary-Adaptive Shapley Valuation for Skill Step Attribution in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13173)
*Chang Liu,Yuqi Zhang,Yiman Zhong,Boyi Liu,Hengjun Wang,Shuyue Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillShapley is a Shapley value-based framework for quantifying the contribution of individual steps in language agent skills, addressing the challenge of skill-step attribution that existing methods lack.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agent skills are created manually or from execution traces without understanding how each step contributes to overall performance, creating an open problem in quantifying individual step contributions within skills.

Method: Models skill-step attribution as a Shapley value-based contribution estimation problem, with a two-phase approach: first identifies informative coalitional regions, then adaptively samples new coalitions yielding reusable marginal evidence, motivated by empirical insights about discretized benchmark rewards and additive step interactions.

Result: Experiments on SkillsBench skills show that SkillShapley effectively and efficiently identifies high- or low-value skill steps, providing key takeaways for agent skill creation.

Conclusion: The proposed SkillShapley framework successfully addresses the skill-step attribution problem, offering a principled approach to understanding individual step contributions in agent skills, which can inform better skill creation practices.

Abstract: Agent skills are crucial external instructions that enable language agents to execute long procedural tasks such as coding or document processing. Existing agent skills are primarily created through human manual crafting or agent execution traces, with limited understanding of how each step contributes to overall skill performance on specific tasks; i.e., there remains an open problem in quantifying the contribution of individual steps within an agent skill. To address this issue, we first model skill-step attribution as a Shapley value-based contribution estimation problem, and then propose SkillShapley, a step-level attribution framework for agent skills. Notably, SkillShapley operates in two phases, motivated by key empirical insights, i.e., discretized benchmark rewards that create sharp performance cliffs, and step interactions that are largely additive rather than synergistic. Specifically, it first identifies informative coalitional regions, and then adaptively samples new coalitions that can yield reusable marginal evidence. Experiments on skills from the widely adopted SkillsBench demonstrate that our SkillShapley can effectively and efficiently identify high- or low-value skill steps, providing several key takeaways for agent skill creation.

</details>


### [63] [Teach the Magnitude, Not the Direction: Verifier-Bounded Credit Assignment for Multi-Turn Multi-step LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13179)
*Zechuan Wang,Siyuan Lu,Hongxuan Zhang,Linjian Mo,Chenyi Zhuang,Leilei Gan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CrEST is a hierarchical credit assignment framework that combines reinforcement learning's verifier-bounded performance with dense token-level supervision from a self-teacher, outperforming both RL and distillation methods on multi-turn tool-use tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods for training multi-turn tool-use agents have limitations: RLVR provides verifier-bounded performance but suffers from trajectory-level credit assignment that conflates heterogeneous per-turn outcomes, while on-policy distillation offers dense supervision but is either teacher-bounded or prone to gradient collapse.

Method: CrEST introduces a hierarchical credit assignment framework with two levels: (1) turn-segmented verified advantages to address inter-turn dilution, and (2) entropy-gated self-teacher modulation to refine intra-turn token contributions. It retains RL's verifier-bounded ceiling while incorporating dense token-level signals from a privileged self-teacher.

Result: Experiments on BFCL V3 and WildToolBench show that CrEST consistently outperforms both RL and distillation baselines across two model scales, with the largest gains on long-trajectory and strict session-level metrics.

Conclusion: CrEST demonstrates that a teacher's role in policy optimization can be reduced from determining update directions to modulating update magnitudes, unlocking dense credit assignment without sacrificing the verifier-bounded ceiling, representing an important advancement for training robust multi-turn tool-use agents.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) offers a verifier-bounded performance ceiling for training multi-turn tool-use agents, yet its trajectory-level credit assignment conflates heterogeneous per-turn outcomes into a single reward signal. On-policy distillation provides dense per-token supervision but is either teacher-bounded or prone to gradient concentration collapse. We introduce $\textbf{CrEST}$, a hierarchical credit assignment framework that retains RL's verifier-bounded ceiling while incorporating dense token-level signals from a privileged self-teacher. $\textbf{CrEST}$ resolves credit at two levels: turn-segmented verified advantages address inter-turn dilution, while entropy-gated self-teacher modulation refines intra-turn token contributions. Experiments on BFCL V3 and WildToolBench show that $\textbf{CrEST}$ consistently outperforms both RL and distillation baselines across two model scales, with the largest gains on long-trajectory and strict session-level metrics. Our work demonstrates that the teacher's role in policy optimization can be reduced from determining update directions to modulating update magnitudes, unlocking dense credit assignment without sacrificing the verifier-bounded ceiling.

</details>


### [64] [TsuGO: Probing Search Efficiency in LLM Reasoning via Go Life-and-Death Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13221)
*Shunwen Bai,Ziping Ma,Chaoyang Zhang,Yarong Wang,Jiale Liu,Zhen Qin,Qingpei Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TsuGO is a new benchmark that evaluates LLM reasoning search efficiency using Go tsumego problems, focusing on how models organize search strategies rather than just final answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM reasoning evaluation focuses on final-answer accuracy or basic process coherence, but fails to measure how models plan reasoning paths and allocate reasoning resources—specifically, how they organize search strategies. Existing methods don't capture search organization, as most benchmark tasks are solvable through static capabilities without requiring complex search.

Method: TsuGO uses Go life-and-death problems (tsumego) which provide closed, verifiable solution spaces with adversarial structure, requiring candidate generation, response checking, branch comparison, and backtracking. The benchmark constrains the solution space to disentangle domain knowledge from search organization, parses chain-of-thought into structured search trees, and reports metrics including Search Efficiency and Token Efficiency.

Result: Experiments show current LLMs are far from stable tsumego solving: stronger models succeed by finding correct candidates earlier and sustaining effort on productive branches, but most models behave closer to unguided search algorithms than to neural-guided KataGo. Longer chain-of-thought or higher token efficiency doesn't necessarily imply better search performance.

Conclusion: Search organization and reasoning-resource allocation are identified as missing dimensions in LLM reasoning evaluation. TsuGO provides a framework to measure these aspects through structured analysis of search efficiency in constrained problem spaces.

Abstract: The evaluation of LLM reasoning is moving from final-answer accuracy to process-level assessment, yet existing methods still fail to capture how models plan reasoning paths and allocate reasoning resources--that is, how they organize search. Prior process-level methods focus on the coherence and redundancy of chain-of-thought (CoT), and most benchmark tasks have a single objective solvable by static capabilities such as derivation and tool use, leaving search organization unmeasured. We introduce TsuGO, a process-level reasoning benchmark for evaluating Search Efficiency in LLM reasoning through Go life-and-death problems. These problems provide closed and verifiable solution spaces with an inherent adversarial structure, making candidate generation, response checking, branch comparison, and backtracking necessary parts of reasoning rather than incidental trace patterns. By constraining the solution space, TsuGO disentangles domain knowledge from search organization, parses CoT into a structured search tree, and reports Search Efficiency together with Token Efficiency and other diagnostic metrics and visualizations. Experiments show that current LLMs remain far from stable tsumego solving: stronger models succeed by finding the correct candidate earlier and sustaining effort on productive branches, but most models still behave much closer to unguided search algorithms than to neural-guided KataGo. Longer CoT or higher Token Efficiency does not necessarily imply better search. Our results identify search organization and reasoning-resource allocation as missing dimensions in LLM reasoning evaluation.

</details>


### [65] [Capability Sheaves for Compositional Agent-Harness Repair: Controlled Quotients and a Real-Repository Stress Test](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13228)
*Saveliy Batruin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Researchers model agent disagreement using "capability sheaves" from algebraic topology, apply this to software bug fixing, and find the method matches but doesn't outperform exact reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent-based systems (like retrieval, routing, state management) can have locally successful components that disagree on shared state, leading to coordination failures that need systematic modeling and diagnosis.

Method: 1. Model agent disagreement using finite "capability sheaves" (stalks = typed behavior signatures, restriction maps = shared fields).
2. Frame acceptance as exact finite constraint-satisfaction problem (CSP).
3. Use linearized relative cohomology classes for diagnosis and search.
4. Test on controlled experiments (20 task clusters with hidden mediators) and real-world SWE-bench software repository issues.

Result: 1. Controlled experiments: Quotienting coboundaries reduces candidate budget from 2,000 to 1,000 per cluster.
2. SWE-bench evaluation: Method resolves 118 issues vs 116 for baseline (not statistically significant). Leave-one-repository-out abstention gate reaches 127/160 issues, tying best baseline.
3. Key finding: Exact CSP matches the quotient method, showing invariance to stale representatives rather than superiority over exact reasoning.

Conclusion: The capability sheaf approach successfully models agent coordination failures and demonstrates invariance to stale state representations, but does not provide real-world advantage over exact reasoning methods. The method supports the invariance mechanism but fails to show cohomological advantage in practical applications.

Abstract: Agent harnesses combine retrieval, routing, state, provenance, and verification, but locally successful components may disagree on shared state. We model this failure with a finite \emph{capability sheaf}: stalks encode typed behavior signatures, restriction maps retain shared fields, and accepted runs are useful global sections. An exact finite constraint-satisfaction problem (CSP) defines acceptance, while a linearized relative cohomology class provides a diagnostic and search feature.
  A controlled experiment over 20 task clusters introduces hidden interior mediators whose raw states are nuisance variables. Quotienting their coboundaries reduces the candidate budget from 2,000 to 1,000 per cluster; aligning the hidden state removes the gap. Exact CSP matches the quotient, so the result demonstrates invariance to stale representatives, not superiority over exact reasoning.
  We then test the method on a discovery split from the SWE-bench Multilingual pool of PatchFuseBench: 160 issues from 20 repositories, 875 real candidate patches, 2,579 source-aware edit atoms, and 153 newly executed patches. A first pool-level construction is constant because $[b-Dx]=[b]$ in $\operatorname{coker}D$ and therefore cannot rank configurations. A candidate-indexed repair is nontrivial on 848/875 candidates and varies within 120/160 issues. It resolves 118 issues versus 116 for a matched noncohomological selector, but the difference is not supported across repositories (exact sign-flip $p=0.75$). A leave-one-repository-out abstention gate reaches 127/160, tying the strong anchor and exceeding its matched gate by one issue ($p=1.0$). The discovery gate therefore fails and the confirmatory split remains sealed. The study supports the controlled invariance mechanism and an identifiability correction, but not a real-world cohomological advantage.

</details>


### [66] [vToken: Token-Level Virtualization for Reclaimable KV Caches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13263)
*Yuanhang Gao,Xiangrui Yang,Yuanfeng Chen,Hongjia Chen,Qianru Lv,Wenfei Wu,Dongsheng Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: vToken introduces a token-level virtualization layer to reduce KV cache memory fragmentation in LLM serving by decoupling logical token liveness from physical block placement, enabling better memory reclamation while maintaining compatibility with existing systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language model serving faces critical memory bottlenecks from KV cache growth. While PagedAttention reduces allocator-level fragmentation, current KV eviction algorithms operate at token granularity, creating a mismatch with block-level management that leaves allocated KV memory unreclaimable due to intra-block fragmentation.

Method: vToken is a lightweight token-level virtualization layer that decouples logical token liveness from physical block placement. It maintains a stable logical token view through token-table indirection and realizes physical reclamation by asynchronously repacking live tokens. The design preserves PagedAttention kernels and CUDA Graph compatibility.

Result: Implemented in vLLM and evaluated with H2O, Random, and Scissorhands across models. Compared with Naive-Evict baseline, vToken reduces retained KV blocks per request by 27.2%-72.3% and improves SLA-constrained throughput by up to 1.37×. Under constrained active-KV budget, it extends maximum feasible concurrency by up to 2× while reducing per-policy integration footprint from 500+ to under 50 lines.

Conclusion: vToken effectively addresses the KV cache memory bottleneck in LLM serving by introducing token-level virtualization that eliminates intra-block fragmentation, significantly improves memory efficiency and throughput, and reduces system complexity for KV eviction policy integration.

Abstract: Large language model serving faces a critical memory bottleneck: the KV cache grows with sequence length and batch size. PagedAttention uses fixed-size memory blocks to reduce allocator-level fragmentation, but recent KV eviction algorithms operate at a token granularity finer than block-level management. This mismatch causes intra-block fragmentation, leaving a large fraction of allocated KV memory unreclaimable. We present vToken, a lightweight token-level virtualization layer that decouples logical token liveness from physical block placement. vToken maintains a stable logical token view through token-table indirection and realizes physical reclamation by repacking live tokens asynchronously. The design preserves PagedAttention kernels and CUDA Graph compatibility. We implement vToken in vLLM and evaluate it with H2O, Random, and Scissorhands across models. Compared with a paired Naive-Evict baseline, vToken reduces retained KV blocks per request by 27.2\%--72.3\% and improves SLA-constrained throughput by up to 1.37$\times$. Under a constrained active-KV budget, it extends the maximum feasible concurrency by up to 2$\times$, while reducing the per-policy integration footprint from 500+ lines to under 50.

</details>


### [67] [Sovereign by necessity? Frontier AI export controls, cyber security, and the limits of national AI capability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13272)
*Alan Woodward,Andrew Rogoyski*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Frontier AI models are controlled by just two countries, with the US restricting foreign access. This affects national cyber defense, as sovereignty over AI access is increasingly crucial but limited for most states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The concentration of frontier AI development in two states, recent US export restrictions on advanced AI models, and evidence that frontier models change the economics of cyber warfare create concerns about national sovereignty and cyber defense capabilities for most countries.

Method: The article examines the interaction between US export restrictions on frontier AI models and the changing cyber defense landscape, analyzing market dynamics, training costs, computing power concentration, and national AI programs to assess realistic sovereignty options.

Result: Access to frontier AI is becoming a critical component of national cyber defense, but this access can be revoked by controlling states, leaving most countries vulnerable. Sovereign AI capability remains largely infeasible for all but a handful of nations.

Conclusion: The article proposes a layered strategy including negotiated access guarantees, inference-level sovereignty, open-weight hedging, regional capability pooling, talent development, and cyber resilience investment, noting that deployment and containment risks matter more than apparent performance.

Abstract: A small number of firms based in two states produce the most capable frontier AI models. The governments of those states have shown both the legal power and the political will to decide which other countries may use these systems. In June 2026 the United States required a leading developer to obtain licences before releasing its most advanced models to any foreign person, including foreign nationals resident in the United States. The affected models were withdrawn worldwide at short notice, partly because the restriction proved impractical to administer. This followed within months of the first documented case of a largely autonomous, AI-run cyber espionage campaign, and coincided with mounting evidence that frontier models alter the economics of both cyber attack and cyber defence. This article examines how these two developments interact, and situates them within the unusual market dynamics now driving large-scale AI development. It argues that access to frontier AI is becoming part of national cyber defence, that such access can be revoked, and that the obvious remedy of sovereign capability remains only partly feasible for all but a handful of states. Drawing on evidence about training costs, the concentration of computing power and the support offered by national AI programmes, it asks what sovereignty can realistically mean for small and middle powers, and for large powers as well. The article proposes a layered strategy: negotiated access guarantees, sovereignty at the level of inference, hedging with open-weight models, pooled regional capability, sustained talent development and continued investment in basic cyber resilience. The open-weight hedge proves at once more capable and more politically exposed than is commonly assumed. Much of the near-term risk lies in how capable models are deployed and contained rather than in their apparent performance.

</details>


### [68] [Towards Context-Aware Clinical Motion Understanding in Daily Living at Home: Freezing of Gait Detection with Egocentric Vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13283)
*Vayalet Stefanova,Diwas Lamsal,Margot Genbrugge,Maxim Yudayev,Christian Schlenstedt,Moran Gilat,Bart Vanrumste,Benjamin Filtjens*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Combining egocentric video with wearable IMUs improves detection of freezing of gait in Parkinson's disease, with IMU-based models outperforming video alone but video providing valuable contextual information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motion analysis needs contextual understanding beyond just kinematics, as similar inertial patterns in daily activities can represent different scenarios - intentional stopping, object interaction, or pathological impairment like freezing of gait. Egocentric vision could provide the missing task-related context to disambiguate these cases.

Method: Used synchronized egocentric video, wearable IMUs, and expert-annotated FOG labels from 13 Parkinson's patients in home environments. Evaluated frozen representations from pretrained ego-video foundation models (V-JEPA2) and time-series foundation models, compared with an IMU-based Temporal Convolutional Network (TCN) trained from scratch. Used leave-one-subject-out evaluation for testing.

Result: IMU-based TCN achieved best performance: 42.3 F1 score and 83.0 AUROC. Ego-video features (V-JEPA2) reached 32.6 F1 and 77.2 AUROC. While video alone didn't outperform IMUs, it showed above-chance discrimination and qualitative analysis suggested video captures FOG-relevant information independent of IMUs.

Conclusion: Pretrained ego-video representations can add valuable contextual information to complement wearable-sensor-based motion understanding for clinical applications in daily living settings, supporting a multimodal approach for improved detection of freezing of gait.

Abstract: Understanding motion in daily living requires context beyond kinematics, because similar inertial patterns during activities of daily living (ADLs) can reflect intentional stopping, object interaction, or pathological movement impairment. Egocentric vision provides task-related context that may help disambiguate these cases. We investigate this challenge through freezing of gait (FOG) detection in Parkinson's disease (PD), a symptom strongly influenced by contextual factors during ADLs. Using synchronized egocentric video, wearable IMUs, and expert-annotated FOG labels collected from 13 PD participants in their homes, we evaluate frozen representations from pretrained ego-video and time-series foundation models, alongside an IMU-based TCN trained from scratch, under leave-one-subject-out evaluation. The IMU-based TCN achieved the strongest event-detection performance, reaching 42.3 F1 and 83.0 AUROC, compared with 32.6 F1 and 77.2 AUROC for V-JEPA2 ego-video features. Although ego-video alone did not outperform IMU-based sensing, it showed above-chance discrimination, and qualitative analyses suggest that egocentric vision may capture FOG-relevant information independent of IMUs. Together, these results support the use of pretrained ego-video representations to add contextual information to wearable-sensor-based clinical motion understanding in daily living.

</details>


### [69] [NAS-Driven Hardware Accelerator Exploration for Edge AI and Quantization Effects on the Pareto Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13293)
*Eleftherios Mylonas,Angelos Kouprizas,Michael Birbas,Alexios Birbas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A three-stage pipeline for hardware-aware Neural Architecture Search with Post-Training Quantization analysis and automated hardware mapping exploration on reconfigurable accelerators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Edge AI needs neural architectures that are accurate, computationally efficient, and hardware-deployable. While hardware-aware NAS exists, approaches with quantization expand search complexity and tightly couple architecture/quantization design. Post-search quantization effects remain uncharacterized, and no framework combines quantized architecture mapping with automated hardware exploration.

Method: Three-stage pipeline: 1) Hardware-agnostic Pareto rank surrogate frontend on NAS-Bench-201, 2) Quantization bridge with Pareto-aware filtering and feedback control, 3) Evolutionary Domain Space Exploration backend on CGRA4ML for optimal hardware mapping. Plus empirical study of INT4 PTQ effects on NAS-Bench-201 Pareto space using formal stability metrics.

Result: Characterized how INT4 PTQ perturbs NAS-Bench-201 Pareto space through formal stability metrics on all 15,625 architectures. Demonstrated that FP32 zero-shot surrogate outperforms dedicated INT4-trained surrogate in Pareto space coverage across two standard search strategies.

Conclusion: The paper addresses gaps in hardware-aware NAS by providing a framework for quantized architecture mapping on reconfigurable accelerators and analyzing PTQ effects on NAS Pareto structures, showing simpler FP32 surrogates can be effective for quantized deployment.

Abstract: Edge AI deployment demands neural architectures that are simultaneously accurate, computationally efficient, and hardware-deployable - a challenge addressed by hardware-aware Neural Architecture Search (NAS). While recent works incorporate quantization directly into the NAS loop, these approaches expand search complexity and tightly couple architecture and quantization design. The simpler post-search quantization strategy has received little analytical attention: the effects of Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) on the NAS-discovered Pareto structure remain uncharacterised, and no framework combines quantized architecture mapping onto reconfigurable accelerators with automated hardware exploration. This paper addresses both gaps. First, a three-stage pipeline is proposed: a hardware-agnostic Pareto rank surrogate frontend on NAS-Bench-201, a quantization bridge with Pareto-aware filtering and feedback control, and an evolutionary Domain Space Exploration (DSE) backend on CGRA4ML for optimal hardware mapping. Second, an empirical study characterises how INT4 PTQ perturbs the NAS-Bench-201 Pareto space through formal stability metrics on ground-truth data for all 15,625 architectures, and demonstrates that an FP32 zero-shot surrogate outperforms a dedicated INT4-trained surrogate in Pareto space coverage across two standard search strategies.

</details>


### [70] [StateBridge: Training-free Hidden-state Alignment for Latent Communication in LLM Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13317)
*Yanwen Peng,Delvin Ce Zhang,Xi Wang,Nikolaos Aletras*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: StateBridge enables LLM agents to communicate via continuous hidden states instead of text using a training-free orthogonal transformation, improving performance across reasoning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Text-based communication between LLM agents creates a discrete bottleneck where converting continuous hidden states to tokens loses valuable information. Existing latent communication methods either interfere with transformer layers or require trained projectors that limit portability.

Method: StateBridge uses a closed-form orthogonal transformation to align sender's final-layer hidden states to receiver's input space without training. It includes norm calibration and vocabulary anchoring for compatibility with pretrained input distributions. The aligned states are prepended to receiver's input as a continuous prefix.

Result: Evaluated on math reasoning, code generation, and question answering with four models from two families. StateBridge achieves best or tied-best performance on 22 out of 26 model-task pairs, consistently outperforming the strongest baseline.

Conclusion: StateBridge provides an effective, training-free approach for latent communication between LLM agents that avoids information loss in text-based communication while maintaining portability and compatibility with pretrained models.

Abstract: Large language model based multi-agent systems usually communicate in text, i.e., using discrete tokens. However, text introduces a discrete bottleneck. Converting the sender's continuous hidden states into discrete tokens discards information that token identities alone cannot capture. Recent work proposes latent communication as an alternative, where agents transmit hidden representations directly without converting them to text. However, existing latent methods either inject working memory layer by layer across the transformers, or require trained projectors that limit portability. We propose StateBridge, a training-free latent communication approach that aligns the sender's final-layer hidden states to the receiver's input space via a closed-form orthogonal transformation. Lightweight norm calibration and vocabulary anchoring ensure compatibility with the pretrained input distribution. The aligned states are prepended to the input of the receiver agent as a continuous prefix. We evaluate StateBridge on math reasoning, code generation, and question answering with four models from two families. StateBridge achieves the best or tied-best score on 22 out of 26 model-task pairs, consistently outperforming the strongest baseline.

</details>


### [71] [LLM-Guided Graph Generation for Structure-Based Local Improvement Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13333)
*Hai Xia,Vaidyanathan Peruvemba Ramaswamy,Stefan Szeider*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A framework that uses LLMs to automatically extract structural features from constraint optimization problems (MiniZinc format) to improve variable selection in large neighborhood search.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional large neighborhood search uses random variable selection, but domain-specific strategies work better. There's a need for a problem-agnostic approach that can automatically extract structural features without manual design for each problem type.

Method: Use LLM prompts with semantic guidelines to generate a graph generator that converts any problem instance into a uniform weighted graph (nodes=variables, edges=constraints). This enables structure-based local improvement (SLIM) for variable selection and allows feature extraction for configuration selection.

Result: Tested on 20 MiniZinc competition problems: algorithm selection achieves 39.5% average win rate vs. Gurobi baseline (vs. 19.3% best single config). Configuration and feature ablation boosts performance to 44.0%.

Conclusion: LLM-based semantic generation enables effective automated structure and feature extraction for constraint optimization, providing a problem-agnostic approach that significantly outperforms both baseline and single configurations.

Abstract: Large neighborhood search normally selects a random subset of decision variables for iterative optimization. For efficiently solving different problems, researchers tend to design variable selection strategies by taking into account structural features from different domains. In this paper, we build an automatic pipeline that is problem-agnostic to all problems in the MiniZinc format. By prompting an LLM with our semantic guidelines, we guide the LLM to produce a graph generator that maps any instance of a problem type to a uniform weighted graph, where nodes represent decision variables and edges represent constraint relationships. These problem-agnostic graphs guide our structure-based local improvement framework (SLIM) in variable selection. Meanwhile, the weighted graph enables all problem instances to share the same generic graph representation, from which the same graph features can be extracted and used for configuration selection. We evaluated our pipeline on instances across 20 MiniZinc competition problems, finding that algorithm selection achieves a 39.5% average problem-weighted win rate against a one-shot Gurobi baseline, more than doubling the best single configuration (19.3%). Configuration and feature ablation boost the performance further to 44.0%, demonstrating that LLM-based semantic generation enables effective automated structure extraction and feature extraction for constraint optimization.

</details>


### [72] [LongEarth-R1: Benchmarking and Aligning Vision-Language Models for Long-Horizon Earth Observation Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13344)
*Yupan Ding,Jing Xiao,Zhenyuan Zhang,Chaofeng Chen,Liang Liao,Gui-Song Xia,Mi Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LongEarth is a new benchmark and model for long-horizon Earth observation reasoning that handles extended image sequences with 30+ frames, addressing spatial-temporal reasoning across 12 tasks where previous models only handled isolated images or short sequences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current remote sensing vision-language models focus on isolated images, image pairs, or short sequences, limiting their ability to reliably ground reasoning in relevant frames and regions for long-horizon Earth observation tasks that require organizing multi-stage geographic evolution, spatial change localization, temporal anomaly detection, and future inference.

Method: The authors introduce LongEarth-Bench with 120k QA samples from 117k images, with sequences averaging 15.14 frames (up to 30 frames). They develop LongEarth through supervised fine-tuning with explicit sequence identifiers and structured chain-of-thought supervision. LongEarth-R1 applies group relative policy optimization with format, temporal, and spatial rewards.

Result: LongEarth-R1 achieves the best results on all 12 long-sequence tasks in the benchmark while remaining competitive on standard remote sensing benchmarks, demonstrating superior performance in long-horizon Earth observation reasoning.

Conclusion: The LongEarth benchmark and models successfully address the limitations of existing remote sensing vision-language models by enabling effective long-horizon reasoning over extended image sequences, with significant improvements across multiple tasks involving spatial-temporal analysis and reasoning.

Abstract: Long-horizon Earth observation reasoning requires models to organize multi-stage geographic evolution, localize spatial changes, detect temporal anomalies, and infer future from extended image sequences. However, existing remote sensing vision-language models mainly focus on isolated images, image pairs, or short sequences, limiting reliable grounding in the relevant frames and regions. We introduce LongEarth-Bench, a benchmark containing approximately 120k question-answering samples derived from 117k unique images. Its sequences average 15.14 frames and extend to 30 frames, covering 12 tasks across evolution summarization, spatial reasoning, anomaly identification, and logical prediction. A 30k-sample subset further provides structured reasoning traces linking key frames and changed regions to final answers. We develop LongEarth through supervised fine-tuning with explicit sequence identifiers and structured chain-of-thought supervision. Building on LongEarth, LongEarth-R1 applies group relative policy optimization with format, temporal, and spatial rewards. LongEarth-R1 achieves the best results on all 12 long-sequence tasks while remaining competitive on standard remote sensing benchmarks.

</details>


### [73] [Rules or Character? Scaling Laws for AI Safety Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13345)
*Satoshi Takahashi,Nobuji Kouno,Masaaki Komatsu,Ryuji Hamamoto*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper develops a formal model to determine the optimal balance between character shaping (like RLHF) and rule enforcement (like output filters) in AI safety systems as deployment scales increase, finding that character fragility under novel conditions is more important than deployment scale itself.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI safety currently combines character shaping (training-time modification) and rule enforcement (inference-time blocking), but there's little formal analysis on how to optimally balance these approaches as AI systems are deployed at larger scales.

Method: Developed a stylized comparative-statics model that parameterizes safety design as resource allocation (alpha in [0,1]) between character shaping and rule enforcement. The model incorporates scale-dependent filter degradation, common-mode failures, and character fragility. Used multiplicative Pareto damage model with closed-form expected harm analysis and supplemented with tail-risk (CVaR) analysis via Monte Carlo simulation across three scenarios.

Result: Optimal alpha* is interior or at rules-only boundary, shifting weakly toward character shaping as deployment scale grows (from +0.01 to +0.21 delta). Baseline character fragility rate has the dominant effect (0.50 shift across range), exceeding effects of tail severity, filter quality, or common-mode failure. CVaR and expected-harm optima converge at large scales.

Conclusion: Safety architecture decisions depend less on deployment scale itself than on the reliability of character shaping under distributional shift. Character fragility is the key parameter determining optimal balance between character shaping and rule enforcement approaches.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI) safety systems combine character shaping (e.g., Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback [RLHF], Constitutional AI), which modifies behavioral distributions at training time, with rule enforcement (e.g., output filters, safety classifiers), which blocks harmful outputs at inference time, yet little formal analysis exists on how their optimal balance should change as deployment scales increase. We introduce a stylized comparative-statics model that parameterizes safety design as a resource allocation alpha in [0,1] between these two approaches, incorporating scale-dependent filter degradation, common-mode failures, and character fragility -- the risk that shaped behavior degrades or collapses under novel conditions. Under a multiplicative Pareto damage model, we derive closed-form expected harm and supplement it with tail-risk (CVaR) analysis via Monte Carlo simulation. Across three scenarios (optimistic, moderate, pessimistic), the optimal alpha* is interior or at the rules-only boundary and shifts weakly toward character shaping as deployment scale T grows, from negligible (Delta alpha* = +0.01) to pronounced (Delta alpha* = +0.21) depending on scenario. The dominant parameter is the baseline character fragility rate p^(0)_frag, which shifts alpha* by 0.50 across its range -- far exceeding the effect of tail severity, filter quality, or common-mode failure probability. CVaR and expected-harm optima converge at large T. These results suggest that safety architecture decisions depend less on deployment scale per se than on the reliability of character shaping under distributional shift.

</details>


### [74] [TopoIntent: Compiling Security Intent into Executable, Compliance-Checked Network Topologies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13389)
*Xiaokang Qu,Jianliang Ma,Zao Fan,Tianshu Chu,Tianlong Fan,Linyuan Lü*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TopoIntent is a system that automatically generates compliance-checked network security topologies from natural language requirements using template retrieval and staged fusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current NetOps automation tools require fixed designs and offer limited support for generating structured security topologies from underspecified natural language requirements. There's a gap between business intent/regulatory requirements and executable network security implementations.

Method: Uses schema contracts to constrain generation, retrieves reference architectures via dense-vector search from a curated template library, applies staged fusion for intent-template alignment and security completion. Includes CIS Controls compliance checking, structural gap repair through additive schema-preserving edits, and exports to Mininet with iptables ACLs.

Result: On held-out evaluation set: additive repair improves topology-visible CIS satisfaction from 0.78 to 1.00 in <1.5 rounds average; one feedback round raises post-ACL policy pass rate from 0.78 to 0.88. System handles finance/government scenarios excluded from retrieval templates.

Conclusion: TopoIntent successfully bridges the gap between security intent specification and executable network topologies, automating compliance-checked topology generation with significant improvements in security compliance and policy accuracy.

Abstract: Enterprise security topology design requires translating business intent, regulatory requirements, and risk assumptions into zones, boundary devices, inter-zone paths, and access-control policies. Existing NetOps automation tools mainly operate after this design is fixed, providing limited support for generating structured security topologies from underspecified natural-language requirements. We present TopoIntent, a system that compiles security intent into executable, compliance-checked network topologies. It uses a schema contract to constrain generation, retrieves reference architectures from a curated template library via dense-vector search, and applies staged fusion for intent-template alignment and security completion. The generated topology is checked against CIS Controls v8.1.2 safeguards visible at the topology layer, while unresolved cases are marked for manual review. Structural gaps are repaired through additive schema-preserving edits. The final topology is exported to Mininet scripts with kernel-level iptables ACLs, enabling executable reachability and allow/deny tests. Because no public benchmark exists for this requirement-to-topology task, we construct an evaluation set from reference security architecture diagrams. The retrieval set contains 22 templates and 44 synthetic intents across five scenarios, while the held-out set contains 7 templates and 14 intents from finance and government scenarios excluded from retrieval. On the held-out set, additive repair improves topology-visible CIS satisfaction from 0.78 to 1.00 in fewer than 1.5 rounds on average, and one feedback round raises the post-ACL policy pass rate from 0.78 to 0.88.

</details>


### [75] [Jointly Predicting Courses and Grades Using a Transformer-Based Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13409)
*Paul Savala*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRACE model jointly predicts both course selection and grades per semester using transformer architecture, reducing grade prediction error by ~50% compared to grade-only models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
