Recent automatic research (AutoResearch) agents bring this goal within reach, as modern LLMs show the capability to independently implement solutions and learn from the execution outcomes. Behind these gains, post-training (especially RL) plays a central role. In this paper, we identify a fundamental tension when scaling RL for these agents: the two components of every AutoResearch trajectory (agent generation and environment execution) scale in very different manners, since all generation shares compute through batching, while each execution occupies its exclusive sandbox and real machine time. As a result, the environment execution dominates the training cost and becomes the bottleneck as trajectories grow. To resolve this tension, we propose World Model RL (WMRL), which replaces environment execution with a world model to remove this bottleneck. Additionally, the world model can be imperfect, as its rewards are corrupted by bias and noise. Therefore, we further equip WMRL with two mitigations, Online Debiasing and Inverse-Variance Denoising, which offset the bias and suppress the noise respectively. Theoretically, we prove that both mitigations of WMRL strictly improve the convergence guarantee. Empirically, WMRL accelerates training by 3-4x on various tasks at different agent scales, while exceeding the performance of standard RL baselines. Moreover, our post-trained 4B and 9B agents outperform much larger open-weight agents of 48B and 120B on held-out benchmarks. Beyond AutoResearch, WMRL also transfers to post-training embodied VLA policies, which demonstrates the generalizability of our method.

</details>


### [100] [Prof-K: Probabilistic One-Pass Filtering for Efficient Top-k Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12573)
*Tadeusz Dziarmaga,Witold Sikora,Łukasz Struski,Jacek Tabor,Marcin Mazur*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Prof-K is a single-pass, distribution-agnostic probabilistic top-k selection algorithm that uses random sampling to estimate an adaptive threshold, achieves significant speedups over existing methods, and provides theoretical guarantees for correctness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional top-k selection methods face efficiency issues as data scales: exact methods have high memory/compute overhead, while approximate methods rely on brittle heuristics that degrade under adversarial or heavy-tailed inputs. The need arises for a robust, scalable top-k algorithm with theoretical guarantees.

Method: Prof-K performs a single-pass filtering procedure: (1) uses a small random sample to estimate an adaptive threshold, (2) streams N input elements once into a compact buffer using the threshold, (3) runs exact top-k on the buffer to recover true top-k elements with probability at least 1-ε. The method includes theoretically derived sample size optimization and buffer size guarantees.

Result: Prof-K achieves 1.5x-10x speedups over highly optimized PyTorch topk and RadiK implementations, with largest gains in large-scale, small-to-moderate-k regimes. It maintains probabilistic correctness guarantees independent of input distribution and demonstrates practical impact on training BatchTopK Sparse Autoencoders where top-k selection is a major cost component.

Conclusion: Prof-K provides a fast, scalable, distribution-agnostic solution to top-k selection with probabilistic correctness guarantees, offering significant performance improvements over existing methods while ensuring robustness to adversarial inputs. The algorithm also enables principled accuracy-speed trade-offs by allowing relaxed recall targets.

Abstract: Top-k selection is a fundamental computational primitive with applications spanning databases, information retrieval, signal processing, and modern machine learning workloads, including sparse activations and attention pruning. As data sizes grow, existing approaches become inefficient: exact methods incur high memory and compute overhead, while approximate methods often rely on brittle heuristics that degrade under adversarial or heavy-tailed inputs. In this paper, we introduce Prof-K, a fast, scalable, and distribution-agnostic top-k algorithm with probabilistic correctness guarantees. Prof-K performs a single-pass filtering procedure: a small random sample estimates an adaptive threshold, the N input elements are streamed once into a compact buffer, and an exact top-k routine on this buffer recovers the true top-k elements with probability at least 1 - $ε$, where $ε$ > 0 is user specified. We derive high-probability guarantees for correctness and buffer size, together with an approximately optimal sample size that minimizes overhead as a function of N and k. Empirically, Prof-K achieves 1.5x-10x speedups over the highly optimized PyTorch topk and recent RadiK implementations, with the largest gains in the large-scale, small-to-moderate-k regime where prior methods struggle most. Unlike previous approaches, these guarantees hold independently of the input distribution, ensuring robustness to adversarial settings. By relaxing the recall target (e.g., recovering 95% of the true top-k values), Prof-K additionally provides a principled accuracy-speed trade-off. We further demonstrate its impact on training BatchTopK Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs), where top-k selection constitutes a significant portion of the training cost.

</details>


### [101] [Represent, Then Generate: Multimodal-Conditioned Time-Series Generation under Irregular Missingness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12592)
*Haochen Zhang,Jiaheng Guo,Yu-Chao Huang,Nicholas Knoz,Tianlong Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ReCoGen: A two-stage framework that decouples multimodal condition representation from target generation for synthesizing absent physiological signals from co-recorded data, achieving state-of-the-art performance across three clinical benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many informative physiological signals in clinical monitoring are invasive, expensive, or unavailable for patients. While conditional generation offers a solution, existing approaches struggle with heterogeneous, irregularly missing multimodal data combining time-variant signals and static covariates seen in real clinical practice.

Method: Two-stage framework: Stage I trains one masked autoencoder per modality to distill time-variant conditions into compact, missingness-tolerant token sequences. Stage II trains a flow-matching generator that fuses these tokens with static conditions (using learnable cross-attention over frozen per-modality encoders and dual token-plus-AdaLN route) to synthesize target signals.

Result: Achieves best downstream utility on all 16 (dataset, task, metric) settings across three physiological benchmarks (continuous glucose monitoring on AI-READI, arterial blood pressure on MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV), surpassing six representative conditional generators. On 13/16 settings, utility reaches or exceeds that measured on real signals.

Conclusion: ReCoGen successfully turns routinely collected signals into informative surrogates for invasive or unavailable ones, representing a step toward less invasive, lower-cost continuous clinical monitoring by effectively handling heterogeneous multimodal clinical data.

Abstract: Continuous physiological time series underpin modern clinical monitoring, yet many of the most informative signals are invasive, expensive, or simply unavailable for a given patient. Conditional generation offers a remedy: an absent signal can be synthesized from co-recorded signals and routine clinical variables. Existing generators, however, are built around a single conditioning modality and degrade when forced to handle the heterogeneous, irregularly missing mix of time-variant signals and static covariates seen in practice. We propose ReCoGen (Represent Conditions, then Generate), a two-stage framework that decouples multimodal condition representation from target generation. Stage I trains one masked autoencoder per modality, distilling each time-variant condition into a compact and missingness-tolerant token sequence. Stage II trains a flow-matching generator that fuses these tokens with static conditions to synthesize the target signal. Across three physiological benchmarks, including continuous glucose monitoring on AI-READI and arterial blood pressure generation on MIMIC-III and MIMIC-IV, ReCoGen attains the best downstream utility on all sixteen (dataset, task, metric) settings, surpassing six representative conditional generators; on thirteen of them its utility also reaches or exceeds the utility measured on the real signal, a reference we read as an approximate anchor rather than a ceiling. Ablations trace the gains to the conditioning path: learnable cross-attention over the frozen per-modality encoders, and a dual token-plus-AdaLN route for the static conditions. ReCoGen thus turns routinely collected signals into informative surrogates for invasive or unavailable ones, a step toward less invasive, lower-cost continuous clinical monitoring.

</details>


### [102] [Predicting When Random Low-Dimensional Reparameterizations Train Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12597)
*Andrew Cheng,Ali Eslamian,Jie Cheng,Mehdi Zargham,Qiang Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Random low-dimensional reparameterization for neural networks can achieve low-loss regions with small latent dimensions, analyzed through a novel orientation-resolved quadratic master formula and implemented as Random Mapping Networks (RaMaN) for memory-efficient training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural networks can be trained or fine-tuned through random low-dimensional reparameterization, but there's a practical question about how large the latent search space must be to reach low-loss regions. The paper aims to understand and predict this accessibility transition more accurately than previous orientation-agnostic approximations.

Method: 1) Expresses the known accessibility transition in conic form centered at statistical dimension; 2) Develops an orientation-resolved quadratic master formula that predicts random-slice residual from curvature spectrum and reference-to-solution displacement profile; 3) Introduces Random Mapping Networks (RaMaN) using structured Hadamard or seed-regenerated Gaussian maps to avoid O(dP) storage; 4) Implements matrix-free curvature approximations and sweep-free dimension selection.

Result: The orientation-resolved predictor closely tracks measured transition locations in controlled quadratic and neural-curvature experiments, outperforming orientation-agnostic approximations when displacement direction matters. End-to-end experiments show sharp, protocol-dependent training transitions across image and language models. RaMaN reduces optimizer-state memory from O(P) to O(d).

Conclusion: The orientation-resolved quadratic master formula provides accurate predictions for low-dimensional reparameterization accessibility, and the RaMaN framework offers practical memory-efficient implementations with structured random maps, enabling effective low-dimensional training of neural networks.

Abstract: Neural networks can often be trained or fine-tuned through random low-dimensional reparameterization, where a small latent vector is mapped into a full parameter update by a frozen random map. This raises a practical question: how large must the latent search space be to reach a low-loss region? We first express the known accessibility transition in an equivalent conic form, centered for compact convex targets at the statistical dimension of the polar cone. Our main theoretical contribution is an orientation-resolved quadratic master formula that predicts the random-slice residual from both the curvature spectrum and the reference-to-solution displacement profile. It yields a self-consistent isotropic-orientation predictor and, in a conservative radius-only specialization, recovers the earlier Gaussian-width quadratic bound. Building on this analysis, we introduce Random Mapping Networks (RaMaN), which instantiate the predicted latent dimension using structured Hadamard or seed-regenerated Gaussian maps. These constructions avoid the O(dP) storage of dense random maps and reduce optimizer-state memory from O(P) to O(d). We also develop matrix-free curvature approximations and sweep-free dimension selection. Across controlled quadratic and neural-curvature experiments, the orientation-resolved predictor closely tracks measured transition locations and outperforms orientation-agnostic approximations when displacement direction matters. End-to-end experiments further show sharp, protocol-dependent training transitions across image and language models.

</details>


### [103] [The Boolean Power of ReLU](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12617)
*Pablo Barceló,Floris Geerts,Matthias Lanzinger,Klara Pakhomenko,Jan Van den Bussche*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ReLU-based message passing networks are strictly more powerful than those using eventually constant activation functions (like TrReLU) for expressing Boolean queries on graphs with Boolean node features.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to settle an open problem about whether ReLU-based message passing languages (MPLang) are more powerful than those using eventually constant activation functions (specifically trReLU) when it comes to expressing Boolean queries on graphs with Boolean node features.

Method: The authors prove a theoretical result using mathematical analysis. They examine Boolean queries expressible in Σ-MPLang (with eventually constant activation functions) versus ReLU-MPLang. The method involves proving that the class of Boolean queries expressible with eventually constant activations is a strict subclass of those expressible with ReLU.

Result: The paper proves that ReLU-MPLang is strictly more powerful than any Σ-MPLang using eventually constant activation functions for Boolean queries. This specifically implies ReLU-GNNs are strictly more expressive than {TrReLU,id}-GNNs for Boolean queries on Boolean-featured graphs.

Conclusion: The open problem is settled: ReLU-based message passing networks have strictly greater expressive power for Boolean queries compared to networks using eventually constant activation functions like TrReLU, establishing an important hierarchy in graph neural network expressiveness.

Abstract: We prove that, on finite simple undirected graphs equipped with a single Boolean node feature, the Boolean queries expressible in $Σ$-MPLang, for any collection $Σ$ of eventually constant activation functions and with arbitrary real coefficients, form a strict subclass of the Boolean queries expressible in ReLU-MPLang. We thereby settle a recently posed open problem: whether ReLU-MPLang is more powerful than trReLU-MPLang when it comes to Boolean queries. In particular, this implies that ReLU-GNNs are strictly more expressive than {TrReLU,id}-GNNs with respect to Boolean queries on Boolean-featured graphs.

</details>


### [104] [Structure-preserving uncertainty quantification for GENERIC dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12624)
*Zequn He,Celia Reina*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: S-PENNs: Structure-Preserving Epistemic Neural Networks for uncertainty quantification in physics-constrained ML models, maintaining physical admissibility while providing efficient UQ.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard uncertainty quantification methods for structure-preserving machine learning models either violate physical constraints, require architectural changes, or are computationally expensive. There's a need for UQ that preserves hard physical constraints while being efficient.

Method: Proposed Structure-Preserving Epistemic Neural Networks (S-PENNs) - attach lightweight epinets to constrained components of pretrained models to ensure physical admissibility. Combined with split conformal prediction for post-hoc calibration to guarantee finite-sample marginal coverage.

Result: S-PENNs produce thermodynamically consistent stochastic realizations and well-calibrated prediction intervals while reducing computational cost by 1-3 orders of magnitude compared to deep ensembles. Validated on three numerical examples including ODE and PDE systems.

Conclusion: S-PENNs provide a general framework for UQ in structure-preserving scientific ML that maintains physical constraints while being computationally efficient, with potential extensions to broader computational mechanics applications.

Abstract: Structure-preserving machine learning embeds physical structure directly into model architectures, yet uncertainty quantification (UQ) for such hard-constrained models remains limited because standard UQ methods may violate the encoded admissibility conditions, require architectural modifications, or impose substantial computational costs. In this work, we propose Structure-Preserving Epistemic Neural Networks (S-PENNs), a general framework for UQ in scientific machine learning models with hard architectural constraints, and instantiate it for GENERIC (General Equation for Non-Equilibrium Reversible-Irreversible Coupling) dynamics. S-PENNs preserve the structural constraints of a pretrained model by attaching lightweight epinets to its constrained components, ensuring that every sampled realization remains physically admissible by construction. When applied to GENERIC dynamics, such a proposed framework yields thermodynamically consistent rollouts that preserve the first and second laws. Furthermore, we combine S-PENNs with split conformal prediction as a post-hoc calibration method to produce prediction intervals with finite-sample marginal coverage guarantees. We validate S-PENNs on three numerical examples: a harmonic oscillator coupled to a heat bath and an idealized chemical motor, both governed by ODEs, and a one-dimensional viscoplastic model governed by PDEs. Across all three examples, S-PENNs produce thermodynamically consistent stochastic realizations and well-calibrated prediction intervals while reducing the computational cost by about one to three orders of magnitude compared to deep ensembles. Although the present study focuses on GENERIC dynamics, S-PENNs can be extended more broadly to scientific machine learning models in computational mechanics with either hard or soft constraints.

</details>


### [105] [CAKE: Compiler-Agent Co-Design for Frontier Kernel Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12629)
*Zihao Ye,Yingyi Huang,Hongyi Jin,Bohan Hou,Junru Shao,Zhongming Yu,Jinqi Chen,Meghan Cowan,Shiyi Cao,Shanli Xing,Hanfeng Chen,Vinod Grover,Tianqi Chen,Luis Ceze*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CAKE is a compiler-agent co-design framework where AI agents author specialized GPU kernel representations (CAKE IR) that expose hardware details like warp roles and memory movement, enabling automated verification and optimization while achieving competitive performance with expert-tuned kernels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current GPU kernel development suffers from a disconnect between AI agents and GPU programming languages. Agents treat compilers as black boxes with limited feedback, while existing domain-specific languages either hide critical scheduling decisions or expose them through difficult abstractions, making expert kernels hard to reproduce.

Method: CAKE introduces a typed, hardware-explicit schedule representation (CAKE IR) that exposes warp roles, memory movement, synchronization, and pipelines. It supports verification, cost modeling, and localized diagnostics. The system evolves through learning from failures, converting them into verifier rules, IR primitives, model calibrations, and reusable optimization tactics.

Result: In experiments, CAKE IR achieved 1.144x performance of tuned FlashML baseline at 80M token budget, outperforming direct CUDA/PTX (0.928x). Agent-generated Kimi Delta Attention showed 2.05x geometric-mean speedup over official FlashKDA. Dispatcher-backed KNN and KMeans improved performance by 1.42x-2.12x across 400+ shapes, with four kernel changes available as upstream pull requests.

Conclusion: CAKE demonstrates that compiler-agent co-design can bridge the gap between AI-assisted kernel generation and expert performance. By exposing hardware details through a typed IR and enabling learning from failures, it enables agents to produce competitive GPU kernels while separating single-shape evolution from library generalization and dispatch.

Abstract: GPU kernel agents and GPU programming languages have advanced separately, leaving expert kernels difficult to reproduce. Agents usually treat the compiler as a fixed black box and receive only errors, correctness outcomes, and timing, while existing DSLs either hide critical scheduling decisions or expose them through difficult layout abstractions. We present CAKE, a compiler-agent co-design in which agents author CAKE IR, a typed, hardware-explicit schedule representation. CAKE exposes warp roles, memory movement, synchronization, and pipelines while supporting verification, cost modeling, and localized diagnostics. The harness itself evolves: recurring failures become verifier rules, IR primitives, model calibrations, and reusable optimization tactics. In matched implementation-hidden Flash-KMeans clean starts on B200, the best CAKE IR candidate at an 80-million-token budget runs at 1.144x the tuned FlashML baseline, compared with 0.928x for direct CUDA/PTX. Beyond this benchmark, agent-generated Kimi Delta Attention achieves a 2.05x geometric-mean speedup over official FlashKDA and passes end-to-end serving validation. Dispatcher-backed KNN and KMeans improve performance by 1.42x to 2.12x across more than 400 shapes, and four kernel changes are available as upstream PRs. CAKE targets NVIDIA GPUs from Ampere through Blackwell and separates single-shape evolution from library generalization and dispatch.

</details>


### [106] [Interpretable Causal Discovery via Causal-Effect Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12640)
*Cixuan Zhang,Guy Van den Broeck,Benjie Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A Bayesian method for conditional causal discovery that can handle rare events by using rare-event estimation techniques to efficiently sample from conditional posteriors over causal graphs given specific constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional causal discovery focuses on predicting causal edges from data, but researchers also need to interpret and explain specific observed or hypothesized phenomena, such as large causal effects. This requires conditional causal discovery - finding causal structures given specific events or constraints, which is challenging when those events have small posterior probability.

Method: The paper casts conditional causal discovery as a Bayesian inference problem targeting the posterior over causal graphs and parameters conditional on specific events (like causal-effect constraints). The method adapts rare-event estimation techniques to perform inference in the joint graph-parameter space, gradually driving a particle population toward the constrained region while maintaining samples that approximate the conditional posterior.

Result: Empirical evaluation on synthetic graphs shows the method's accuracy at both small and large scales. A case study on the Sachs protein dataset demonstrates how the method can aid scientific exploration by providing pathway-level summaries, showing its practical utility for conditional causal discovery tasks.

Conclusion: The paper successfully addresses the computational challenge of conditional causal discovery for rare events by adapting rare-event estimation techniques, enabling efficient Bayesian inference over causal graphs and parameters conditional on specific constraints, with demonstrated effectiveness in both synthetic and real-world biological applications.

Abstract: Causal discovery aims to uncover the underlying causal relationships given data generated from a system. The goal, however, is not merely to predict causal edges given data, but also to be able to interpret and explain either observed or hypothesized phenomena, such as a particularly large causal effect. We consider this task of conditional causal discovery and cast it as a Bayesian inference problem, in which we target the posterior over causal graphs and parameters conditional on an event such as a causal-effect constraint. Unfortunately, this poses a computational challenge: existing approaches to Bayesian causal discovery struggle when the event has small posterior mass. To address this, we adapt rare-event estimation techniques to perform inference the joint graph-parameter space. Our method gradually drives a particle population toward the constrained region while maintaining samples that approximate the conditional posterior. Empirical evaluation on synthetic graphs validates the accuracy of our approach at small and large scales, and we show in a case study on the Sachs protein dataset how our method can be used to aid scientific exploration by providing pathway-level summaries.

</details>


### [107] [Training Under Challenge: Executable Certificates and Challenge-Closed Optimality for Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12655)
*Farhang Yeganegi,Arian Eamaz,Mojtaba Soltanalian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Training Under Challenge (TUC) is a framework that provides executable certificates for neural network optimality by constructing and testing alternative models in the same class, with any lower-valued candidate revealing a lower bound on the empirical global-optimality gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Flat training curves don't reveal whether a neural network has reached global optimum, is locally trapped, representation-limited, or mismatched to its trainer. There's a need for verifiable certificates of optimality.

Method: TUC framework uses predeclared, architecture-valid procedures to construct complete alternatives in the same certified class and reevaluate the same objective. It introduces a resource-indexed challenge-power modulus characterizing the largest gap compatible with passage.

Result: For squared loss, block-decrease operators make coverage checkable and yield uniform and realized-residual bounds. On channel-gated ResNet-18 distillation with known optimum, eight internal challenges cover all 240 audited output directions, with realized-residual bounds within 1.74-3.02× of true gap. Paired predictive certificates separate decoder under-use from representation insufficiency.

Conclusion: TUC provides a practical framework for certifying neural network optimality, with demonstrable effectiveness on real problems. The method enables diagnosis, repair, and recertification while revealing theoretical limits of certification without proper coverage mechanisms.

Abstract: A flat training curve does not reveal whether a neural network has reached a global optimum, is locally trapped, is representation-limited, or is mismatched to its trainer. We introduce Training Under Challenge, an executable-certificate framework in which predeclared, architecture-valid procedures construct complete alternatives in the same certified class and reevaluate the same objective. Any lower-valued candidate is a replayable witness that lower-bounds the checkpoint's empirical global-optimality gap. Passing a finite suite is only suite-relative; global-gap conclusions require a separately justified coverage mechanism. We define a resource-indexed challenge-power modulus that characterizes the largest gap compatible with passage. For squared loss, current block-decrease operators make coverage checkable and yield uniform and realized-residual bounds. We prove the converse frontier: without coverage, a first-order ReLU trainer can reach infinitely many exact conditional head optima while converging to a non-global point. On a channel-gated ResNet-18 distillation problem with known optimum, eight internal challenges cover all 240 audited output directions, and realized-residual bounds lie within factors of 1.74--3.02 of the true gap. Paired predictive certificates separate decoder under-use from representation insufficiency, while quantized-denoising studies demonstrate diagnosis, repair, and current-state recertification.

</details>


### [108] [Demand Transfer Estimation at Scale via Restricted Logit Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12680)
*Lakshya Garg,Deep Narayan Mishra,Swapnil Yadav,Haoan Wang,Sujal Alugubelli,Karthik Kumaran,Anupriya Sharma*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A scalable approach for estimating Demand Transfer coefficients to improve item demand forecasting in large assortments by accounting for substitution effects between items.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional customer choice models for demand forecasting become inefficient for large item universes (1M+ items) because they require separate demand forecasts for every possible item assortment. An alternative is needed that combines efficient independent item forecasting with adjustments for substitution effects.

Method: The approach focuses on estimating Demand Transfer (DT) coefficients, which represent the percentage of a target item's demand that gets redirected to other items when the target is unavailable. The method enables computation of these coefficients for large item universes by leveraging reasonable assumptions about substitution behavior.

Result: Experiments on data and historical transaction data across multiple categories and locations show that when assumptions about substitution behavior are satisfied, the procedure accurately estimates underlying DT coefficients and leads to improvements in demand forecasting.

Conclusion: The proposed scalable method for estimating Demand Transfer coefficients effectively addresses the efficiency challenges of traditional choice models for large assortments, providing accurate demand forecasting improvements while accounting for item substitution effects.

Abstract: Item demand forecasting is an integral component of store assortment optimization. Existing literature focuses on learning a suitable customer choice model and using this model to determine the value of an objective function (i.e. expected demand) with respect to an assortment proposal. However, for large item universe with many categories, this approach can prove inefficient, needing a separate demand forecast for every possible item assortment. An alternate approach exists whereby we combine the efficiency of forecasting item demand independently, while at the same time applying adjustments to the independent forecasts that account for the relations between item demand and the availability of other similar items on the shelf.
  Central to this approach is the estimation of Demand Transfer (DT) coefficients. These DT coefficients represent the percent of a particular target item's (item that the customer walked in the store to buy) demand that is redirected to each other item in the universe should the target item be removed from the shelf. We introduce an approach that allows us to compute these DT coefficients on large item universes (assortments having 1 million+ items). Experiments on data as well as historical transaction data for multiple locations within categories demonstrate that when certain reasonable assumptions about substitution behavior are satisfied, our procedure is able to accurately estimate underlying DT coefficients and lead to improvements in demand forecasting.

</details>


### [109] [Finding the Needle in a Haystack: Test-Time Analog Circuit Representation Adaptation for Bayesian Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12687)
*Fin Amin,Sounak Dutta,Paul D. Franzon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TTARO is an online Bayesian optimization framework that adapts circuit representations during search using new performance data, outperforming fixed-embedding methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing representation-based Bayesian optimization methods treat circuit embeddings as fixed after training, creating a mismatch between representation learning and optimization. Embeddings learned for encoding/reconstruction may not be organized according to the actual figure of merit being optimized.

Method: TTARO is an online deep-kernel Bayesian optimization framework that jointly learns a nonlinear feature transformation and Gaussian-process surrogate using FoM labels of evaluated circuits. It continuously updates both representation and surrogate after each new evaluation before selecting the next candidate.

Result: TTARO reduces regret AUC by 15.2% on average relative to standard BO and by 20.7% relative to Deep Kernel Learning across 40 encoder/kernel/acquisition settings, with reductions as large as 46.7% in some cases.

Conclusion: Continuously adapting circuit representations with newly observed performance data during Bayesian optimization aligns the search space with the optimization objective, leading to significant improvements over fixed-embedding methods for analog circuit topology search.

Abstract: Bayesian optimization (BO) is a sample-efficient framework for analog circuit topology search, where evaluating each candidate topology can require costly simulation. However, representation-based BO methods typically treat circuit embeddings as fixed after encoder training. This creates a mismatch between representation learning and optimization: embeddings learned to encode or reconstruct circuit structure are not necessarily organized according to the figure of merit (FoM) being optimized. This paper introduces Test-Time Analog Representation Adaptation for Bayesian Optimization (TTARO), an online deep-kernel BO framework that adapts circuit representations throughout the search process. Starting from pretrained circuit embeddings, TTARO jointly learns a nonlinear feature transformation and a Gaussian-process surrogate using the FoM labels of the circuits evaluated so far. Following each new evaluation, TTARO updates the representation and surrogate before selecting the next candidate. We compare TTARO with conventional Gaussian Process-based BO over fixed embeddings and with Deep Kernel Learning (DKL), which learns the representation only from the initial evaluated designs and keeps it fixed throughout the remainder of the search. By continually incorporating newly observed FoM labels into representation learning, TTARO aligns the search space with the optimization objective as BO progresses. In our experiments, TTARO reduces regret AUC by 15.2% on average relative to BO and by 20.7% relative to DKL across 40 encoder/kernel/acquisition settings, outperforming prior art in most settings with reductions as large as 46.7%.

</details>


### [110] [The Impact of Temporal Context Length and Encoding Strategies on Self-Supervised ECG Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12695)
*Ahmed Sameh,Ramzi Al-Sharawi,Yogatheesan Varatharajah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Longer ECG temporal context (5-10 minutes) and continuous patch embeddings outperform short 16-second windows and discretized tokens for rhythm detection and patient-level consistency in self-supervised ECG models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-supervised ECG models typically use short signal windows (seconds) and discretized token sequences, but it's unclear if these approaches sacrifice information needed for rhythm inference and longitudinal consistency in real-world ambulatory recordings.

Method: Controlled study on Icentia11k single-lead dataset varying (1) input horizon (16s, 1min, 5min, 10min) and (2) front-end representation (continuous convolutional patch embeddings vs. fixed vector-quantized tokens), while keeping Transformer backbone and training protocol constant. Evaluated on downstream abnormal rhythm detection and patient-level retrieval for cross-session stability.

Result: Longer temporal context (beyond 16s) improves transfer performance and retrieval accuracy, with best results at 5-10 minutes. Continuous patch embeddings consistently outperform discretized tokens across all horizons, suggesting quantization discards clinically relevant waveform details.

Conclusion: Extended temporal context and continuous encoders are important for ECG foundation models to capture slow-varying rhythm dynamics and individual-specific structure, supporting their use in clinical prediction and similarity-based applications.

Abstract: Self-supervised electrocardiogram (ECG) models are often trained on a few seconds of ECG signal and, increasingly, on discretized token sequences. It remains unclear whether these choices sacrifice information needed for rhythm inference and longitudinal consistency in real-world ambulatory recordings. We present a controlled study on the Icentia11k single-lead dataset that varies (i) the input horizon (16 seconds, 1 minute, 5 minutes, and 10 minutes) and (ii) the front-end representation (continuous convolutional patch embeddings vs. fixed vector-quantized tokens), while holding the Transformer backbone and training protocol constant. Representations are assessed by downstream abnormal rhythm detection and by patient-level retrieval that probes cross-session stability. Our results show that increasing temporal context beyond 16-second snapshots yields stronger transfer and higher retrieval accuracy, with the strongest performance achieved by the 5- and 10-minute models, indicating improved capture of slow-varying rhythm dynamics and individual-specific structure. Across all evaluated horizons, continuous patch embeddings outperform discretized tokens, suggesting that quantization can discard clinically relevant waveform detail. These findings motivate ECG foundation models that emphasize extended context and continuous encoders for clinical prediction and similarity-based applications. Our code and pretrained models are publicly available at https://github.com/muha-0/ecg-ssl-representation-learning.

</details>


### [111] [A Contract-Grade Verifier for LLM-Generated GPU Kernels, and a Native Blackwell Backward for the Gated-Linear-Recurrence Family](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12700)
*Rishi Shah,Rishav Shrestha*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Paper exposes serious flaws in current GPU kernel correctness testing by creating a rigorous verifier that finds 39.5% of machine-generated kernels accepted by current methods are actually broken, and develops the first native Blackwell training backward for GLR family.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current GPU kernel generation systems report high correctness rates based on inadequate testing with random inputs at fixed shapes. These tests miss critical failures like NaN/infinity handling, shape sensitivity, numerical precision issues, and non-deterministic behavior.

Method: Built a contract-grade verifier with twelve adversarial gates - properties correct kernels must satisfy. Several gates are tolerance-free to avoid threshold arguments. Used this verifier to audit 2,638 machine-generated kernels from a public system.

Result: Found 39.5% of kernels broken beyond tolerance arguments and 62.1% with at least one violation. Standard test accepted 1,487 kernels the verifier rejected, with only 14 cases the other way. Also developed first native Blackwell tcgen05 training backward for GLR family and validated it independently.

Conclusion: Correctness signal in kernel generation progress reports is much weaker than numbers suggest. Tolerance-free contracts would close most of the gap. The field needs more rigorous verification methods beyond simple random testing.

Abstract: Systems that generate GPU kernels with language models report high correctness rates. Those rates come from a single loose test: run the kernel on a few random inputs at one fixed shape and accept it if the output is close to a reference. A kernel can pass that test and still be silently wrong. It can return an ordinary number where the true answer is a NaN or an infinity, differ from run to run, break when the shape changes, or accumulate in fp16 where the reference keeps an fp32 total. We build the instrument that checks correctness properly: a contract-grade verifier of twelve adversarial gates, each a property a correct kernel must satisfy, several of them tolerance-free, so no choice of threshold can explain a failure away. Aimed outward, the verifier audits 2,638 machine-generated kernels that a public system's own harness had already accepted as correct. It finds 39.5% broken beyond any tolerance argument and 62.1% carrying at least one violation. The field's standard test accepts 1,487 kernels the verifier rejects, against only 14 the other way. We defend the finding four independent ways: a 7/7 positive control, a threshold-calibration sweep, 98.5% agreement with the reference benchmark's own correctness code, and a stratified hand-audit. Aimed inward, the verifier judges a kernel of our own: the first native Blackwell tcgen05 training backward for the gated-linear-recurrence (GDN) family, including the reverse-state stage the field still runs on a fallback. We establish its correctness independently, against a double-precision oracle, and train five family members through it. The correctness signal behind reported progress in kernel generation is far weaker than the numbers suggest, and a set of tolerance-free contracts would close most of the gap.

</details>


### [112] [Federated Compositional Muon Optimizer for Matrix-Wise Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12710)
*Wang Yan,Feihu Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposed FedCoMuon and FedCoMuon-VR optimizers for distributed matrix-wise compositional optimization, achieving better sample complexity than existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Muon optimizer is useful for matrix-wise models but not well-suited for hierarchical structured problems, and existing methods lack effectiveness for distributed compositional optimization.

Method: Developed FedCoMuon based on compositional gradient tracking and orthogonalized momentum, plus FedCoMuon-VR variant using momentum-based variance reduction technique.

Result: FedCoMuon-VR achieves O(ε^{-3}) sample complexity for ε-stationary solution, better than existing FedMuon algorithms. Experiments on robust federated learning and risk-sensitive meta learning show competitive performance.

Conclusion: The proposed FedCoMuon optimizers effectively solve distributed matrix-wise compositional optimization problems, offering improved theoretical convergence and practical performance over existing methods.

Abstract: Muon, a more recently developed optimizer, is useful for matrix-wise models in AI areas. Although many works have studied Muon and its variants, these methods are still not particularly well-suited for hierarchical structured problems. To fill this gap, we propose an effective federated compositional Muon (FedCoMuon) optimizer to solve distributed matrix-wise compositional optimization problems. Specifically, our FedCoMuon optimizer builds on compositional gradient tracking and orthogonalized momentum. Moreover, we propose a variance reduced variant of FedCoMuon (FedCoMuon-VR) based on a momentum-based variance reduced technique. In theory, we analyze the convergence properties of our algorithms under the non-i.i.d. and non-convex settings. In particular, we prove that our FedCoMuon-VR obtains a lower sample complexity of $O(ε^{-3})$ for finding an $ε$-stationary solution than the existing FedMuon algorithms. Extensive numerical experiments on robust federated learning and task-distributed risk-sensitive meta learning show that our proposed methods are competitive with existing compositional baselines and achieve the best reported accuracy in several settings.

</details>


### [113] [Perturbation-based Regional Interpretability through Subtraction Mapping (PRISM): naming-error dissociations in language models and post-stroke aphasia](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12717)
*Xiang Guan,Roger D. Newman-Norlund,Yong Yang,Saeed Ahmadi,Regan Willis,Nadra Salman,Kalil Warren,Srihari Nelakuditi,Chris Rorden,Leonardo Bonilha,Julius Fridriksson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PRISM adapts subtraction analysis from human neuroimaging to test cognitive specialization in transformer LLMs, revealing parallel phonemic-favoring dissociations in both LLM layers and stroke patients' brains that replicate robustly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current mechanistic interpretability of large language models lacks falsifiable, spatially resolved tools to test whether internal components are specialized for distinct cognitive operations. The paper aims to bridge human neuroimaging methods with transformer analysis.

Method: Adapts subtraction analysis from human neuroimaging to perturbed transformers. Uses PRISM to map seven clinical naming test categories, subtracts error classes pairwise, treats each perturbation seed as a subject in group analysis with threshold-free cluster enhancement along layer axis. Runs structurally matched analysis on 213 chronic post-stroke aphasia patients using correlation-difference lesion-symptom mapping, with both sides replicated on held-out splits.

Result: Both substrates (LLM layers and human cortex) recover robust phonemic-favoring dissociations: a deep layer cluster in LLM and frontal-perisylvian cortical cluster in patients, both replicating. Semantic-favoring direction shows consistently signed but non-significant trend in both. Spatial and subject dimensions match between analyses.

Conclusion: PRISM provides a falsifiable, spatially resolved test of functional specialization claims in transformer language models, demonstrating parallel cognitive dissociations between artificial neural networks and biological brains. Confirmatory ROI-level intervention for stronger causal claims is left for future work.

Abstract: Mechanistic interpretability of large language models lacks spatially resolved, falsifiable tools for testing whether internal components are specialized for distinct cognitive operations. We adapt subtraction analysis, the standard framework of human neuroimaging, from biological brains to perturbed transformers, and apply the same logic to both substrates in parallel. Building on the Brain-LLM Unified Model (BLUM), which showed that layer-perturbed LLaVA-1.6-Vicuna-13B error profiles match the lesion patterns of aphasic patients, we develop PRISM (Perturbation-based Regional Interpretability through Subtraction Mapping). PRISM maps the seven clinical Philadelphia Naming Test categories, subtracts error classes pairwise, and treats each perturbation seed as a subject in a group analysis with threshold-free cluster enhancement along the layer axis. We run a structurally matched analysis on 213 chronic post-stroke aphasia patients using correlation-difference lesion-symptom mapping, and replicate both sides on held-out splits. The designs match in subject dimension (seeds, patients), spatial dimension (layers, atlas-parcellated cortex) and thresholding, but the contrast operator differs: a within-subject error-proportion difference for the LLM, a between-subject correlation difference for the cortex. Both substrates recover a robust phonemic-favoring dissociation, a deep layer cluster and a frontal-perisylvian cortical cluster, both replicating; the semantic-favoring direction is a consistently signed but non-significant trend on both. PRISM thus gives a falsifiable, spatially resolved test of functional-specialization claims in transformer language models. A confirmatory ROI-level intervention (PRISM Stage 3) licensing the strongest causal-mechanism claim is left to subsequent work.

</details>


### [114] [MAG: MAnifold Guided Semi-Supervised Multi-modal In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12724)
*Zirui Cheng,Xun Xu,Tiankai Chen,Fady Rezk,Bowen Zheng,Xiaodong Shi,Shijie Li,Kangkang Lu,Bharadwaj Veeravalli,Nancy F. Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MAG is a semi-supervised framework that leverages unlabeled multi-modal data to improve few-shot in-context learning by selecting high-quality demonstrations through graph-based propagation and multi-modal relevance scoring.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Few-shot multi-modal ICL performance is highly sensitive to demonstration quality/coverage, but abundant unlabeled multi-modal data remains unexploited for improving ICL. Current methods lack efficient ways to leverage unlabeled data for better demonstration selection.

Method: Two-stage framework: 1) Relevance score propagation on multi-modal graph identifies compact set of high-impact unlabeled samples for pseudo-labeling (reducing MLLM inference cost), 2) Multi-modal relevance used to select final demonstrations, with textual representations for propagation and both visual/textual modalities for final selection.

Result: Experiments on eight multi-modal benchmarks show MAG consistently outperforms strong baselines in label-scarce regimes, achieving significant gains with limited pseudo-labeling budget. Textual representations more effective for relevance propagation, while both modalities crucial for demonstration selection.

Conclusion: MAG provides an efficient semi-supervised framework to exploit unlabeled multi-modal data for improving few-shot ICL, demonstrating that thoughtful combination of unlabeled data utilization and multi-modal representation analysis can substantially enhance in-context learning performance in label-limited scenarios.

Abstract: Few-shot in-context learning (ICL) with multi-modal large language models (MLLMs) enables task adaptation without parameter updates, but its performance is highly sensitive to the quality and coverage of the selected demonstrations. While unlabeled multi-modal data is abundant, it remains elusive how to exploit them for ICL. We propose MAG (MAnifold-Guided semi-supervised in-context demonstra- tion selection), an efficient framework that leverages unlabeled data to improve multi-modal ICL. MAG formulates demonstration selection as a semi-supervised propagation problem on a multi-modal graph and adopts a two-stage strategy: (i) relevance score propagation identifies a compact set of high-impact unlabeled samples for pseudo-labeling, reducing MLLM inference cost; (ii) multi-modal relevance is used to select the final demonstrations. We show that textual represen- tations are more effective for relevance propagation, while both visual and textual modalities are crucial for high-quality demonstration selection. Experiments on eight multi-modal benchmarks demonstrate that MAG consistently outperforms strong baselines in label-scarce regimes, achieving significant gains with a limited pseudo-labeling budget.

</details>


### [115] [A Cloud-Edge System for Multimodal Clinical Screening in Resource-Constrained Rural Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12745)
*Hei Ting,Chan,Chenwei Wu,Xueshen Liu,Zesen Zhao,Boyuan Zheng,Luis Filipe Nakayama,Michael G. Morley,Liyue Shen,Jiasi Chen,Z. Morley Mao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A cloud-edge collaborative architecture enables medical AI in resource-constrained rural settings by using lightweight edge models to process raw data and cloud LLMs for clinical synthesis, achieving high diagnostic accuracy with low bandwidth usage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medical AI has demonstrated specialist-level diagnostic accuracy but remains inaccessible in resource-constrained rural settings due to limited bandwidth, compute limitations, and the need to integrate heterogeneous medical modalities for clinical decision-making.

Method: Introduces a cloud-edge collaborative architecture with: 1) lightweight, domain-specific models on edge devices that transform raw medical data into compact structured outputs; 2) a cloud LLM that synthesizes these outputs into clinical summaries; and 3) an LLM-based orchestrator that dynamically selects diagnostic tools based on patient context to ensure comprehensive modality coverage without processing irrelevant inputs.

Result: Evaluated on 20 multimodal clinical cases spanning cardiac, obstetric, trauma, and screening scenarios under three simulated network profiles (500 kbps - 5 Mbps). Achieved 98-99% diagnostic tool recall with 92-96% precision, matched or exceeded cloud-only baselines on clinical accuracy, maintained bandwidth-invariant latency (25-35 seconds), and achieved 4-15x lower token cost.

Conclusion: The hybrid cloud-edge architecture demonstrates that architectural design can enable efficient multimodal integration and improve factual grounding compared to cloud-only approaches, making medical AI accessible in deployment-constrained environments like rural healthcare settings.

Abstract: Medical AI has demonstrated specialist-level diagnostic accuracy, yet these capabilities remain largely inaccessible in resource-constrained rural settings where bandwidth is scarce, compute is limited, and clinical decision-making requires integrating heterogeneous modalities. We introduce a cloud--edge collaborative architecture that addresses these constraints: lightweight, domain-specific models on the edge transform raw medical data into compact structured outputs, while a cloud LLM synthesizes these outputs into clinical summaries. An LLM-based orchestrator dynamically selects diagnostic tools based on patient context, promoting comprehensive modality coverage without processing irrelevant inputs. We evaluate on 20 multimodal clinical cases spanning cardiac, obstetric, trauma, and screening scenarios under three simulated network profiles (500,kbps--5,Mbps). The hybrid system achieves 98--99% diagnostic tool recall with 92--96% precision, matches or exceeds cloud-only baselines on clinical accuracy, and maintains bandwidth-invariant latency (25--35,s) at 4--15x lower token cost. These results highlight the role of architectural design in enabling efficient multimodal integration and improving factual grounding compared to cloud-only approaches under deployment constraints.

</details>


### [116] [Decentralized Multi-Player Q-Learning in Episodic Markov Decision Processes with Information Asymmetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12753)
*Larissa Xu,King Bi,William Chang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Decentralized multi-player RL in tabular MDPs with three information asymmetry settings: unobserved actions with common rewards, observed actions with independent rewards, and unobserved actions with independent rewards, achieving near-optimal regret bounds compared to centralized benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To study decentralized multi-agent reinforcement learning under practical constraints where agents cannot observe each other's actions or have independent reward functions, which are common in real-world multi-agent systems, and to understand how information asymmetry affects learning efficiency compared to centralized approaches.

Method: For problems A (unobserved actions, common rewards) and B (observed actions, independent rewards): mQ-learning and mQ-learning-intervals algorithms achieving Õ(√(H⁴S A_joint T)) regret. For problem C (unobserved actions, independent rewards): mEXC and mEXC-Bellman, two-phase explore-then-commit algorithms achieving Õ(H(S A_joint)^{1/3} T^{2/3}) regret. No communication during learning but predetermined protocols allowed.

Result: Achieved regret bounds of Õ(√(H⁴S A_joint T)) for settings A and B, and Õ(H(S A_joint)^{1/3} T^{2/3}) for setting C. These match the single-agent Q-learning rate up to logarithmic factors compared to centralized joint-action benchmark, despite information asymmetry constraints. Results are most meaningful for small number of players or small per-player action sets due to exponential growth of joint action space.

Conclusion: Decentralized learning under information asymmetry can achieve near-optimal regret rates comparable to centralized approaches, with algorithm choice and regret bounds depending on the specific form of information asymmetry (action observability and reward independence). The exponential dependence on number of players highlights fundamental challenges in decentralized multi-agent RL.

Abstract: We study decentralized multi-player reinforcement learning in episodic tabular Markov decision processes (MDPs) under three forms of information asymmetry: (A) unobserved actions with common rewards, (B) observed actions with independent rewards, and (C) unobserved actions with independent rewards. Players cannot communicate during learning but may agree on a protocol a priori. For Problems A and B we propose \texttt{mQ-learning} and \texttt{mQ-learning-intervals}, achieving $\tilde{O}(\sqrt{H^4 S A_{\text{joint}}\, T})$ regret, where $H$ is the horizon, $S$ the state count, $T = KH$ the total steps, and $A_{\text{joint}} = \prod_{i=1}^M |\mathcal{A}_i|$ the joint action space across $M$ players. For Problem C we give \texttt{mEXC} and \texttt{mEXC-Bellman}, two-phase explore-then-commit algorithms with regret $\tilde{O}(H (S A_{\text{joint}})^{1/3} T^{2/3})$. Against the centralized joint-action benchmark, decentralized learning under information asymmetry matches the single-agent Q-learning rate of \cite{jin2018q} up to logarithmic factors. Because $A_{\text{joint}}$ grows exponentially in $M$, the bounds are most meaningful for small $M$ or small per-player action sets.

</details>


### [117] [Beyond Outcome Rewards: Step-Level Self-Distilled Policy Optimization for Deep Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12764)
*Haoze Wu,Chuqiao Kuang,Tianyi Zhuang,Xiaoguang Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SSPO introduces Evidence Anchors and step-level distillation to improve credit assignment for search agents by using teacher-student disagreement as advantage weights, outperforming GRPO with minimal overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RL provides only sparse trajectory-level rewards for deep search agents spanning dozens of steps, making credit assignment ineffective. While OPSD addresses this with dense token-level teachers, extending it to search agents creates information asymmetry where teachers with privileged information (like correct answers) differ from student's exploration-based reasoning, causing naive distillation to inherit this asymmetry rather than teaching better search strategies.

Method: Two key contributions: 1) Evidence Anchors - concise step-level evidence snippets extracted from the web that capture key reasoning steps without revealing entire answer paths. 2) Step-Level Self-Distilled Policy Optimization (SSPO) - converts teacher-student disagreement into step-level advantage weights within GRPO, applied only to incorrect trajectories. This decouples "what to update" (determined by outcome reward) from "how much to update" (modulated by teacher at each step). Correct trajectories are left untouched to preserve diversity.

Result: On Qwen3-8B, SSPO consistently outperforms GRPO across BrowseComp, GAIA, and FRAMES benchmarks. It surpasses or matches GRPO trained with twice as many gradient steps while adding only about 5% overhead per step from a single additional forward pass.

Conclusion: SSPO effectively addresses the information asymmetry problem in search agent distillation by using Evidence Anchors and step-level advantage weighting, enabling efficient credit assignment while preserving exploration diversity in correct trajectories, resulting in superior performance with minimal computational overhead.

Abstract: Deep search agents operate over trajectories spanning dozens of steps, yet standard reinforcement learning provides only a single outcome reward per trajectory, which is far too sparse for effective credit assignment. On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) addresses this by using the model's own logits as dense token-level teachers, but extending it to search agents introduces a fundamental tension: the teacher, having access to privileged information such as the correct answer, produces a distribution that differs systematically from the student's exploration-based reasoning, and naive distillation causes the student to inherit this information asymmetry rather than learn better search strategies. We resolve this tension through two contributions. First, we construct Evidence Anchors, which are concise, step-level evidence snippets extracted from the web, as privileged information that captures key reasoning steps without revealing the entire answer path. Second, we propose Step-Level Self-Distilled Policy Optimization (SSPO), which converts teacher-student disagreement into step-level advantage weights within GRPO, applied exclusively to incorrect trajectories. This design decouples what to update from how much to update: the outcome reward determines the direction of policy change, while the teacher modulates its magnitude at each step. Correct trajectories are left untouched, preserving their diversity. On Qwen3-8B, SSPO consistently outperforms GRPO across BrowseComp, GAIA, and FRAMES, surpassing or matching GRPO trained with twice as many gradient steps while adding only about 5 percent overhead per step from a single additional forward pass.

</details>


### [118] [CoMedBench: A Multi-Source Benchmark of Synthetic Medical Data Fidelity and Downstream Utility](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12805)
*Akanta Das,Al Amin Farhad,Mrinmoy Sarkar Anto,David Rehkopf,Ayin Vala,Tanmoy Sarkar Pias*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CoMedBench is a reproducible benchmark for evaluating synthetic clinical data generators across 37 dataset-task pairs, showing that synthetic data can preserve most downstream signal but temporal ICU tasks are more challenging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct use of electronic health records for healthcare ML development is constrained by privacy regulations and re-identification risks. Synthetic data offers a practical alternative but prior studies have limited scope, making it difficult to know when synthetic data effectively preserves task-critical clinical signals.

Method: Created CoMedBench, a reproducible benchmark with common clinical-validity framework and shared training/evaluation engine. Evaluated family of synthetic data generators across 37 dataset-task pairs spanning static tabular (20) and temporal ICU time-series (17) tasks from seven public data sources including MIMIC databases and CDC datasets.

Result: Synthetic training data preserves most downstream signal: on tabular tasks, CoMed-CTGAN retains mean AUROC utility of 90.6%, rising to 97.3% for CoMed-TVAE. Temporal ICU tasks are more generator-sensitive: CoMed-CTGAN retains 81.6% (AUROC) and 64.0% (AUPRC), while CoMed-TVAE retains ~95% (AUROC).

Conclusion: Synthetic clinical data can effectively support model development, particularly for tabular tasks where high utility is maintained. However, temporal ICU tasks present greater challenges and require more advanced generators. CoMedBench provides a standardized framework for evaluating synthetic data quality in healthcare applications.

Abstract: Access to clinical data is essential for developing reliable healthcare machine learning systems, but direct use of electronic health records is constrained by privacy regulation, institutional review, data-use agreements, and the risk of re-identification. Synthetic data promises a practical alternative: it can preserve useful statistical and clinical structure while reducing exposure of sensitive patient records. Prior studies often evaluate a single generator, one dataset, or a narrow downstream task, making it difficult to know when synthetic data can support model development and when it fails to preserve task-critical signal. We introduce CoMedBench, a reproducible benchmark that evaluates a family of generators under a common clinical-validity framework and one shared training and evaluation engine, spanning static tabular and temporal downstream tasks on established critical-care datasets. In total the benchmark spans 37 dataset-task pairs across two modalities consists of 20 static tabular and 17 temporal ICU time-series-drawn from seven public data sources: three intensive-care databases (MIMIC-III, MIMIC-IV, and eICU) together with the UCI Machine Learning Repository, the CDC BRFSS diabetes cohort (2015), NHANES (1999-2014), and the pycox survival datasets (GBSG and METABRIC). The benchmark evaluates both statistical fidelity and task utility by comparing models trained and tested across real and synthetic data. In these settings, synthetic training data preserves most of the downstream signal: on tabular tasks the reference generator CoMed-CTGAN retains a mean AUROC utility (the synthetic-to-real performance ratio) of 90.6%, rising to 97.3% for the strongest generator, CoMed-TVAE. Temporal ICU tasks are harder and more generator-sensitive: CoMed-CTGAN retains 81.6% (AUROC) and only 64.0% under the imbalance-sensitive AUPRC, whereas CoMed-TVAE still retains ~95% (AUROC).

</details>


### [119] [HiRoute: Hierarchical Routed Prompt Tuning for Safety Alignment of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12821)
*Fangzhou Chen,Shiji Zhao,Mengyang Wang,Qihui Zhu,Ranjie Duan,Maoxun Yuan,Xingxing Wei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HiRoute is an input-adaptive hierarchical prompt-tuning framework that uses a two-stage approach with a router to detect harmful content and apply risk-specific prompt experts, achieving better safety while reducing over-refusal compared to static prompt methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current parameter-efficient safety alignment methods for LLMs using prompt tuning rely on static designs (single global prompt or externally selected prompt modules), which struggle to maintain cross-category safety boundaries while generating constructive responses tailored to specific risks and avoiding over-refusal of benign inputs.

Method: HiRoute uses an input-adaptive hierarchical prompt-tuning framework that separates category-agnostic safety control from category-specific response guidance. It trains a lightweight hierarchical router on LLM representations to detect harmful intent and predict multi-label risk scores. Then freezes both backbone and router, using preference optimization with alternating gradient updates to learn a shared coarse-grained prompt and fine-grained prompt experts as continuous embeddings.

Result: Experiments across three instruction-tuned models show HiRoute achieves high safety rates across multiple safety benchmarks while preserving safe-response helpfulness, reducing over-refusal, and maintaining competitive performance on general-purpose tasks.

Conclusion: HiRoute provides an effective hierarchical prompt-tuning approach that adapts to input risks, improving safety alignment while maintaining response quality and reducing false refusals compared to static prompt-based methods.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) remain vulnerable to harmful requests and jailbreak attacks. Parameter-efficient safety alignment methods based on prompt tuning typically rely on a single global prompt or externally selected prompt modules. Such static designs struggle to maintain a cross-category safety boundary while generating constructive responses tailored to specific risks and avoiding over-refusal of benign inputs. To address these limitations, we propose HiRoute, an input-adaptive hierarchical prompt-tuning framework that separates category-agnostic safety control from category-specific response guidance. HiRoute first trains a lightweight hierarchical router on representations extracted from a frozen LLM to jointly detect harmful intent and predict multi-label risk scores. It then freezes both the backbone model and the router and uses preference optimization with alternating gradient updates to learn a shared coarse-grained prompt and a set of fine-grained prompt experts as continuous embeddings. At inference time, benign inputs bypass the safety branch, whereas risky inputs are processed using the shared prompt together with a router-weighted mixture of risk-specific prompt experts. Experiments across three instruction-tuned models show that HiRoute achieves high safety rates across multiple safety benchmarks while preserving safe-response helpfulness, reducing over-refusal, and maintaining competitive performance on general-purpose tasks.

</details>


### [120] [Fast A/B/n Testing: Exact Multi-Policy Comparison via Tree-Coupled Feedback Sharing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12831)
*Yuxiao Wen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TCAB is a novel feedback-sharing design for A/B testing multiple adaptive policies that reduces the number of expensive reward queries from JT to T+o(T) while maintaining exact policy trajectory laws.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online platforms need to compare many adaptive decision policies (ranking systems, recommendation algorithms, etc.), but each reward-bearing interaction is costly or risky. Traditional A/B/n testing requires JT outcomes, which is inefficient when J is large.

Method: Tree-Coupled A/B Testing (TCAB) creates a predictable tree connecting policy histories at each round. Parent-child context-action laws are maximally coupled, with one reward shared within each component of matched tree edges. Policies remain dependent while retaining their exact standalone trajectory laws. The design uses a current-round minimum-spanning tree that is myopically optimal.

Result: The number of reward queries N(T)=T+∑_{t,e}D_{e,t}, where D_{e,t} records mismatches. This equals T plus cumulative tree-edge total variation in expectation. For fixed J, if every policy has sublinear pseudo-regret and the oracle action is almost surely unique, then 𝔼[N(T)]=T+o(T), compared to JT for independent runs. The method also provides finite-sample variance bounds for pairwise policy contrasts.

Conclusion: TCAB significantly improves the cost-precision frontier for evaluating multiple adaptive policies by sharing feedback across dependent policy trajectories while maintaining exact policy laws, offering substantial practical benefits for reward-model evaluation, language-model evaluation, and adaptive search policies.

Abstract: Online platforms increasingly compare many adaptive decision policies---ranking systems, recommendation algorithms, pricing rules, and language-model agents---while each reward-bearing interaction can be costly or risky. A direct A/B/n design gives each of $J$ policies its own horizon-$T$ trajectory and therefore uses $JT$ outcomes. We introduce Tree-Coupled A/B Testing (\TCAB), an exact feedback-sharing design for arbitrary history-dependent contextual-bandit policies. At each round, a predictable tree connects the current policy histories; every parent--child context--action law is maximally coupled, and one reward is shared within each component of matched tree edges. Every policy retains exactly its standalone finite-horizon trajectory law, even though the policies are deliberately dependent. If $D_{e,t}$ records a mismatch on tree edge $e$ at round $t$, the number of reward queries satisfies the pathwise identity $N(T)=T+\sum_{t,e}D_{e,t}$ and hence equals $T$ plus cumulative tree-edge total variation in expectation. This cost is conditionally optimal among exact edge-local designs on the selected tree, and a current-round minimum-spanning tree is myopically optimal among tree designs. For fixed $J$, sublinear pseudo-regret of every policy and almost-sure uniqueness of the oracle action imply $\mathbb{E}[N(T)]=T+o(T)$, versus $JT$ for independent runs. We also obtain finite-sample variance bounds for pairwise policy contrasts. Experiments on reward-model evaluation, multiple-choice language-model evaluation, and adaptive search policies demonstrate substantial improvements in the cost--precision frontier.

</details>


### [121] [A Compositional Theory of Curvature in Probabilistic Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12869)
*Hrithik Suresh,Sahil Sidheekh,Shelar Parth Vijay,Yasir Z,Sriraam Natarajan,Narayanan Chatapuram Krishnan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper analyzes Probabilistic Circuits' loss surface curvature, showing that global sharpness regularization is misspecified for these models. It introduces an adaptive, node-specific regularization that preserves closed-form EM updates and improves generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Global sharpness regularization for Probabilistic Circuits is problematic because it doesn't account for the compositional nature of PC curvature, leading to depth bias and underfitting.

Method: The authors prove that each sum node's Hessian trace contribution factorizes into circuit flow (usage) and local sharpness. They then develop an adaptive sharpness-aware regularizer that penalizes nodes based on their intrinsic local curvature rather than applying global regularization.

Result: The adaptive regularization maintains closed-form EM updates and empirically recovers the generalization performance that global regularization sacrifices, while retaining the robustness benefits of sharpness-aware learning.

Conclusion: Targeted, node-specific sharpness regularization that respects the compositional structure of Probabilistic Circuits outperforms global regularization approaches, offering better generalization without sacrificing computational efficiency.

Abstract: Probabilistic Circuits (PCs) are generative models that support exact inference and, unlike deep neural networks, admit an exact and tractable measure of loss-surface curvature: the trace of the Hessian of the log-likelihood. Recent work regularizes this trace globally to bias learning toward flatter, better generalizing optima. We show that treating sharpness as a global regularizer can be misspecified for PCs, whose curvature is inherently compositional. We prove that each sum node's contribution to the Hessian trace factorizes exactly into its circuit flow, which measures how heavily the node is used, and a local sharpness term determined by its output distribution. This decomposition provides insights into why global sharpness regularization is depth biased and can lead to underfitting. Building on it, we introduce an adaptive sharpness aware regularizer that penalizes nodes based on intrinsic local curvature and preserves closed form EM updates. We also show that empirically, this targeted regularization recovers the generalization that global regularization sacrifices while retaining the robustness and benefits of sharpness aware learning.

</details>


### [122] [Sustaining Plasticity via Learnable Wavelet Activations in Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12874)
*Zeyang Zhang,Tieliang Gong,Junyan Lu,Weizhan Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a novel learnable wavelet activation function that decomposes activations into low and high-frequency components to address plasticity loss in continual learning, with dynamic injection and regularization to balance plasticity and stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Plasticity loss in continual learning significantly hinders acquisition of sequential tasks. Existing fixed activation functions suffer from spectral bias toward low-frequency variations, while learnable variants allow unconstrained updates that cause catastrophic forgetting.

Method: Proposes a learnable wavelet activation that decomposes activation functions into low-frequency and high-frequency components to counter spectral bias. Uses dynamic wavelet injection to adaptively enhance plasticity for new tasks, and regularization to maintain stability of previously learned knowledge.

Result: The approach maintains superior trainability and generalization throughout the learning process and achieves state-of-the-art performance across diverse continual learning benchmarks. Theoretical guarantees include mathematical proofs of structural necessity and decoupled learning rate mechanism effectiveness.

Conclusion: The proposed wavelet-based activation framework successfully addresses plasticity loss in continual learning by balancing spectral decomposition, adaptive injection for new tasks, and regularization for stability, with both theoretical guarantees and empirical validation.

Abstract: Plasticity loss has emerged as a critical challenge in continual learning that significantly hinders the acquisition of sequential tasks. While optimizing activation designs offers a potential solution, current fixed-form functions suffer from an inherent spectral bias towards low-frequency variations, whereas learnable variants permit unconstrained updates that induce catastrophic forgetting. To address these limitations, we propose a novel learnable wavelet activation that decomposes the activation function into low-frequency and high-frequency components to explicitly counter spectral bias. Furthermore, we employ dynamic wavelet injection to adaptively enhance plasticity for new tasks, alongside a regularization strategy to ensure the stability of previous learned knowledge. Theoretically, we provide rigorous mathematical guarantees for the proposed framework, proving the structural necessity of the hybrid wavelet architecture for efficient $L^2$ approximation and demonstrating that the decoupled learning rate mechanism successfully restores network plasticity for high-frequency information. Additionally, we provide a formal derivation of the loss-driven injection trigger mechanism to precisely guide the injection. Extensive empirical evaluations demonstrate that our approach maintains superior trainability and generalization throughout the learning process and achieves state-of-the-art performance across diverse continual learning benchmarks.

</details>


### [123] [Robust data-driven discovery of fractional differential equations via weak formulations and Pareto-based subset selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12879)
*Pongpisit Thanasutives,Yoshinobu Kawahara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Weak-Pareto is a method for discovering fractional partial differential equations from noisy data by combining weak formulations with Pareto-based subset selection, demonstrating superior noise robustness compared to strong-form and neural approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fractional PDE discovery from noisy data is challenging because fractional differentiation amplifies high-frequency measurement noise and derivative orders are typically unknown. Traditional approaches struggle with noise sensitivity in both linear and nonlinear terms.

Method: Combines adjoint-consistent weak formulation of fractional terms (transferring fractional operators from measured fields to smooth test functions) with Pareto-based subset selection over discrete term types and continuous fractional orders. Uses ridge regression for coefficients within branch-aware differential-evolution search over orders, then selects support size at validation-error-complexity elbow.

Result: Weak-Pareto successfully recovers parsimonious structures from clean and noisy measurements across fractional advection-diffusion, reaction-diffusion, and Burgers benchmarks. Retains correct support at all tested multiplicative-noise levels, unlike unregularized strong-form counterpart which fails with noise. Shows consistent operator recovery and lower runtime than neural baseline.
