Conclusion: The weak formulation enables noise-robust discovery of fractional PDEs, with Pareto search avoiding support-selection failures of dense fixed dictionaries. The approach effectively handles both linear and nonlinear terms, demonstrating practical applicability for scientific discovery from noisy experimental data.

Abstract: Fractional partial differential equations describe nonlocal dynamics, but discovering them from noisy data is difficult because fractional differentiation amplifies high-frequency measurement noise and the derivative orders are unknown. We propose Weak-Pareto, which combines an adjoint-consistent weak formulation of fractional terms with Pareto-based subset selection over discrete term types and continuous fractional orders. For linear right-hand-side terms, the adjoint transfers fractional operators from measured fields to smooth test functions, replacing noise-sensitive pointwise differentiation with smoothing integration; for nonlinear terms, the noise-suppression effect is partial yet useful. Coefficients are fitted by ridge regression within a branch-aware differential-evolution search over the orders. The support size is then selected at the validation-error-complexity elbow. We show that the variance of fixed linear right-hand-side weak features vanishes under grid refinement, whereas noise amplification in pointwise fractional features increases with derivative order. Across fractional advection-diffusion, reaction-diffusion, and Burgers benchmarks, Weak-Pareto recovers parsimonious structures from clean and noisy measurements. In controlled advection-diffusion and Burgers comparisons, it retains the correct support at every tested multiplicative-noise level, whereas the unregularised strong-form counterpart largely fails once noise is introduced; this advantage persists under additive Gaussian noise. Ablations show that the weak library drives noise robustness and that continuous-order Pareto search avoids the support-selection failure of a dense fixed dictionary. On the advection-diffusion benchmark, Weak-Pareto yields more consistent operator recovery and substantially lower measured runtime than a contemporary neural baseline.

</details>


### [124] [Adaptive $k$ Nearest Neighbors Classifier via Granular Ball Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12903)
*Xiaoyu Lian,Shuyin Xia,Hongxuan He,Lifeng Shen,Guoyin Wang,Xinbo Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel adaptive KNN method using granular-ball computing that dynamically determines k values through multi-granularity data representation, improving both accuracy and efficiency over traditional KNN variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional KNN has a key limitation in selecting the optimal k value, which significantly impacts performance. Fixed k values are suboptimal across different data distributions, and manual tuning is time-consuming and dataset-dependent.

Method: Two-stage approach: 1) Training stage - coarse data partitioning via granular balls using Fisher criterion for splitting control, creating multi-granularity representation; 2) Prediction stage - weighted distance mechanism to locate nearest granular ball, construct adaptive neighborhood around test sample, with k dynamically determined by actual samples in this neighborhood.

Result: The method outperforms existing KNN variants across multiple datasets in both accuracy and efficiency. The granular-ball neighborhood provides more stable local group information, enhancing robustness against noise and local perturbations.

Conclusion: The proposed granular-ball computing approach provides an effective solution to the k-value selection problem in KNN, offering adaptive neighborhood construction and improved performance while maintaining computational efficiency.

Abstract: The $k$-Nearest Neighbor~(KNN) algorithm is widely used across various tasks. The selection of the $k$ value is a key issue because it significantly impacts performance. In this paper, an adaptive and efficient KNN approach via granular-ball computing is proposed. The method consists of two stages. \textcolor{black}{In the training stage, the dataset is first coarsely partitioned to reduce the complexity of data distributions within a granular ball, and then the Fisher criterion is introduced to control ball splitting and stopping, yielding a multi-granularity granular ball representation. In the prediction stage, the nearest granular ball is first located through a weighted distance mechanism, and an adaptive neighborhood is then constructed around the test sample. The effective $k$ value is dynamically determined by the actual number of samples contained in this neighborhood. The neighborhood induced by the nearest granular ball provides more stable local group information, thereby improving robustness against noise and local perturbations.} Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed method outperforms existing KNN variants across multiple datasets in terms of both accuracy and efficiency. The code has been open-sourced for reproducibility: https://github.com/lianxiaoyu724/Adaptive-GBKNN.

</details>


### [125] [EGRL: Edge generation-guided relation-aware learning for RNA-protein interaction prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12906)
*Danyu Li,Ling Zhou,Rubing Huang,Xian Zhong,Bin Zou,Kui Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EGRL is a novel Graph Neural Network framework that improves RNA-Protein Interaction prediction, especially in cold-start scenarios with unknown molecules, through implicit meta-path learning and edge generation techniques.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional wet-lab experiments for RPI detection are costly and time-consuming, and existing computational methods (particularly GNN-based approaches) struggle with data sparsity and generalization to cold-start scenarios involving unknown molecules.

Method: EGRL includes implicit meta-path learning to capture relational semantics without handcrafted paths, multi-relation-aware attention for adaptive fusion of interaction patterns, a graph generator that predicts potential edges for cold-start nodes, and a multi-feature fusion predictor for final scoring, jointly trained with primary and auxiliary generator losses.

Result: EGRL achieves competitive overall performance and superior generalization in cold-start settings, with AUROC of 0.867 and AUPR of 0.861 on unknown molecules, representing improvements of 8.6% in AUROC and 5.0% in AUPR over prior state-of-the-art methods.

Conclusion: The proposed EGRL framework effectively addresses limitations of existing GNN-based methods for RPI prediction, particularly demonstrating strong generalization capabilities in challenging cold-start scenarios with unknown molecules.

Abstract: RNA-Protein Interactions (RPIs) are critical for regulating cellular functions. While traditional wet-lab experiments for RPI detection are costly and time-consuming, Deep Learning (DL) methods provide an efficient computational alternative for RPI Prediction (RPIP). In particular, Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) are promising, as they naturally model RPI networks. However, existing GNN-based methods often rely on homogeneous graphs or predefined meta-paths, which limit their ability to handle data sparsity and to generalize to cold-start scenarios involving unknown molecules. To address these limitations, we propose Edge Generation-guided Relation-aware Learning (EGRL), a novel framework with several key components: implicit meta-path learning to capture relational semantics without handcrafted paths; a multi-relation-aware attention mechanism for adaptive fusion of interaction patterns; a graph generator that predicts potential ("soft") edges to support cold-start nodes; and a multi-feature fusion predictor for final interaction scoring. EGRL is jointly trained with a primary task loss and an auxiliary generator loss. Comprehensive evaluations on four benchmark datasets demonstrate that EGRL achieves competitive overall performance. More importantly, it exhibits superior generalization in cold-start settings, achieving an Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic curve (AUROC) of 0.867 and an Area Under the Precision-Recall curve (AUPR) of 0.861 on unknown molecules, corresponding to improvements of 8.6% in AUROC and 5.0% in AUPR over prior state-of-the-art methods. The code will be released soon.

</details>


### [126] [Revisiting Overestimation Bias Problem of Q-learning: Settling Large Discrete Action Space via Action Intersection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12912)
*Pu Li,Tao Tan,Hong Xie,Xiaoyu Shi,Mingsheng Shang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Action Intersection Strategy addresses Q-learning overestimation bias in large action spaces by combining coupling and decoupling paradigms through data sharing, enabling controllable bias adjustment from underestimation to overestimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large action spaces increase randomness in Q-value estimation, causing bottlenecks in existing methods: coupling paradigm suffers from positive bias (overestimation) while decoupling paradigm suffers from negative bias (underestimation).

Method: Action Intersection Strategy enables semi-decoupling via: (1) allowing two Q-functions to share a fraction of trajectory data, (2) using coupling paradigm for shared data and decoupling for unshared data, providing fine granularity control over bias.

Result: Method achieves superior performance in deep RL experiments compared to SOTA baselines, with tabular experiments explaining the performance advantages through bias control capabilities.

Conclusion: Action intersection is a simple yet powerful strategy that overcomes bottlenecks in existing Q-learning bias correction methods by enabling controllable semi-decoupling with fine-grained bias adjustment from underestimation to overestimation.

Abstract: This paper considers the overestimation bias problem of Q-learning in the setting of a large action space, for the purpose of relieving the bottleneck of existing methods. We find that the large action space increases the randomness in Q-value estimation. The randomness makes two paradigms that drive the major literature on the overestimation problem have their own bottlenecks: the coupling paradigm, i.e., the optimal action and its Q-value are estimated with the same Q-function, always has a positive bias. This is because randomness leads to some actions having abnormally high estimated values than their true values, and the coupling methods prefer these actions. The decoupling paradigm, i.e., the optimal action and its Q-value are estimated with two independent Q-functions, always has a negative bias. This is because randomness increases the estimation gap between the two independent Q-tables for the same action. This paper shows that action intersection can be a simple yet powerful strategy to relieve these bottlenecks. The action intersection strategy enables semi-decoupling via two designs: (1) it allows two Q-functions to share a certain fraction of trajectory data; (2) if a data sample is shared, each Q-function is updated using the coupling paradigm; otherwise, using the decoupling paradigm. Two properties make the action intersection strategy powerful: (1) attaining a large bias range, i.e., varying the data sharing fraction, the estimation bias varies from underestimating to overestimating; (2) fine granularity: the action intersection size can be made arbitrarily finer to enable finer control. We consider two experiment settings, i.e., tabular and deep RL, deep RL experiments show that our method outperforms several SOTA baselines drastically; tabular experiments reveal why our method can achieve superior performance.

</details>


### [127] [Towards Socially Compliant Navigation in Deep Reinforcement Learning via Proxemics-Based Reward Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12917)
*Takieddine Soualhi,Jacques Saraydaryan,Laetitia Matignon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a novel proxemics-based reward formulation for DRL social navigation that improves social compliance metrics while maintaining navigation efficiency in crowded environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current DRL methods for robot navigation in crowded environments often focus too much on task-centric objectives and underrepresent social compliance objectives, lacking interpretable social learning signals.

Method: The authors propose a novel proxemics-based reward formulation that models each human's personal space as a radial Gaussian-mixture field derived from Hall's proxemics theory, and computes a robot-centric local cost over the robot's field of view. This approach is integrated into established DRL navigation methods.

Result: The proposed reward consistently improves social metrics in simulation across multiple crowd scenarios, reward baselines, and crowd densities while maintaining competitive navigation performance relative to compared reward models.

Conclusion: The proxemics-based reward formulation provides a dense, interpretable social learning signal that enhances social compliance in robot navigation while preserving navigation efficiency in crowded environments.

Abstract: Developing effective robot navigation methods in crowded environments is essential for real-world applications. Although recent deep reinforcement learning (DRL) methods have improved navigation performance in crowded environments, they often focus primarily on task-centric objectives and underrepresent social compliance objectives. In this paper, we introduce a novel proxemics-based reward formulation for DRL social navigation that provides a dense, interpretable social learning signal while maintaining navigation efficiency. Our approach models each human's personal space as a radial Gaussian-mixture field derived from Hall's proxemics theory and computes a robot-centric local cost over the robot's field of view. We integrate the proposed reward into established DRL navigation methods and evaluate it in simulation across multiple crowd scenarios, reward baselines, and crowd densities using both navigation metrics and social metrics. Results show that the proposed reward consistently improves social metrics in simulation while maintaining competitive navigation performance relative to the compared reward models.

</details>


### [128] [Momentum as Residual-Driven Multiplier Correction for Deep Learning Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12925)
*Zhixin Ren,Yau Lyu,Congrong Li,Liping Zhang,Shengbo Eben Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AIM framework interprets momentum through ADMM-style variable splitting, leading to RADAR optimizer with relativistic adaptive geometry and improved momentum estimation for better performance across DL tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current momentum-based optimizers have unclear relations between momentum recursion, update geometry, and acceleration mechanisms - these concepts are usually intertwined without clear theoretical separation.

Method: Developed AIM framework using residual-penalty variable splitting to interpret momentum as multiplier-like correction; then proposed RADAR combining relativistic adaptive geometry, decoupled residual correction, and second-order momentum filtering.

Result: RADAR shows consistent improvements over strong adaptive optimizer baselines in supervised vision learning, language modeling, and reinforcement learning tasks.

Conclusion: The AIM framework provides novel interpretation of momentum via ADMM-style splitting, enabling development of improved optimizers like RADAR with better theoretical understanding and empirical performance.

Abstract: Momentum-based optimizers are widely used in modern deep learning, yet the relations among momentum recursion, update geometry, and acceleration remain only partially understood. We develop an $\textbf{A}$DMM-$\textbf{I}$nspired $\textbf{M}$omentum (AIM) framework based on residual-penalty variable splitting, which interprets momentum as a multiplier-like correction driven by the splitting residual. AIM recovers the exponential moving average of gradients from an ADMM-style multiplier update and separates two mechanisms that are usually intertwined in practical optimizers: the residual penalty determines the update geometry, whereas the approximation of the objective-related subproblem determines the acceleration form. Building on AIM, we propose $\textbf{R}$elativistic $\textbf{A}$daptive gradient $\textbf{D}$escent with $\textbf{A}$ccelerated $\textbf{R}$esidual (RADAR), which combines relativistic adaptive geometry, decoupled residual correction, and second-order momentum filtering to improve the update direction and momentum estimation. We establish stochastic convergence through a variance-perturbed Lyapunov drift analysis. Experiments on supervised vision learning, language modeling, and reinforcement learning show that RADAR achieves consistent improvements over strong adaptive optimizer baselines.

</details>


### [129] [H-VAEP and H-xT: Valuing Offensive On-the-Ball Actions in Handball by Estimating Probabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12926)
*Julius Broermann,Oliver Müller,Michael Döring,Jochen Baumeister*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: First adaptation of xT and VAEP action valuation frameworks to handball using Bundesliga tracking data, with improved zoning layouts and feature optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional handball player evaluation uses basic metrics that fail to capture multi-player build-up chains. While soccer analytics has adopted advanced frameworks like xT and VAEP, these haven't been adapted to handball, creating an analytical gap.

Method: Adapted xT to handball with handball-native court zoning (Handball-xT/H-xT) using simulations to show superiority over rectangular grids. Optimized VAEP for handball (Handball-VAEP/H-VAEP) by tailoring feature space and selecting context length to limit team-identity leakage. Used five seasons of tracking-derived event data from Handball Bundesliga.

Result: H-xT is systematically more robust than standard grids. H-VAEP produces exceptionally stable, discriminative, and intuitive player ratings that highlight build-up play. The complete code repository is released for professional club deployment.

Conclusion: Successfully adapted soccer analytics frameworks to handball, creating robust tools for player evaluation that credit build-up play, with practical deployment potential for professional clubs through released code.

Abstract: Traditional player evaluation in professional handball relies on basic box-score metrics or heuristic indices, which fail to credit the multi-player build-up chain. While football (soccer) analytics has adopted Expected Threat (xT) and Valuing Actions by Estimating Probabilities (VAEP), these event-based action valuation frameworks have not yet been adapted to handball. In this paper, we present the first comprehensive adaptation and evaluation of xT and VAEP for handball, utilizing five seasons of tracking-derived event data from the Handball Bundesliga. We develop Handball-xT (H-xT) using a handball-native court zoning layout, demonstrating via simulations that it is systematically more robust than standard rectangular grids. We optimize Handball-VAEP (H-VAEP) by tailoring its feature space and selecting the context length to limit team-identity leakage. Our evaluation shows that H-VAEP yields exceptionally stable, discriminative, and intuitive player ratings that highlight build-up play. Finally, we release our complete code repository to help professional clubs deploy these models.

</details>


### [130] [Multi-perspective Imbalance-Conscious 6G Beamforming Optimization and Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12929)
*Chukwunonso Henry Nwokoye,Blessing Oluchi Iloka,Chikwue V. Umeugoji,Christopher Anene Egemba,Nnenna D. Duroha*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Systematic ML study comparing supervised and unsupervised approaches for 6G-IoT beamforming optimization, showing network features have best predictive power and deployment environment/device type drive clustering patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically investigate machine learning approaches for optimizing 6G-IoT beamforming (6GBO), comparing different feature groups and exploring unsupervised perspectives to enhance beamforming optimization.

Method: Used both supervised and unsupervised ML approaches. For supervised learning, compared predictive power of four feature groups (network, environmental, device, vision). For unsupervised learning, applied K-means, DBSCAN, hierarchical clustering to cluster network scenarios, evaluated with Elbow, Silhouette score, Davies-Bouldin Index. Conducted imbalance-aware experiments and explainability analysis.

Result: Network features showed best predictive power for 6GBO based on recall, F1-score, ROC-AUC values. Unsupervised clustering revealed deployment environment and device type primarily influence clustering patterns rather than mobility attributes. Explainability analysis identified bandwidth, IoT sensors, and mobility as having highest global feature importance across groups.

Conclusion: Network features are most important for 6GBO prediction, and clustering is driven by environment/device factors. Future work will apply deep learning and reinforcement learning for throughput/latency prediction and performance optimization.

Abstract: The study presents a systematic machine learning (ML) study of 6G-IoT beamforming optimization (6GBO) using supervised and unsupervised approaches. We compared the predictive power of network, environmental, device, and vision feature groups for 6GBO. Additionally, it addressed other unsupervised perspectives that can enhance 6GBO, including clustering network scenarios using methods such as K-means, DBSCAN, and hierarchical clustering. Several imbalance-aware experiments revealed that network features possess better prediction power than device, environmental, and vision feature groups, as evidenced by their recall, F1-score and ROC-AUC values. For unsupervised ML exploration (assessed using Elbow, Silhouette score, and Davies-Bouldin Index methods), the results indicate that the deployment environment and type of device primarily influence clustering, rather than mobility-based attributes. Furthermore, the explainability analysis showed that bandwidth, IoT sensors, and mobility possess higher global feature importance across the feature groups. In the future, we would apply deep and reinforcement learning techniques to predict throughput/latency or to optimize rewards determined by performance indicators like SNR enhancement

</details>


### [131] [Diagnosing JEPA World Models with Action-Conditioned Predictive Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12939)
*Guo An,Zijing Wu,Honghua Dong,Yuhao Yan,Zixuan Gui,Haochong Chen,Shanzhao Ruan,Xiang Wang,Yurong Ling,Qi Tian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces Action-Conditioned Predictive Consistency (ACPC) as a diagnostic tool to measure how visual perturbations affect world models in joint-embedding predictive architectures, with theoretical guarantees and empirical validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Joint-embedding predictive architectures (JEPAs) learn world models in compact latent spaces to reduce modeling of nuisance appearance, but they still lack guarantees against visual perturbations that can alter encoded representations and affect action-conditioned predictions.

Method: The authors introduce Action-Conditioned Predictive Consistency (ACPC), which measures divergence between clean and visually perturbed history rollouts under the same action sequence. They define two complementary measures: Invariance Radius (IR) for clean-perturbed rollout spread and Separation Rate (SR) to check if different states remain distinguishable after rollout.

Result: Experiments on four visual control tasks show that pairwise ACPC predicts perturbation-induced prediction and cost changes. On LeWM, the IR-SR screen transfers across tasks and remains informative under blur and resize. PLDM exhibits similar diagnostic trends under different architectures.

Conclusion: ACPC provides a principled diagnostic tool with theoretical guarantees for assessing visual perturbation robustness in world models, drawing inspiration from bisimulation theory to ensure states with identical action-conditioned consequences are treated similarly.

Abstract: Joint-embedding predictive architectures (JEPAs) learn world models that predict in a compact latent space rather than in pixels, reducing the pressure to model nuisance appearance. Yet this provides no guarantee against visual perturbations: they can still alter the encoded representation and affect subsequent action-conditioned predictions. Bisimulation captures this requirement precisely: two observations should be treated as the same state only when their action-conditioned consequences agree. Guided by this criterion, we introduce Action-Conditioned Predictive Consistency (ACPC), a diagnostic that measures how far a clean history and a visually perturbed view of it diverge after being rolled forward under the same action sequence. We prove that this divergence bounds the perturbation-induced change in multi-step prediction error and planner cost. Building on pairwise ACPC, we define two complementary measures: the Invariance Radius (IR) summarizes clean-perturbed rollout spread, while the Separation Rate (SR) checks whether different states remain distinguishable after rollout. Experiments on four visual control tasks show that pairwise ACPC predicts perturbation-induced prediction and cost changes. On LeWM, the IR-SR screen transfers across tasks, and the joint diagnostic remains informative under blur and resize. PLDM exhibits similar diagnostic trends under a different architecture.

</details>


### [132] [CardioState-JEPA: Delay-Aware Cross-Modal Learning of a Shared Cardiac Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12944)
*Hamza Shafiq,Hung Manh Pham,Bin Zhu,Pan Zhou,Jun Hu,Aaqib Saeed*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CardioState-JEPA is a cardiac foundation model that learns a shared representation across ECG, PPG, and PCG signals using a physiology-aware joint-embedding predictive architecture with temporal alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cardiac foundation models are trained for single sensing modalities (ECG, PPG, or PCG separately), leaving the shared cardiac physiology across these complementary sensors unexploited.

Method: A joint-embedding predictive architecture that maps heterogeneous waveforms into a common token space, processes them with a shared Transformer encoder, and learns by predicting masked latent cardiac states. Uses a learned delay aligner to handle temporal offsets between electrical, mechanical, and hemodynamic events. First learns within-modality structure from abundant unimodal data, then uses paired data to align modalities in latent cardiac time.

Result: Improves average PPG classification by 8.2 AUROC points, PCG murmur detection by 18.8 AUROC points, and ECG classification by 15.5 AUROC points over best self-supervised signal baselines. Matches or exceeds cardiac models trained with privileged clinical text or supervised labels on several ECG benchmarks.

Conclusion: Heterogeneous cardiac signals (ECG, PPG, PCG) can mutually supervise a single foundation model of cardiac physiology, demonstrating that shared representations across complementary sensors significantly improve performance on downstream cardiac tasks.

Abstract: Electrocardiography (ECG), photoplethysmography (PPG), and phonocardiography (PCG) provide complementary views of the same cardiac cycle, yet existing cardiac foundation models are trained for a single sensing modality, leaving the shared physiology across sensors unexploited. We introduce CardioState-JEPA, a cardiac foundation model to learn a single shared representation jointly across ECG, PPG, and PCG, built on a physiology-aware joint-embedding predictive architecture. The model maps heterogeneous waveforms into a common token space, processes them with a single shared Transformer encoder, and learns by predicting masked latent cardiac states, placing the pretraining target on shared physiology rather than sensor-specific waveform appearance. To handle the temporal offsets between electrical, mechanical, and hemodynamic events, cross-modal prediction uses a learned delay aligner that matches signals at the corresponding cardiac time. Because synchronized multi-sensor recordings are scarce, CardioState-JEPA first learns within-modality structure from abundant unimodal data and then uses paired data to align modalities in latent cardiac time. Evaluated as a frozen encoder across 25 downstream tasks spanning ECG, PPG, and PCG, our encoder improves average PPG classification by 8.2 AUROC points, PCG murmur detection by 18.8 AUROC points, and ECG classification by 15.5 AUROC points over the best self-supervised signal baseline and matches or exceeds cardiac models trained with privileged clinical text or supervised labels on several ECG benchmarks. These results establish that heterogeneous cardiac signals can mutually supervise a single foundation model of cardiac physiology.

</details>


### [133] [I-SDPO: Instance-Level Adaptive Self-Distillation Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12957)
*Yubo Zhang,Xinhong Ma,Zezhong Tan,Ziqiang Dong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: I-SDPO: An adaptive RLHF method that switches between group-relative policy optimization and privileged self-distillation based on whether any responses in a group are successful, avoiding biased teacher influence when reward signals become informative.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GRPO suffers when all sampled responses are incorrect (no useful relative signal), while persistent privileged self-distillation throughout training creates a different problem - the teacher becomes a biased, low-variance surrogate that can oppose reward-improving updates once the policy can produce successful trajectories.

Method: I-SDPO makes per-instance routing decisions shared across rollout groups: all-incorrect groups use privileged self-distillation, while groups with any success use standard GRPO. This uses imitation only where group-relative rewards are uninformative, with automatic reduction of distillation rate as success probability rises.

Result: On SciKnowEval, I-SDPO achieves best results in all four scientific domains, improving average mean@16 accuracy from 56.67% (GRPO) to 70.31%, with maximum domain gain of 18.24 percentage points.

Conclusion: Treating teacher reliance as capability-dependent through instance-level adaptive routing between self-distillation and group-relative optimization effectively addresses limitations of both approaches, achieving substantial improvements on scientific reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) learns from reward differences within a rollout group, but receives no useful relative signal when every sampled response is incorrect. Privileged self-distillation can fill this gap with dense token supervision, yet applying it throughout training creates a different failure mode: the teacher is a biased, low-variance surrogate for the reward objective, so persistent imitation can oppose reward-improving updates after the policy becomes capable of producing successful trajectories. We introduce I-SDPO (Instance-Level Adaptive Self-Distillation Policy Optimization), which treats teacher reliance as capability-dependent. I-SDPO makes one routing decision per input instance and shares it across that instance's rollout group: all-incorrect groups use a privileged self-distillation objective, whereas any-success groups remain intact for GRPO. This design uses imitation only where group-relative rewards are uninformative. A local analysis characterizes when teacher and reward directions align and shows that a non-vanishing biased distillation weight induces an optimization bias floor. The routing rule automatically reduces the expected distillation rate as success probability rises, withdrawing teacher influence without a hand-designed schedule. On SciKnowEval, I-SDPO obtains the best result in all four scientific domains and improves average mean@16 accuracy from 56.67% with GRPO to 70.31%, with a maximum domain gain of 18.24 points.

</details>


### [134] [The Objective Is the Bottleneck: Latent World Models Encode What Their Planners Cannot Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12959)
*Joyjeet Singh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper shows that planning failure in latent world models isn't due to prediction degradation but rather the planner's objective function (squared latent distance) which poorly correlates with true distance and even rewards moving away from goals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When planning fails at long horizons in latent world models, conventional wisdom attributes it to prediction degradation. The authors challenge this by investigating whether the planner's objective function is actually the limiting factor.

Method: Analyzed LeWorldModel on TwoRoom environment, using metrics: prediction accuracy vs. horizon, correlation between squared latent distance and true distance, ridge regression probes to recover spatial information from latent embeddings, and comparison of different cost functions.

Result: 1) Prediction remains accurate even at 75 steps ahead (0.189 error vs. frozen world baseline). 2) Squared latent distance correlates poorly with true distance (r=0.426), saturates at intermediate distances, and can reward moving away from goals. 3) Spatial information is fully preserved in latent embeddings (R^2=0.9922). 4) Replacing the objective with a better cost function (without retraining) improved long-horizon goal achievement from 26% to 98%.

Conclusion: The fundamental limitation in latent world model planning is the objective function, not prediction accuracy. Good planners should use cost functions that reflect reachability rather than simple proximity, and the best cost function may not be the most accurate predictor of spatial distance.

Abstract: Latent world models are judged by how well they predict, so when planning fails at long horizons the natural reading is that the predictor degrades. On a reproduction of LeWorldModel on TwoRoom we show the binding constraint is the planner's objective instead.
  The predictor is not the limit: its imagined state seventy-five environment steps ahead is still only 0.189 as wrong as assuming the world froze, while the planner never imagines beyond twenty-five. The objective is. Cross-entropy-method planning minimises squared latent distance, which tracks true distance at r = 0.426, saturates by about eighty arena units and decreases beyond a hundred and twenty, so moving away from the goal can lower the cost. The information is present throughout: a ridge probe recovers position from the frozen embedding at R^2 0.9922.
  The pathology is the method's, not one reimplementation's. It is present in the authors' released weights, and across four checkpoints long-horizon success rank-orders exactly with metric quality and inversely with prediction accuracy.
  Replacing only the objective, with nothing retrained and no GPU, lifts goals reached at offset 100 from 26.0% to 98.0%, equals the 98.0% at offset 25, and reaches 92.0% under a third of the budget: planning stops depending on the horizon. The best cost is not the most accurate. A head learned from frame separation alone predicts spatial distance worse than a position probe (r = 0.819 against 0.9897) yet plans better, charging 24% more to cross the environment's dividing wall where squared latent distance charges 4% less. It has learned reachability, not proximity.

</details>


### [135] [Understanding Backdoor Vulnerabilities in Vertical Federated Learning: The Gap Between Research and Practice](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12962)
*Ziqi Zhao,Jialin Lu,Junjie Shan,Junyuan Zhang,Shuya Yang,Ka-Ho Chow*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Current VFL backdoor attack/defense research fails under realistic conditions due to unrealistic assumptions and poor evaluation practices; this paper proposes practical threat models, attack workflows, and BVBench benchmark for more realistic assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There's a fundamental gap between research and practice in VFL backdoor vulnerability studies. Most existing approaches rely on unrealistic assumptions and knowledge, while evaluation practices are poorly designed, hiding these limitations.

Method: 1) Systematic practice-oriented study of VFL backdoor vulnerabilities; 2) Redefine threat models under realistic constraints; 3) Propose practical backdoor workflows; 4) Introduce BVBench - a backdoor-centric benchmark with state-of-the-art baselines for fair, practical, comprehensive evaluation.

Result: The study reveals that existing approaches overlook key practical constraints and rely on unrealistic prior knowledge. BVBench provides strong evidence of the fragility of current understanding of VFL backdoor risks.

Conclusion: BVBench establishes a foundation for steering VFL backdoor research toward uncovering practical vulnerabilities and developing more meaningful, realistic defenses, bridging the gap between theoretical research and practical deployment.

Abstract: Vertical Federated Learning (VFL) enables organizations holding complementary features of shared entities to collaborate and train models. In this setting, the initiator can withhold information about the learning task, while other contributors participate without exposing their local datasets, creating an asymmetric information structure aligned with growing privacy demands. However, this asymmetry is a double-edged sword. Among various threats, backdoor attacks are particularly concerning because VFL not only enables malicious contributors to poison the model during training, but also allows them to activate the backdoor at inference time to manipulate predictions. Although prior work has reported near-perfect attack success rates and proposed effective defenses, we find that most findings fail to hold under realistic conditions, exposing a fundamental gap between research and practice. In this paper, we present a systematic, practice-oriented study of backdoor vulnerabilities in VFL, revealing this gap in both methodological design and evaluation practices. We show that existing approaches overlook key practical constraints and therefore rely on unrealistic prior knowledge. Furthermore, these limitations have remained hidden due to poorly designed evaluation practices in the literature. To bridge this gap, we redefine threat models under realistic constraints, propose practical backdoor workflows, and introduce BVBench, a backdoor-centric benchmark that enables fair, practical, and comprehensive evaluation, preloaded with state-of-the-art baselines. BVBench provides strong evidence of the fragility of the current understanding of VFL backdoor risks and establishes a foundation for steering research toward uncovering practical vulnerabilities and developing more meaningful defenses.

</details>


### [136] [Comment on "Modeling rapid language learning by distilling Bayesian priors into artificial neural networks"](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12974)
*Orr Well,Idan Tarshish,Nur Lan,Roni Katzir*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper critiques a previous claim that MAML can instill Bayesian priors in ANNs, arguing it merely provides favorable initialization without true Bayesian learning, and shows the approach overfits compared to genuine Bayesian methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To critically examine McCoy & Griffiths (2025)'s claim that Model-Agnostic Meta-Learning (MAML) can distill Bayesian priors into Artificial Neural Networks, and to assess whether their approach truly implements Bayesian learning or merely provides favorable weight initialization.

Method: 1. Theoretical analysis of whether M&G's procedure instills a true Bayesian prior under standard interpretation
2. Consideration of a more permissive interpretation where the system as a whole might implement Bayesian learning
3. Empirical evaluation comparing MAML's performance to genuine Bayesian learners in terms of overfitting and generalization to unseen data

Result: 1. M&G's procedure does not instill a Bayesian prior under standard interpretation - it only initializes network weights favorably while leaving the objective function unchanged
2. Even under a more permissive interpretation, implementing a Bayesian learner without explicit prior faces nontrivial challenges
3. Unlike genuine Bayesian learners, M&G's MAML model overfits and generalizes poorly to unseen data

Conclusion: McCoy & Griffiths' claim that MAML can distill Bayesian priors into ANNs is unfounded - their approach does not implement true Bayesian learning but merely provides good initialization, and empirical results show it lacks the generalization capabilities of genuine Bayesian methods.

Abstract: McCoy & Griffiths (2025, henceforth M&G) suggest that a Bayesian prior can be distilled into Artificial Neural Networks (ANNs) through Model-Agnostic Meta-Learning (MAML, Finn et al., 2017). They support this empirically by showing that meta-trained networks demonstrate formal language learning abilities comparable to Yang & Piantadosi (2023)'s Bayesian learner, significantly outperforming standard ANNs. We point out that under the standard interpretation of a prior, M&G's procedure does not actually instill one; it merely initializes network weights favorably, leaving the objective function unchanged. We then consider a more permissive interpretation, where the system as a whole can be seen as implementing a Bayesian learner even without an explicit prior in the objective. We show that this interpretation faces nontrivial challenges. Finally, we assess how well MAML approximates the empirical results of Bayesian learning, showing that unlike genuine Bayesian learners, M&G's model overfits and generalizes poorly to unseen data.

</details>


### [137] [Learning the Mathematical Property for Designing Low Mutual Coherence Binary Sensing Matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12982)
*Rekha,Santosh Singh,S. K. Neogy*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A learning-based neural network approach constructs binary sensing matrices with low mutual coherence for compressive sensing without using training datasets or specific applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Perfect signal recovery remains challenging in real-world applications. Compressive sensing requires sensing matrices with special properties (RIP, NSP, SP) that are NP-hard problems. Existing methods need to achieve low mutual coherence for practical signal recovery.

Method: Uses a neural network framework to construct binary sensing matrices with low mutual coherence. The method doesn't use training datasets or specific applications - instead uses mathematical constraints/properties to define the loss function. Entries are generated through a shared underlying rule.

Result: Produces binary sensing matrices with low mutual coherence. The architecture is simple, doesn't require large-scale training data, reduces computational costs, and provides generality and robustness while reducing storage requirements.

Conclusion: The proposed approach represents a novel, dataset-free, application-agnostic method for constructing sensing matrices using mathematical properties in a neural network framework, offering computational efficiency and practical advantages for compressive sensing applications.

Abstract: In this research work, we are constructing the sensing matrix, which is essential for the success of the compressive sensing technique. We have chosen a learning-based technique for the construction of the sensing matrix. The novelty and uniqueness of the proposed technique is that it does not use any data set and also does not use a specific application. It uses the mathematical property/constraint for the construction of the sensing matrix for the perfect recovery of the signal. The perfect recovery of signals is an old and still very challenging problem in real-world applications. In late 2000, compressive sensing became a popular mathematical tool for the perfect recovery of sparse signals. The core of the compressive technique is the construction of the sensing matrix, which satisfies certain special properties such as restricted isometry property (RIP), null space property (NSP), and spark property (SP). All these properties are NP-hard problems and hence computationally challenging to solve. For all practical purposes, the construction of the sensing matrix needs to achieve low mutual coherence to achieve the perfect recovery of the signals. We have used a neural network for the construction of the sensing matrix, and this framework constructs a binary sensing matrix with low mutual coherence. The entries in the matrix are generated through a shared underlying rule. The proposed architecture is simple and does not use large-scale training data sets. Such uniqueness and novelty bring a drastic reduction in computational cost, and also, for the first time in literature, the use of a mathematical property for defining the loss function. In this proposed research work, the mutual coherence property has been used in the neural network framework. Such a neural network framework brings generality, robustness, and reduces storage requirements.

</details>


### [138] [Balanced Adaptive Prototype Selection for Scalable TabPFN Inference on Large-Scale Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12989)
*Mahboobe Jadid,Melika Rezaye Garkani,Ali Mousavi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BAPS framework enables TabPFN inference on million-row datasets using just 512 prototypes, achieving 1,953x context compression without model retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pretrained tabular foundation models have strong predictive capability but face constraints with large-scale datasets due to limited inference context windows.

Method: Balanced Adaptive Prototype Selection (BAPS) framework that constructs compact, information-preserving contexts by jointly preserving representative structure, decision boundaries, local density, class balance, and feature-space diversity without model modification.

Result: On million-row HIGGS and SUSY datasets, 512 prototypes retain strong predictive performance and reliable calibration, achieving approximately 1,953-fold context compression. All experiments ran on CPU without GPU acceleration.

Conclusion: Effective context construction serves as a practical mechanism for extending pretrained tabular foundation models to million-scale datasets, making large-scale tabular inference feasible with existing hardware.

Abstract: Pretrained tabular foundation models have demonstrated strong predictive capability; however, their application to large-scale datasets remains constrained by the limited inference context. This paper introduces Balanced Adaptive Prototype Selection (BAPS), a framework for constructing compact, information-preserving contexts for scalable TabPFN inference. Without modifying or retraining the pretrained model, BAPS jointly preserves representative structure, informative decision boundaries, local density, class balance, and feature-space diversity. Experiments on the million-row HIGGS and SUSY datasets show that 512 prototypes retain strong predictive performance and reliable calibration, corresponding to an approximately 1,953-fold context compression. All experiments were conducted on an Intel Core i7 CPU with 16 GB RAM and no GPU acceleration. These findings establish effective context construction as a practical mechanism for extending pretrained tabular foundation models to million-scale datasets.

</details>


### [139] [Incremental Evaluation and Training in Relational Deep Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13023)
*Jakub Peleška,Gustav Šír*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a new incremental training and evaluation paradigm for Relational Deep Learning to address the temporal nature of real-world databases, showing that transfer learning is effective and incrementally fine-tuned models outperform standard from-scratch training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RDL evaluation practices use static, single-episode dataset snapshots that don't reflect the continuous, time-evolving nature of real-world databases, failing to capture how model performance changes as new data accumulates over time.

Method: The paper introduces an incremental, multi-episode evaluation and training paradigm using large-scale datasets to examine data evolution and model training dynamics, implementing multiple incremental training regimes for fine-tuning RDL models with transfer learning, and proposes a new temporal evaluation metric focusing on near-future accuracy.

Result: Temporal concept drifts occur in majority of predictive tasks; transfer learning is feasible and highly effective in RDL setting; incrementally fine-tuned models consistently outperform standard from-scratch trained baselines using the new temporal evaluation metric.

Conclusion: Incremental evaluation and training paradigms are essential for assessing temporal robustness in RDL, and transfer learning with incremental fine-tuning provides a more effective and efficient approach than standard from-scratch training for handling evolving real-world databases.

Abstract: Relational Deep Learning (RDL) models multi-tabular databases as temporal heterogeneous graphs to enable end-to-end representation learning. However, prevailing RDL evaluation practices rely on static, single-episode dataset snapshots, overlooking the continuous, time-evolving nature of real-world databases. Consequently, current RDL benchmarks fail to capture how model performance changes as new data accumulates over time. To address this limitation, we introduce an incremental, multi-episode evaluation and training paradigm to assess and improve the temporal robustness and adaptability of state-of-the-art RDL models. Using established large-scale datasets, we examine data evolution and model training dynamics, demonstrating that temporal concept drifts occur in the majority of predictive tasks. We present multiple incremental training regimes for fine-tuning the models and demonstrate that transfer learning is both feasible and highly effective in the RDL setting. Alongside a new temporal evaluation metric that prioritizes near-future accuracy, we show that our incrementally fine-tuned models consistently outperform the standard, expensive, from-scratch trained baselines.

</details>


### [140] [On the global feature importance for interpretable and trustworthy heat demand forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13039)
*Milan Zdravković*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces ante-hoc Explainable AI to assess global feature importance in heat demand forecasting models for District Heating Systems, comparing Gradient Boosting's intrinsic interpretability with post-hoc methods (PDP, ALE, SHAP) to improve model trustworthiness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enhance interpretability and trustworthiness of ML models for heat demand forecasting in District Heating Systems, addressing challenges related to adherence to communal standards, customer satisfaction, and liability risks.

Method: Uses ante-hoc Explainable AI methodology with four approaches: intrinsic interpretability of Gradient Boosting, and post-hoc methods including Partial Dependence Plots (PDP), Accumulated Local Effects (ALE), and SHAP - avoiding feature permutation/perturbation methods that can introduce bias through unrealistic random data.

Result: Provides discussion of results including assessment of complementarities between methods and specific interpretations within the context of district heating processes, demonstrating how different XAI methods can be applied to heat demand forecasting models.

Conclusion: The ante-hoc Explainable AI methodology combined with carefully selected post-hoc methods (avoiding biased permutation approaches) provides effective tools for assessing global feature importance, enhancing interpretability and trustworthiness of ML models for District Heating Systems control.

Abstract: The paper introduces the ante-hoc Explainable AI methodology to assess the global feature importance of the Machine Learning models used for heat demand forecasting in intelligent control of District Heating Systems, with motivation to facilitate their interpretability and trustworthiness, hence addressing the challenges related to adherence to communal standards, customer satisfaction and liability risks. Methodology includes use of four different approaches, namely intrinsic interpretability of Gradient Boosting method and selected post-hoc methods, namely Partial Dependence, Accumulated Local Effects and SHAP. None of the selected methods assume feature permutation or perturbations which can introduce bias due to introduction of random unrealistic values of data instances. Discussion of results is provided, including the assessment of complementarities where applicable, with specific interpretations in context of the district heating processes.

</details>


### [141] [Latent On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13040)
*Guibin Zhang,Jiayang Lyu,Ran Sun,Xinlei Yu,Haoyu Zhao,Qibing Ren,Shuicheng Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LOPD introduces learnable latent tokens for privileged context in on-policy self-distillation, enabling end-to-end learning without handcrafted artifacts and achieving better performance with higher efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing on-policy self-distillation methods rely heavily on designer-specified privileged artifacts (answers, feedback, skills, trajectories), limiting end-to-end learnability and scalability for continual self-improvement in self-evolving AI.

Method: LOPD retrieves relevant experiences and composes them into continuous latent tokens that condition a self-teacher. The student generates trajectories from task/interaction history and receives dense token-level supervision at every visited prefix, with a privileged-margin objective to stabilize latent context learning.

Result: LOPD outperforms RLVR and representative OPSD methods (OPSD, SDPO, Skill-SD) across agentic tool use and code generation tasks, and achieves higher learning efficiency than GRPO and Skill-SD with less than 30% of their rollout budget.

Conclusion: LOPD represents a step toward a more scalable and self-directed paradigm for agent evolution by making privileged context learnable end-to-end from experience, enabling more autonomous self-improvement.

Abstract: Enabling agents to learn from experience and internalize it into their policy has become a central problem in self-evolving AI. On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) offers an effective pathway by using a privileged self-teacher to provide dense supervision on the student's own trajectories; however, existing methods still rely heavily on designer-specified privileged artifacts (e.g., answers, feedback, skills, or trajectories), limiting the end-to-end learnability and scalability required for continual self-improvement. In this work, we introduce Latent On-Policy Self-Distillation (LOPD), which, rather than proposing another hand-crafted OPSD variant with a newly prescribed form of privileged context, makes the teacher's privileged context itself learnable end-to-end from experience. Technically, LOPD retrieves relevant experiences and composes them into continuous latent tokens that condition a self-teacher, while the student generates trajectories from the task and interaction history and receives dense token-level supervision at every visited prefix. We further introduce a privileged-margin objective to stabilize and regulate the learning of latent context. Empirically, LOPD demonstrates (I) strong performance, outperforming RLVR and representative OPSD methods including OPSD, SDPO, and Skill-SD across both agentic tool use and code generation; and (II) high learning efficiency, surpassing GRPO and Skill-SD with less than 30% of their rollout budget. Ablation studies further provide direct evidence that making privileged context learnable is necessary for realizing these gains. Together, these results position LOPD as a step toward a more scalable and self-directed paradigm for agent evolution.

</details>


### [142] [A Multispectral Framework for the Detection of Calcium Carbide-Induced Ripening and Shelf-Life Estimation in Climacteric Fruits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13073)
*Gurbhit Chaurakoti,Harshit Kumar,Hani Kumar,Anurag Singh,Ram Asrey*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A non-invasive multispectral framework using visible-NIR spectroscopy with XGBoost algorithms to detect calcium carbide-ripened fruits (mango and banana), estimate ripening progression, and predict remaining shelf life.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Health risks from illegal use of industrial-grade calcium carbide for fruit ripening, which leaves toxic arsenic and phosphorus residues, necessitate non-invasive detection methods to distinguish safely ripened fruits from hazardous ones.

Method: Uses AS7265x spectral triad sensor to measure 18 discrete wavelengths (410-940 nm) in visible-NIR range. Features engineered from spectral variance, intensity ratios, and environmental parameters. PCA for dimensionality reduction, then XGBoost algorithms for classification and regression tasks.

Result: 95% classification accuracy with 0.67 carbide recall for mango; 81% accuracy with 0.74 carbide recall for banana. PCA retains >90% spectral variance in 5-7 components. CaC2-treated samples show sharper spectral intensity drops in visible region.

Conclusion: The instrumentation and data-driven approach demonstrates an effective non-invasive framework for detecting calcium carbide-ripened fruits while estimating ripening progression and shelf life, with promising results particularly for mango detection.

Abstract: Significant health risks are associated with the illegal, yet commonly practiced use of industrial-grade Calcium Carbide (CaC2) for ripening climacteric fruits like mango and banana, which leaves behind trace residues of arsenic and phosphorus. To address this, the proposed study explores a novel, non-invasive multispectral framework for distinguishing safely ripened fruits (naturally ripened and ethephon-induced) from calcium carbide-ripened samples, while also estimating their ripening progression (in percentage) and remaining shelf life (in days). The spectral profiles of mango (Mangifera indica) and banana (Musa acuminata) at 18 discrete wavelengths in the visible-near infrared (NIR) range (410 nm - 940 nm) are studied using the AS7265x spectral triad sensor. CaC2-treated samples exhibit sharper spectral intensity drops in the visible region, consistent with accelerated chlorophyll degradation and carotenoid development. To characterize these physiological changes, the feature engineering strategy integrates inter-method spectral variance, intensity ratios at distinct wavelengths, and environmental parameters including temperature and humidity. Dimensionality reduction using Principal Component Analysis (PCA) retains >90% of spectral variance within the first 5-7 components. The resulting feature set is used to train three independent eXtreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost)-based learning algorithms for ripening method classification, along with quantitative estimation of remaining shelf life and ripening progression. A classification accuracy of 95% along with carbide class recall of 0.67 is observed for mango samples, while the model achieves an accuracy of 81% and carbide class recall of 0.74 for banana. This instrumentation and data-driven approach demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed non-invasive framework.

</details>


### [143] [Learning Discrete Decisions for MIPs with Constraint-Aware Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13079)
*Vincenzo Di Vito,Mehdi Taghizadeh,Deepjyoti Deka,Kaarthik Sundar,Ferdinando Fioretto*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Constrained Graph Diffusion (CGD) is a learning-based method that uses diffusion models to solve mixed-integer optimization problems by learning discrete components and using feasibility projection operators, achieving significant speedups over traditional solvers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mixed-integer optimization problems are computationally challenging due to the need to determine both discrete and continuous decisions while satisfying complex combinatorial constraints. Traditional solvers are often slow, creating a need for faster, learning-based approaches that can handle these complex problems efficiently.

Method: The proposed Constrained Graph Diffusion (CGD) method uses a graph-based generative diffusion model to learn the discrete component of mixed-integer problems. It integrates a training-free feasibility projection operator directly into the reverse diffusion process to steer intermediate samples toward feasible solutions. After generating discrete decisions, the remaining continuous optimization is solved using standard numerical methods.

Result: CGD demonstrates substantial improvements in feasibility and solution quality compared to learning-based baselines. It achieves speedups of up to 425× over state-of-the-art numerical solvers for MINLPs, as shown in evaluations on optimal transmission switching for ACOPF and discrete portfolio optimization problems.

Conclusion: CGD provides an effective, problem-agnostic framework for solving mixed-integer optimization problems by combining generative diffusion models with feasibility projection operators, offering both computational efficiency and solution quality improvements over existing approaches.

Abstract: This paper proposes a novel learning-based approach to approximately solve instances of mixed-integer optimization problems. These problems are computationally challenging, as they require jointly determining discrete and continuous decisions while satisfying complex combinatorial constraints. The proposed method relies on a graph-based generative diffusion model that learns the discrete component of mixed-integer optimization problems while integrating a training-free feasibility projection operator directly into the reverse diffusion process to steer intermediate samples toward the feasible set throughout generation. Once the discrete decisions are generated, the remaining optimization reduces to a continuous problem that can be solved efficiently (relative to the original problem) using existing numerical methods. The resulting framework named Constrained Graph Diffusion (CGD), is problem-agnostic and can accommodate a broad class of mixed-integer optimization problems through suitable projection operators. We evaluate CGD on optimal transmission switching for ACOPF and discrete portfolio optimization, demonstrating substantial improvements in feasibility and solution quality over learning-based baselines while achieving speedups of up to $425\times$ over state-of-the-art numerical solvers for MINLPs.

</details>


### [144] [Sampling Luck Masquerades as Allocation Gain: Auditing Test-Time Budget Allocation for Neural Combinatorial Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13087)
*Jinhyung Bae*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NCO solvers conventionally use uniform sample allocation, but this paper shows that non-uniform allocation gains measured in-sample are illusory, while real gains exist under distribution shift when properly measured with correction procedures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates whether non-uniform allocation of sampling budgets in neural combinatorial optimization (NCO) could improve performance beyond the conventional uniform allocation approach used by all solvers. Previous research hasn't measured this potential gain or audited the measurement methods properly.

Method: The study uses three pretrained NCO solvers (POMO, AM, SymNCO) on TSP-100 problems. They compare in-sample vs out-of-sample measurements, calibrate bias against instance-wise null distributions, and conduct pre-registered confirmatory experiments under distribution shift conditions (mixing uniform and clustered instances). They also test exploratory policies with probe samples.

Result: 1) For in-distribution workloads, apparent 2.2-2.6% gains from non-uniform allocation are measurement artifacts - out-of-sample measurements show no significant gain (0.457, 0.015, -0.512%). 2) Under distribution shift, properly measured non-uniform allocation improves best-of-k by 11.5% (AM) and 12.0% (SymNCO) with confidence intervals excluding zero, exceeding baseline by 4.2 points. 3) A 20-sample probe retains 3.4-4.6% gains. 4) The measurement bias doesn't shrink with more samples or instances.

Conclusion: Conventional uniform allocation gains reported in NCO literature are largely measurement artifacts. Real gains from adaptive allocation exist only under distribution shift when properly measured using correction procedures. The paper provides correction methods and reporting guidelines to prevent future measurement artifacts.

Abstract: Neural combinatorial optimization (NCO) solvers report the best of many sampled solutions per instance, and the sample count is, by convention, identical for every instance. Whether a non-uniform allocation of a fixed total budget would buy anything has not been measured. We measure it, and we audit the measurement itself.
  First, on in-distribution workloads the allocation headroom is not detectable. Across three pretrained solvers (POMO, AM, SymNCO) on uniform TSP-100, an oracle allocation computed and evaluated on the same stored samples reports a 2.2-2.6% gain with intervals excluding zero; measured out of sample the same gain is indistinguishable from zero (0.457, 0.015, -0.512 percent). Following the customary in-sample procedure, all three solvers would have supported a published 2%-level gain that does not exist. We calibrate this bias against an instance-wise null in which the true gain is zero by construction; over the ranges we test it does not shrink with more samples or more instances.
  Second, the same correction that removes the phantom gains preserves a real one. Under distribution shift (a workload mixing uniform and clustered instances), a pre-registered confirmatory experiment finds that allocation guided by held-out sample statistics improves best-of-k by 11.5% (AM, primary endpoint; 95% CI [7.4, 19.7]) and 12.0% (SymNCO, replication) at equal evaluation budget, with the signal-acquisition cost not charged; a pre-registered negative control (POMO, an order of magnitude more robust to shift) shows -0.3% [-0.7, 0.24]. The gain exceeds a frozen distribution-label baseline by 4.2 points [1.9, 7.7]. An exploratory policy charging a 20-sample probe against the same budget retains 3.4% (AM) and 4.6% (SymNCO).
  We give a correction procedure and a reporting checklist, and release all data, code, and the pre-registration record.

</details>


### [145] [FlowLOB: Efficient and Controllable Limit Order Book Generation with Flow Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13096)
*Zhuohan Wang,Andreea Bacalum,Ollie Olby,Carmine Ventre,Namid Stillman*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FlowLOB is a flow-matching model for generating realistic limit order book trajectories that outperforms diffusion models and other baselines in sampling efficiency and realism while enabling controllable scenario generation and zero-shot transfer to unseen instruments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LOB simulators have limitations: agent-based models and deep generative models only partially provide the desired properties of realistic market dynamics, computational efficiency, controllable scenario generation, and generalization to unseen instruments.

Method: FlowLOB uses conditional flow-matching to generate LOB trajectories, trained on multiple HKEX symbols at three sampling frequencies (0.1s, 1s, 10s) in tick-relative representation. Both flow and diffusion models were trained with identical data, architecture, and budget, and sampled through same ODE solvers for controlled comparison.

Result: Flow matching achieves best quality with only 10 ODE-solver steps, while diffusion needs many more function evaluations for similar fidelity. FlowLOB outperforms baselines (two learned and two agent-based models) in most distributional metrics at finer sampling frequencies, shows effective counterfactual controllability, and transfers zero-shot to held-out symbols.

Conclusion: FlowLOB provides a superior LOB simulation framework that combines realistic market dynamics, computational efficiency, controllable scenario generation, and generalization capability, making it particularly useful for practitioners who need efficient and realistic market simulations.

Abstract: Limit order book (LOB) simulators are most useful to practitioners when they combine realistic market dynamics, computationally efficient sampling, controllable scenario generation, and the ability to generalize beyond the instruments seen during training---properties that existing agent-based and deep generative simulators provide only partially. We present \textbf{FlowLOB}, a conditional \textbf{flow}-matching generator of \textbf{LOB} trajectories, trained on multiple Hong Kong Exchange (HKEX) symbols at three sampling frequencies ($0.1$s, $1$s, $10$s) in tick-relative representation that transfers to unseen instruments. Because flow and diffusion models admit a common formulation, we train both with identical data, architecture, and budget, and sample both through the same fixed-step ODE solvers, yielding a controlled comparison of sampling efficiency and fidelity. Flow matching attains its best quality with only $10$ ODE-solver steps, whereas diffusion needs many more function evaluations to approach the same fidelity. At this efficient operating point, FlowLOB improves realism over baselines, two learned and two agent-based models, in most distributional metrics at the two finer sampling frequencies. We evaluate counterfactual controllability with a distributional test that asks whether changing a scenario condition moves the generated statistic toward the corresponding real tail regime; FlowLOB satisfies this criterion in most tested settings. Both realism and control effects transfer zero-shot on a held-out symbol. We additionally conduct ablation studies on the network architecture and the learning rate.

</details>


### [146] [Branch and Bound for Relational Verification of Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13118)
*Kota Fukuda,Zhenya Zhang,Guanqin Zhang,Jianjun Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A branch-and-bound framework called SaBRe for verifying neural networks against relational specifications, featuring relational neuron splitting with optimal selection strategy based on dual formulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Relational specifications like global robustness are crucial for safety-critical CPS applications, but verification requires reasoning about multiple network inferences, posing technical challenges. Existing abstraction techniques are incomplete and may produce false alarms, needing effective refinement.

Method: Proposed branch-and-bound framework that splits relational neurons (not individual neurons) using a relational neuron selection strategy based on dual formulation of verification problem to efficiently select optimal relational neuron for maximizing refinement.

Result: Evaluated on 817 verification problems across ACAS Xu, MNIST-F, MNIST-C, CIFAR and GTSRB. Outperforms baseline approaches in number of solved instances and verification efficiency.

Conclusion: The proposed SaBRe framework with relational neuron splitting and dual-based selection strategy effectively addresses relational specification verification challenges, demonstrating improved performance over existing methods.

Abstract: Verification of neural networks against relational specifications, such as global robustness, is crucial for safety-critical applications of cyber-physical systems (CPS), given their increasing adoption of AI components. Compared to simple trace properties (e.g., local robustness), verifying relational specifications requires reasoning about the relationship between multiple network inferences, which brings significant technical challenges. Existing research has explored abstraction techniques based on sound and convex over-approximation of neural network outputs; however, since these approaches are inherently incomplete and may raise false alarms, they further underscore the need of effective abstraction refinement.
  In this paper, we propose a branch-and-bound (BaB) framework to mitigate the issue, which iteratively splits the problem until all sub-problems are verified. Specifically, our BaB framework features splitting of relational neurons rather than individual neurons as prior works do, and as the core of our technique, we devise a relational neuron selection strategy based on the dual formulation of the verification problem, which allows us to efficiently select the (most likely) optimal relational neuron that maximizes the refinement brought by problem splitting. We evaluate SaBRe on 817 verification problems across ACAS Xu, MNIST-F, MNIST-C, CIFAR and GTSRB. The results show that SaBRe outperforms different baseline approaches, in terms of the number of solved instances and verification efficiency, which demonstrates the effectiveness of our proposed techniques.

</details>


### [147] [ProME: Prototype-Margin Environments with Repair-Aware Selection for Group-Robust Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13190)
*Qianqian Wang,Yunshan Li,Dawei Huang,Wenwu Gong,Lili Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ProME is a novel method for group-robust learning without training-group labels that aligns environment inference and representation selection with the deployed predictor using prototype margins and classifier repair.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for group-robust learning without training-group labels have misalignment issues: they infer environments from separate reference models and select representations before fitting the final classifier, which doesn't align with the deployed predictor's needs.

Method: ProME (Prototype-Margin Environments) formulates group robustness as an endogenous environments with repair-aware selection (ERAS) problem. It constructs balanced environments by splitting prototype margins at their median along the training trajectory, then fits a group-balanced linear head on validation data to rank predictors by worst-group accuracy.

Result: ProME achieves the highest average worst-group accuracy among compared methods with the same group-label access. The approach demonstrates that prototype margins enrich shortcut-conflicting examples and classifier repair reshapes candidate evaluation effectively.

Conclusion: ProME provides an effective solution for group-robust learning without training-group labels by aligning environment inference and representation selection decisions with the deployed predictor, supported by theoretical guarantees on worst risk bounds.

Abstract: Group-robust learning is crucial for maintaining accuracy on rare subpopulations when training-group labels are unavailable. However, existing methods often infer environments from a separate reference model and select representations before fitting the classifier used at deployment, leaving both decisions misaligned with the deployed predictor. In this work, we formulate group robustness without training-group labels as the endogenous environments with repair-aware selection (ERAS) problem, and propose ProME (Prototype-Margin Environments) to align both decisions with the deployed predictor. ProME splits prototype margins at their median to construct approximately balanced environments along the training trajectory, and fits a group-balanced linear head on group-annotated validation data to rank the resulting predictors by validation worst-group accuracy. We theoretically bound the worst risk across the inferred environments for a fixed predictor and partition, showing that this bound transfers to the oracle groups under an explicit alignment condition. Extensive experiments show that prototype margins enrich shortcut-conflicting examples, classifier repair reshapes candidate evaluation, and ProME achieves the highest average worst-group accuracy among the compared methods with the same group-label access.

</details>


### [148] [Beyond Simulated Benchmarks: Evaluating Motion Representations for Fall Detection Under Real-World Data Scarcity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13197)
*Timilehin B. Aderinola,Ilaria D'Ascanio,Luca Palmerini,Lorenzo Chiari,Jochen Klenk,Clemens Becker,Brian Caulfield,Georgiana Ifrim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Paper systematically evaluates motion representations for wearable fall detection under real-world data scarcity, showing that simpler, interpretable symbolic approaches with physical augmentation degrade less under domain shift than complex models that excel on simulated data but fail in real-world conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world falls are extremely rare, making labeled data scarce for training fall detection models. Most approaches rely on simulated datasets with high lab performance but poor real-world generalization. Need to evaluate motion representations under realistic data scarcity conditions.

Method: Systematic evaluation of motion representations using accelerometer signals: interval-based, kernel-based, symbolic, and foundation model representations. Also investigate lightweight symbolic representation that converts motion segments into symbolic sentences augmented with physically-grounded impact descriptors. Use FallAllD (simulated falls) and FARSEEING (real-world clinically verified falls) datasets. Conduct cross-validation, controlled data scarcity tests, and cross-dataset transfer evaluation.

Result: Highly parameterized kernel and foundation models perform well on simulated data but degrade severely under both data scarcity and domain shift. Interval-based representation achieves strongest absolute real-world performance. Symbolic representation with physically-grounded impact descriptors yields smallest degradation under domain shift and retains detection sensitivity under extreme scarcity (though with lower precision).

Conclusion: Representation choice is critical for deployable fall detection given real-world data scarcity. Simpler, interpretable representations with physical augmentation degrade less under domain shift than complex models. Evaluation must go beyond simulated benchmarks to real-world scarcity conditions for practical deployment.
