Abstract: Falls are a major health concern for older adults, and wearable sensors have been widely explored for detecting falls and enabling timely intervention. However, real-world falls are extremely rare: collecting 100 of them requires an estimated 100,000 days of monitoring, resulting in severely limited labelled data for training machine learning models. Consequently, many approaches rely on simulated datasets, often reporting high laboratory performance but limited real-world generalisation. We present a systematic evaluation of motion representations for wearable fall detection under real-world data scarcity. Using accelerometer signals, we compare interval-based, kernel-based, symbolic, and foundation model representations. As an interpretable baseline, we additionally investigate a lightweight symbolic representation that converts short motion segments into symbolic sentences augmented with physically-grounded impact descriptors. Experiments use FallAllD, a simulated falls dataset, and FARSEEING, a clinically verified real-world falls dataset. Through cross-validation, controlled data scarcity, and cross-dataset transfer, we examine how representation choices affect robustness under realistic deployment. Our results reveal that highly parameterised kernel and foundation models excel on simulated data but degrade severely under both data scarcity and domain shift. Although the interval-based representation achieves the strongest absolute real-world performance, augmenting a symbolic representation with physically-grounded impact descriptors yields the smallest degradation under domain shift and retains detection sensitivity under extreme scarcity, albeit at lower precision. These findings highlight the importance of evaluating beyond simulated benchmarks and show that representation choice is critical for deployable fall detection given the scarcity of real-world data.

</details>


### [149] [TANGCO: Learning Topology-Aware Capacity Allocation for Overload-driven Cascading Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13212)
*Orkun Irsoy,Leman Akoglu,Osman Yagan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TANGCO: Topology-Aware Neural Graph-Guided Capacity Optimization uses graph neural networks to optimize capacity allocation in networks to prevent cascading failures, outperforming traditional heuristics across synthetic and real-world networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Networked systems have nodes with limited capacity; when overloaded, they fail and redistribute load to neighbors, potentially causing system-wide cascades. Optimizing capacity allocation to resist these cascades is difficult due to non-differentiable, piecewise-constant objectives and lack of optimal solutions.

Method: TANGCO uses a graph neural network policy trained through cascade simulator with policy-gradient learning and a heuristic anchor. The system evaluates on five synthetic graph families and five real networks (power, road, air, Internet) with 450 synthetic instances.

Result: TANGCO outperforms four hand-designed heuristics in all 450 synthetic instances and 40 of 45 real-network conditions, with robustness gains from 1.6% to 246%. Learned policies transfer to unseen graphs and TANGCO-pre (pre-trained on synthetic graphs) matches per-network training on real networks. Training scales near-linearly with graph size.

Conclusion: Graph neural networks effectively optimize capacity allocation against cascading failures, substantially outperforming hand-designed heuristics. The graph representation is crucial for gains, as non-GNN variants stay close to heuristics. Learned allocations provide insights for improved heuristics while revealing regimes where topology-aware learned policies remain essential.

Abstract: Networked systems, from power grids to traffic networks and cloud clusters, carry loads across nodes with limited capacity. A node whose load exceeds its capacity fails and sheds its load onto its neighbors, which can trigger a system-wide cascade. We study how to allocate a fixed capacity budget across nodes to resist these cascades under local load redistribution. The problem is difficult because no optimal allocation is known, and the fail-or-survive objective is non-differentiable and piecewise constant, so exact and gradient-based optimization methods do not directly apply. We introduce TANGCO (Topology-Aware Neural Graph-Guided Capacity Optimization), which uses a graph neural network policy trained through the cascade simulator with policy-gradient learning and a heuristic anchor. We evaluate TANGCO on five synthetic graph families and five real networks spanning power, road, air, and Internet topologies. The learned policy improves on the best of four hand-designed heuristics in all 450 synthetic instances and in 40 of 45 real-network conditions, with robustness gains ranging from 1.6% to 246%. The learned policies transfer to unseen graphs within a family and partially across related topologies, and TANGCO$^{pre}$, pre-trained on synthetic graphs, matches per-network training on unseen real networks. Training scales near-linearly with graph size, and TANGCO$^{pre}$ allocates on a new network with no per-target training, matching the deployment cost of a hand-designed heuristic. Free-vector variants without the GNN, stay close to the heuristics, so the graph representation carries the gain beyond numerical search. Finally, analysis of the learned allocations identifies when local risk is sufficient, leads to an improved closed-form heuristic, and reveals the regimes where a topology-aware learned policy remains necessary.

</details>


### [150] [History-informed Lagrangian Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13215)
*Tianshuo Zhang,Xianglei Xing,Wenzhe Zhai,Jia Gao,He Cao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HiLNN is a physics-guided neural network that forecasts long-horizon mechanical system evolution from position-only observations by using a recurrent encoder to extract latent context from history, enabling velocity reconstruction and adaptive modulation of system parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the challenge of forecasting long-horizon evolution of mechanical systems from position-only observations, where hidden velocities and trajectory-specific physical properties must be inferred simultaneously. Existing physics-guided neural networks like Lagrangian Neural Networks require complete state inputs and lack adaptability to changing system parameters.

Method: HiLNN employs a recurrent encoder to extract a latent context from historical position sequences. This context reconstructs unobserved initial velocity and adaptively modulates the mass matrix, potential energy, and damping coefficients of a structured Lagrangian system. The method uses a differentiable RK4 rollout scheme optimized end-to-end under multi-step trajectory supervision and energy-consistency regularization.

Result: Empirical evaluations across conservative, dissipative, and heterogeneous variable-parameter systems show that HiLNN delivers superior long-term prediction accuracy and maintains precise energy profiles compared to state-of-the-art baselines.

Conclusion: HiLNN effectively overcomes the limitations of existing physics-guided neural networks by enabling position-only forecasting with adaptive parameter modulation, making it suitable for real-world mechanical systems where velocities are often unobservable and parameters may vary.

Abstract: Forecasting the long-horizon evolution of mechanical systems from position-only observations is a pivotal yet difficult task, as hidden velocities and trajectory-specific physical properties must be inferred simultaneously. Although physics-guided neural networks like Lagrangian Neural Networks (LNNs) guarantee physical plausibility, they generally require complete state inputs and lack adaptability to changing system parameters. To break these limitations, we introduce History-informed Lagrangian Neural Networks (HiLNN). Grounded in the insight that temporal position sequences implicitly encode underlying dynamics, HiLNN employs a recurrent encoder to extract a latent context from history. This context not only reconstructs the unobserved initial velocity but also adaptively modulates the mass matrix, potential energy, and damping coefficients of a structured Lagrangian system. By leveraging a differentiable RK4 rollout scheme, the entire pipeline is optimized end-to-end under multi-step trajectory supervision and energy-consistency regularization. Empirical evaluations across conservative, dissipative, and heterogeneous variable-parameter systems show that HiLNN delivers superior long-term prediction accuracy and maintains precise energy profiles compared to state-of-the-art baselines. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/yingtian22/History-informed-LNN.

</details>


### [151] [Knowledge-guided Pattern Discovery via Coupled Tensor Factorizations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13234)
*Gaute Johannessen,Geert Roelof van der Ploeg,Evrim Acar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Knowledge-guided tensor factorization jointly analyzes real and simulated data to improve pattern discovery in complex multiway datasets like metabolomics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional tensor factorizations are purely data-driven and don't incorporate prior knowledge from computational models. There's a need to bridge data-driven analysis with rich computational models to enhance pattern discovery in complex systems like the metabolome.

Method: Introduces a knowledge-guided approach using coupled tensor factorizations with linear coupling to jointly analyze real data and simulated data generated from computational models.

Result: Experiments on real metabolomics data show that guiding analysis with simulated data improves pattern discovery performance and can reveal discrepancies between data and computational models.

Conclusion: The proposed knowledge-guided tensor factorization framework successfully integrates data and computational models, enhancing pattern discovery while providing insights into model-data alignment in complex systems analysis.

Abstract: In order to understand complex systems such as the human metabolome or human brain, different sensing technologies are used, generating complex data. These datasets are often multiway, i.e., with more than two axes of variation such as a subjects by metabolites by time array. While tensor factorizations have successfully revealed interpretable patterns from such complex data, they have so far been mainly data-driven. On the other hand, there is more to data -- there are computational models (of these systems), which are rich sources of prior information. In this paper, we introduce a knowledge-guided approach that brings together data and computational models by jointly analyzing real data and simulated data (generated using a computational model) using coupled tensor factorizations with linear coupling. Our experiments on real metabolomics measurements demonstrate that guiding the analysis of such noisy data with simulated data improves the pattern discovery performance while also revealing potential discrepancies between data and computational models.

</details>


### [152] [Novel Knowledge-Guided Generative Methods for Synthetic Transcriptomic Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13256)
*Francesca Pia Panaccione,Sofia Mongardi,Marco Masseroli,Pietro Pinoli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Comparative analysis of GAN variants for transcriptomic synthetic data generation, with MK-TGAN (multi-kernel GNN-based model) outperforming others by incorporating biological knowledge graphs to produce realistic and useful synthetic transcriptomic data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biomedical research faces challenges with data quality, imbalances, biases, and ethical/legal constraints limiting access to high-quality transcriptomic data. Synthetic data generation can overcome these limitations, but needs to capture real-world biological patterns to remain useful for downstream analysis.

Method: Comparative analysis of three GAN variants for transcriptomic data generation, focusing on strategies to incorporate prior biological knowledge via gene graphs. Introduces MK-TGAN - a novel multi-kernel, Graph Neural Network-based model that leverages prior knowledge graphs using GNNs to ensure synthetic data maintains real-world gene patterns.

Result: MK-TGAN outperforms alternative methods in terms of both realism and utility of generated synthetic transcriptomic data. Results show that prior knowledge integration strategies improve performance, and MK-TGAN consistently produces synthetic samples with superior realism and biological plausibility.

Conclusion: Integration of prior biological knowledge through knowledge graphs significantly enhances synthetic transcriptomic data generation. MK-TGAN's GNN-based approach effectively leverages gene graphs to produce high-quality synthetic data that maintains biological relevance and utility for downstream biomedical research tasks.

Abstract: As biomedical research increasingly relies on data-intensive tools, the quality and utility of datasets are critical. Challenges such as imbalances, biases, and ethical or legal constraints often limit access to high-quality data. Synthetic data generation can help overcome these limitations. Here, we present a comparative analysis of generative models for transcriptomic data, investigating strategies to incorporate prior biological knowledge via gene graphs. This ensures that synthetic data capture real-world gene patterns, maintaining their usefulness for downstream tasks. In particular, we introduce and benchmark three variants of the Generative Adversarial Network. Among the alternatives, MK-TGAN - an innovative multi-kernel, Graph Neural Network-based model - stands out for its performance in terms of both the realism and utility of the generated data. Unlike other methods, MK-TGAN leverages prior knowledge graphs by exploiting graph neural networks. Our results show that prior knowledge integration strategies improve performance, and that MK-TGAN consistently produces synthetic samples with superior realism and biological plausibility.

</details>


### [153] [Virtual Temperature Sensors in Power Transformers Using Neural Ordinary Differential Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13260)
*Berk Hadzhamolla,Alexander Johannes Stasik,Signe Riemer-Sørensen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A physics-aware Neural ODE framework is developed for forecasting power transformer thermal behavior, combining simplified heat-transfer physics with neural networks to provide accurate, interpretable temperature predictions across diverse transformer units.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for transformer thermal modeling have significant limitations: numerical approaches (FEM/CFD) are computationally expensive and impractical for real-time use; lumped-parameter models depend on specific thermal constants and may miss dynamic responses; purely data-driven ML methods require large datasets and can produce physically inconsistent results.

Method: Develops a physics-aware Neural Ordinary Differential Equation (Neural ODE) framework that integrates simplified heat-transfer equations directly into the Neural ODE formulation. This models system dynamics in continuous time, providing smooth trajectory prediction and representing continuously evolving thermal dynamics naturally.

Result: Evaluated across datasets from fifteen transformers in different regions of Norway with varying designs and cooling mechanisms. The Neural ODE framework demonstrates standardized, physics-aware, and robust forecasting capabilities for heterogeneous transformer units.

Conclusion: The physics-aware Neural ODE framework provides a practical, accurate, and interpretable solution for transformer thermal forecasting that addresses limitations of existing methods, offering standardized applicability across diverse transformer configurations.

Abstract: Accurate modeling and forecasting of power transformer thermal behavior are critical for reliability, asset lifetime, and optimized power system operation. Numerical approaches such as finite element methods (FEM) and computational fluid dynamics (CFD) offer high fidelity but are computationally expensive, require complex mesh generation, and are often impractical for real-time or large-scale applications, particularly when transformer geometries are unknown. Lumped-parameter thermal models are more practical but depend on transformer-specific thermal constants and may fail to capture dynamic responses under varying operating and environmental conditions. Purely data-driven machine learning methods, including artificial neural networks, convolutional neural networks, and long short-term memory (LSTM) networks, have shown success in forecasting transformer temperatures but typically require large volumes of high-quality training data and may produce physically inconsistent or uninterpretable results. This paper develops a physics-aware Neural Ordinary Differential Equation (Neural ODE) framework for forecasting transformer thermal behavior from real-world time-series data. Neural ODEs model system dynamics in continuous time, providing smooth trajectory prediction and a natural representation of continuously evolving thermal dynamics. A key contribution is the integration of simplified heat-transfer equations directly into the Neural ODE formulation. The model is evaluated across datasets from fifteen transformers in different regions of Norway with varying designs and cooling mechanisms. The results demonstrate that the developed Neural ODE framework provides a standardized, physics-aware, and robust forecasting approach for heterogeneous transformer units.

</details>


### [154] [Into the ORBIT for Time Series: Training Regimes for Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13262)
*Hongjie Xia,Yiding Liu,Yifan Hu,Peiyuan Liu,Zewei Dong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ORBIT is a novel training paradigm for time series foundation models that provides explicit control over pre-training distributions, combined with Falcon-2.0 architecture and Rank-Guided Cross-Depth Alignment for improved zero-shot forecasting performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current time series foundation models focus on architectural innovation but neglect training regimes for large-scale heterogeneous corpora, leading to poorly controlled pre-training distributions with issues like domain imbalance, varying context requirements, prediction horizons, and missing data.

Method: ORBIT combines Bootstrap Multi-Level Sampling (controls dataset exposure and samples records, targets, context windows, and horizons) with Omni-Range Incremental Training (varies context lengths and prediction horizons during training). The architecture uses Falcon-2.0, a univariate encoder-only Transformer with missingness-aware triple-channel patch tokenization and parallel patch prediction, plus Rank-Guided Cross-Depth Alignment that uses late-layer representations as stop-gradient teachers for shallow layers.

Result: Evaluations on GIFT-Eval and fev-bench demonstrate strong zero-shot forecasting performance across diverse domains and frequencies.

Conclusion: ORBIT provides an effective, controllable training paradigm for time series foundation models that addresses distribution control issues and enables strong zero-shot performance without additional inference cost.

Abstract: Time series foundation models (TSFMs) have advanced primarily through architectural innovation, while training regimes for large-scale heterogeneous corpora remain under-explored. As a result, pre-training distributions are often poorly controlled with respect to domain imbalance, context requirements, prediction horizons, and missingness. We introduce ORBIT (Omni-Range Bootstrap Incremental Training), a training paradigm that makes this distribution explicit and controllable. ORBIT combines Bootstrap Multi-Level Sampling, which controls dataset exposure and samples records, target variables, context windows, and prediction horizons, with Omni-Range Incremental Training, which varies context lengths and prediction horizons throughout a single training stage. Under ORBIT, we train Falcon-2.0, a simple univariate encoder-only Transformer with missingness-aware triple-channel patch tokenization and parallel patch prediction. We further introduce Rank-Guided Cross-Depth Alignment, a training objective that uses late-layer representations as stop-gradient teachers for shallow layers without additional inference cost. Evaluations on GIFT-Eval and fev-bench demonstrate strong zero-shot forecasting performance across diverse domains and frequencies.

</details>


### [155] [EEG Decoding Using CNN and LSTM Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13285)
*Athanasios Karagounis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A hybrid CNN-bi-LSTM architecture improves motor imagery EEG classification by combining spatial-temporal feature learning with temporal dependency modeling, showing robust performance across datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motor imagery BCIs for stroke/neurodegenerative patients face challenges due to noisy EEG signals with weak brain activity relationships. Deep learning approaches for MI-EEG feature learning remain limited despite their potential.

Method: A hybrid deep learning architecture combining CNN for spatial-temporal feature extraction from raw EEG and bi-LSTM for modeling temporal dependencies among features.

Result: The CNN-bi-LSTM architecture shows robust performance for 2- and 3-class motor imagery classification and demonstrates promising subject-independent decoding capability across public and private EEG datasets.

Conclusion: The hybrid CNN-bi-LSTM approach effectively addresses MI-EEG decoding challenges, offering improved classification and potential for practical BCI applications with subject-independent capability.

Abstract: Motor imagery (MI) brain--computer interfaces (BCIs) have emerged as a promising approach for establishing flexible communication pathways between the human brain and external devices , particularly for individuals affected by stroke or neurodegenerative disorders. Reliable decoding of motor-imagery electroencephalography (MI-EEG) remains challenging because EEG recordings contain substantial noise and exhibit complex, weakly informative relationships with the underlying brain activity. Although deep learning provides an effective means of learning representations directly from EEG signals, its application to MI-EEG feature learning remains comparatively limited. This study introduces a hybrid deep-learning architecture that integrates a convolutional neural network (CNN) with a bidirectional long short-term memory (bi-LSTM) network. The CNN is used to learn high-level spatial and temporal representations directly from raw MI-EEG recordings, whereas the bi-LSTM models temporal dependencies and relationships among the extracted features. The proposed approach is evaluated using both a publicly available dataset and a privately acquired dataset obtained with an EEG acquisition system. The experimental results indicate that the CNN\&bi-LSTM architecture provides robust performance for both two- and three-class motor-imagery classification and demonstrates promising subject-independent decoding capability across the evaluated methods.

</details>


### [156] [Large-scale Testing Global Optimization Methods with Black-box Adversarial Attacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13296)
*Wojciech Zarzecki,Jarosław Arabas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Black-box adversarial attack (BBAA) tasks can serve as valuable global optimization benchmarks to address biases in existing benchmark suites and better reflect modern machine learning challenges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing global optimization benchmark suites are limited in size, based on outdated analytical functions dating back to the 1970s, which creates a risk of biasing the development of optimization methods away from modern real-world challenges.

Method: The authors propose using black-box adversarial attack (BBAA) tasks as benchmark problems for global optimization in high-dimensional spaces. They demonstrate the efficiency of several evolutionary algorithms and other metaheuristics on example BBAA problems.

Result: The study shows that BBAA problems can effectively serve as optimization benchmarks, and demonstrates that various evolutionary algorithms and metaheuristics are efficient in solving these types of problems.

Conclusion: BBAA tasks represent valuable global optimization benchmarks that can help align optimization method development with modern machine learning challenges, promoting convergence between optimization research and practical ML needs.

Abstract: Existing global optimization benchmark suites are of a moderate size and are based on a small number of analytical functions that date back even to the 1970s. This causes a risk of biasing the development of global optimization methods. We argue that the tasks related to the black-box adversarial attack (BBAA) can serve as valuable global optimization benchmark in many-dimensional space. We demonstrate the efficiency of several types of evolutionary algorithms and other metaheuristics in solving example BBAA problems. Thus, we take a step towards convergence of global optimization methods to the challenges and needs that arise in the modern machine learning field.

</details>


### [157] [The Time Value of Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13297)
*Matthew Siper,Ahmed Khalifa,Julian Togelius*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LVPG introduces a long-horizon actor-critic framework for evolutionary search that values mutation lineages based on future potential rather than immediate offspring fitness, leading to more efficient policy discovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional evolutionary search uses immediate-return control that penalizes mutations based on immediate offspring fitness, even when those mutations open valuable future lineages. This fails to capture the "time value of evolution" where weak offspring can be valuable ancestors.

Method: Lineage-Value Policy Gradients (LVPG) uses a long-horizon actor-critic framework with decoupled architecture: shared generative backbone, bootstrapped critic head that estimates finite-horizon lineage potential from multi-step mutation trees, and actor head that dynamically modulates mutation intensity over remaining search budget.

Result: Compared to immediate-return optimization across 90 paired runs with matched conditions, LVPG substantially accelerates finite-budget search (increasing validation best-so-far AUC by 0.394 Sharpe units), produces fewer temporary regressions, recovers more often from regressions, and yields more selective non-monotonic search.

Conclusion: Finite-horizon lineage value enables more efficient evolutionary search by valuing mutation lineages based on future potential, resulting in stronger policies within identical resource constraints and better long-term search performance.

Abstract: In evolutionary search, a weak child can be a valuable ancestor that makes high-fitness regions reachable. Immediate-return control is blind to this delayed utility, penalizing mutations through their immediate offspring even when they open productive future lineages. We formalize this hidden dynamic as the time value of evolution within a finite-horizon Markov decision process. To exploit it, we introduce Lineage-Value Policy Gradients (LVPG), a long-horizon actor-critic framework for automated trading policy discovery. Our architecture decouples search control into specialized policy heads over a shared generative backbone: a bootstrapped critic head estimates the value of finite-horizon lineage potential from multi-step mutation trees, while an actor head dynamically modulates mutation intensity over the remaining search budget. We isolate the impact of long-horizon credit assignment against immediate-return optimization across 90 paired runs under matched operators, lineage supervision, folds, seeds, and budgets. Path-based credit assignment substantially accelerates finite-budget search, increasing validation best-so-far AUC by 0.394 Sharpe units. LVPG also produces fewer temporary regressions than immediate-return optimization and recovers from them more often. Finite-horizon lineage value yields more selective non-monotonic search and stronger policies within identical resource constraints.

</details>


### [158] [A Probe Direction Is a Property of Its Prompt](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13329)
*Valentin Noël*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper critiques current methods for detecting if AI models behave differently when being tested, showing that results depend more on prompt choices than model characteristics, and provides guidelines for more reliable comparisons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods for detecting if models sense evaluation from activations are problematic because they use single-prompt designs that don't account for prompt variation, potentially undermining evaluation reliability and model comparison validity.

Method: The authors analyze the standard activation contrast method, showing it has a free parameter (prompt choice) that is not fixed. They systematically vary prompts while holding task text constant, and demonstrate how results change based solely on prompt variation rather than model characteristics.

Result: Results show that reported scores and trends with model size follow prompts rather than models; model accounts for small variance while prompt-response interaction dominates; single-prompt designs cannot support model comparisons; surface form alone can produce substantial fraction of published scores.

Conclusion: Single-prompt activation probe designs are inadequate for comparing models, as results are heavily influenced by prompt choices. Defensible comparisons require multiple prompts, and researchers should treat prompts as a measurement design facet rather than implementation detail.

Abstract: A model that behaves differently when it senses it is being tested would undermine the evaluations we rely on, so recent work has sought to read that sense directly from a model's activations. The standard instrument contrasts activations on prompts that announce an evaluation against prompts that do not, and reports how well the resulting direction separates held-out cases. That number is then compared across models and correlated with scale. We observe that the instrument has a free parameter its readings do not disclose: "a prompt that announces an evaluation" is not a prompt but a choice among many, and nothing in the method fixes which. Holding the task text fixed and varying only that choice, we find that the reported score, and even the direction in which it trends with model size, follows the prompt rather than the model; two published studies that disagree about the sign of that trend are both reproducible from a single design, by choice of prompt alone. Treating the prompt as a facet of a measurement design rather than an implementation detail, we find the model under study accounts for a small share of the variance in the number reported about it, and most of the rest lies in how each model responds to each prompt: collecting more evaluation items cannot repair the measurement, while varying prompts can. A further check finds that the split these probes are scored on is largely separable from surface form alone, so a direction carrying no information about evaluation at all still reproduces a substantial fraction of each published score. We conclude that a single-prompt design cannot support comparison between models, and we give the number of prompts a defensible comparison requires.

</details>


### [159] [Training AI Scientists to Replicate Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13331)
*Damon Falck,Samer Sabri,Anja Surina,Thom Foster,Anya Sims,Sam Devlin,Dylan Rogers,Tantum Collins,Kaloyan Aleksiev,Louis Kirsch,Edward Hughes*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AI agent Faraday (27B parameters) outperforms top LLMs on paper replication tasks using automated rubric-based evaluation and tools for coding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Paper replication is fundamental to scientific reliability but requires hypothesis-driven exploration similar to original research. Current approaches lack scalable evaluation frameworks for AI-assisted replication.

Method: Developed Replica, a scalable task space for paper replication with automated rubric-based judge for low-noise evaluation. Post-trained Faraday, a 27B-parameter AI Scientist agent that uses coding agents as tools.

Result: Faraday surpasses performance of Claude Opus 4.8 and GPT-5.5 on held-out replication tasks. Qualitative analysis shows Faraday adopts more scientifically-principled approaches compared to other models.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates AI agents capable of paper replication with minimal complex harnesses, providing a foundation for AI agents to contribute to long-horizon scientific innovation.

Abstract: The replicability of papers is a cornerstone of scientific knowledge, ensuring the reliability of existing results and providing a base for further experiments. The act of replication typically illuminates details that were previously underspecified, and thus requires similar hypothesis-driven exploration to open-ended research. In this work, we develop Replica, a scalable task space for paper replication. To provide reward signal, we introduce an auto-generated rubric-based judge that has low noise and agrees with human assessment of replication quality. We post-train Faraday, a 27B-parameter "AI Scientist" agent that leverages coding agents as tools, surpassing the performance of Claude Opus 4.8 and GPT-5.5 on held-out replication tasks. Qualitative analysis of individual rollouts reveals that Faraday adopts a more scientifically-principled approach. We believe that our results provide a stepping stone towards AI agents capable of long-horizon scientific innovation without requiring complex harnesses.

</details>


### [160] [Neural Quadratic Forms: A Unified Minimal Model for Sudden Learning and Scaling Laws](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13335)
*Liu Ziyin,Yizhou Xu,Tomaso Poggio,Isaac Chuang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Neural networks exhibit stepwise learning due to symmetry principles that enforce universal dynamics near initialization, reducing diverse architectures to a common model with predictable training plateaus and power-law behaviors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand why neural networks trained by gradient descent exhibit stepwise learning behaviors (long plateaus followed by abrupt drops) and smooth power-law training losses across diverse architectures, which suggests the existence of universal collective variables governing learning dynamics.

Method: The authors use symmetry principles from the invariance of neural network layers under unit relabeling to derive a universal quadratic form Tr[WW^T A(x)] for the expansion near zero weights. They compute the structure matrix A(x) for various architectures (perceptrons, attention, mixtures of experts, convolutions), reducing them to a single model parameterized by A. Dynamics close on the order parameter M=WW^T, and under eigenbasis alignment reduce to Lotka-Volterra equations.

Result: The theory predicts that training plateaus emerge as a singular limit of smooth flow when initial weights are small, with switch-on times of modes becoming well-separated. When many modes are unresolved, events merge into power-law training dynamics with predictable exponents. Numerical experiments confirm these predictions across training methods and architectures.

Conclusion: Stepwise learning in neural networks is a universal phenomenon governed by symmetry principles that enforce common dynamics near initialization, explaining both training plateaus and power-law behaviors across diverse architectures through the framework of structure matrices and reduced collective variables.

Abstract: Neural networks trained by gradient descent on a smooth cost function can nevertheless learn in steps: the cost holds on long plateaus and then drops abruptly. Meanwhile, training losses instead follow smooth power laws. Variants of both behaviors occur in architectures with very different microscopic structures, which is the signature of a few relevant collective variables. We show that a symmetry fixes what those variables are: a network layer is a sum over interchangeable units, so relabeling the units leaves it unchanged; given smoothness and the condition that a unit's gradient vanish at the origin, symmetry then enforces a universal leading form for the expansion about the near-zero weights present at the start of training, the quadratic $\Tr[WW^{\top}A(x)]$, in which every architectural detail is confined to a single ``structure matrix" $A(x)$ that we compute for each architecture. Perceptrons, attention layers, mixtures of experts, and convolutions become one model at different $A$. Its training dynamics then close on the ``order parameter" $M=WW^{\top}$ and, whenever the data matrices share an eigenbasis, reduce to a Lotka--Volterra equation whose modes switch on one after another. The smaller the initial weights, the further apart the switch-on times, and the plateaus appear as a singular limit of a smooth flow; when many modes are unresolved the same events merge into a power law in training time whose exponent the theory predicts. We confirm both numerically across training methods and architectures.

</details>


### [161] [Where You Measure Decides What You Measure: Position Selection in Ablation-Based SAE Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13337)
*Valentin Noël*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Current sparse autoencoder evaluation protocols suffer from measurement position bias - when comparing different dictionaries, they're often measured at different tokens due to automatic selection of where latents fire hardest, making comparisons unreliable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper identifies a critical flaw in how sparse autoencoders (SAEs) are evaluated: current protocols automatically measure latent effects at tokens where the latent fires hardest, but this choice is determined by the dictionary itself rather than standardized across comparisons. This creates unreliable comparisons between different SAEs since they often measure the same latent at different positions.

Method: The authors analyze two Google-released SAEs for the same model, matching their latents by decoder similarity, and show they pick different measurement tokens. They then train six SAEs from the same initialization with different fitting choices, allowing them to compare the same latent across dictionaries. They propose a corrected evaluation protocol that requires reporting both causal numbers and their measurement positions.

Result: Even among latents that two dictionaries encode nearly identically, a large share pick different tokens for measurement. When measured at the same token (through controlled experiments), the variance attributed to dictionary disagreement drops from 7.6-11.9% to near zero. This problem worsens with more evaluation data and larger corpora.

Conclusion: Current SAE evaluation protocols are fundamentally flawed because they conflate dictionary properties with measurement positions. A simple one-line code correction is needed: researchers must report both causal numbers and their measurement positions to enable valid comparisons across papers. Without this, reported results describe the measurement token as much as the latent being measured.

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders are meant to name the things a language model computes, and the usual way to check that a latent matters is to switch it off and see what changes. But a latent fires at many tokens, and the effect has to be measured at one of them. The convention is to measure where the latent fires hardest. That choice is almost never reported, and it is not made by the experimenter: it is made by the dictionary under evaluation. Change the dictionary and the measurement moves to a different token. We show this is not a detail. Take two sparse autoencoders released by Google for the same model and match their latents by decoder similarity: even among the pairs the two dictionaries encode almost identically, they pick different tokens for a large share of them. Two dictionaries compared under the usual protocol are therefore very often compared at different places. To separate the convention from the dictionaries we train six autoencoders from one initialisation, differing only in fitting choices, so that a latent means the same thing in each. Most of the variance such a comparison reads as "these dictionaries disagree about this latent" turns out to be the position instead: it falls from 7.6% and 11.9% of variance to near zero once every dictionary is measured at the same token. More evaluation data does not rescue it. Across a sixteenfold range of corpus sizes the dictionaries agree less about where to measure, not more, so the problem grows with scale. The correction is one line of evaluation code. We give the protocol an ablation-based causal number must report to be comparable across papers, and an audit of five published papers against it. In short: a causal number reported without its position describes the token it was taken at as much as the latent it was taken from.

</details>


### [162] [Simulation-to-real transfer learning for infrared spectroscopic chemical sensing and analysis from molecules to complex samples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13341)
*Yusen Tan,Yixuan Chen,Zheng Fang,Pan Liu,Yifan Li,Qinyu Guo,Zhedong Lin,Yuqiang Li,Xiangxiang Zeng,Tong Wang,Jun Xia*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: UltraIR is a foundation model for IR spectroscopy that uses transfer learning from simulated spectra to outperform traditional methods across multiple chemical sensing tasks with limited experimental data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional IR spectroscopy interpretation is labor-intensive, requires expert knowledge, and doesn't scale well. Machine learning methods are typically task-specific, require large labeled datasets, and don't transfer well across different analytical objectives and datasets.

Method: Developed UltraIR, a foundation model with >100M parameters pretrained on ~60M simulated IR spectra using spectral reconstruction, molecular fingerprint similarity alignment, and functional-group prediction. The model is then adapted to downstream tasks with task-specific labels.

Result: UltraIR outperforms conventional machine learning and task-specific deep learning baselines across multiple domains: functional-group prediction, molecular structure elucidation, physicochemical property prediction, mixture analysis, bacterial classification, medicinal herb traceability, microplastics classification, and soil property prediction. It performs well with limited labeled experimental data and shows zero-shot inference capabilities across different instruments and laboratories.

Conclusion: UltraIR provides a route to adaptable, data-efficient chemical sensing for complex real-world samples by enabling simulation-to-real transfer learning, addressing the scalability and generalization challenges of traditional IR spectroscopy interpretation methods.

Abstract: Infrared (IR) spectroscopy is widely used for chemical sensing, but extracting reliable chemical information from spectra remains challenging. Conventional interpretation is labor-intensive, relies on prior knowledge and reference spectra, and is difficult to scale, whereas most machine-learning methods are tailored to individual tasks or datasets, require large labeled training sets, and transfer poorly across analytical objectives and experimental datasets. Here we introduce UltraIR, a foundation model for IR spectroscopy with more than 100 million parameters that enables simulation-to-real transfer learning for chemical sensing and analysis from molecules to complex samples. UltraIR is pretrained on approximately 60 million simulated IR spectra using spectral reconstruction, molecular fingerprint similarity alignment, and functional-group prediction, then adapted to downstream objectives with task-specific labels or targets. Across functional-group prediction, molecular structure elucidation, physicochemical property prediction, mixture-component identification and quantification, bacterial classification, medicinal-herb geographic origin traceability and constituent quantification, microplastics classification, and soil property prediction, UltraIR outperforms conventional machine-learning and task-specific deep-learning baselines. It performs strongly with limited labeled experimental spectra and in zero-shot inference for the same analytical task across Fourier-transform infrared spectrometers and laboratories, providing a route to adaptable, data-efficient chemical sensing from complex real-world samples.

</details>


### [163] [When Local Variance Optimality Is Not Enough: RoPE-Aligned Q/K Rotations for Dynamic 4-Bit Quantisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13365)
*Shuhan Wang,Yilin Luo,Nan Xu,Chi Wang Cheung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Rotation-based post-training quantization with RoPE shows that pairwise orthogonal transforms respecting RoPE's 2D frequency decomposition don't outperform full-head mixing, and surrogate optimality doesn't guarantee reduced quantization error when misaligned with the quantizer's scale-setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates whether applying orthogonal transforms that respect RoPE's partitioning of attention heads into two-dimensional frequency pairs can improve quantization performance compared to full-head mixing, given that RoPE's structure suggests a more targeted approach might be beneficial.

Method: The authors prove that for distinct frequencies, no other single-head orthogonal map commutes with RoPE beyond per-pair rotations. They derive the rotation angle that minimizes channel variance under a pooled-covariance, position-averaged surrogate, and evaluate this configuration in dynamic W4A4KV4 quantization settings across multiple checkpoints.

Result: The head-shared pairwise configuration does not improve accuracy in dynamic W4A4KV4 quantization. Replacing full-head Hadamard with pairwise rotation increases perplexity at both short and long context lengths. While composing pairwise rotation with Hadamard meets certain PPL criteria, pairwise-only transforms underperform full-head mixing. Interpolation experiments show that K range, relative quantization error, and perplexity degradation decrease as mixing support increases from two-channel to full-head.

Conclusion: Optimality for a structured surrogate (like the position-averaged covariance objective) does not guarantee reduced quantization error when the surrogate and mixing support are misaligned with the quantizer's scale-setting statistic. Full-head mixing generally outperforms pairwise approaches despite RoPE's frequency decomposition structure.

Abstract: Rotation-based post-training quantisation commonly applies an orthogonal transform across an entire attention head to reduce outlier-induced error. RoPE instead partitions each head into two-dimensional frequency pairs, raising the question of whether a transform respecting this decomposition can improve on full-head mixing. Prior work has established the per-pair rotations that commute with RoPE. We state the converse result that, for distinct frequencies, no other single-head orthogonal map commutes with RoPE. For the head-shared parameterisation used in our experiments, we then derive the rotation angle that minimises the larger channel variance under a pooled-covariance, position-averaged surrogate and verify that the implementation attains its analytic minimum. The evaluated head-shared pairwise configuration does not improve accuracy in the tested dynamic W4A4KV4 setting. Across four checkpoints, replacing the full-head Hadamard with this configuration increases perplexity at both short and long context lengths. Composing the pairwise rotation with the Hadamard satisfies the selected $\pm0.05$-PPL interval criterion under the default estimator. Estimating the shared angle from K alone improves pairwise-only on every checkpoint but does not close its gap to full-head mixing. The analytic objective controls a position-averaged second moment of a pooled calibration covariance, whereas the dynamic quantiser sets its step from a tokenwise group range. The pairwise transform also has only two-channel mixing support. Along a controlled interpolation from two-channel to full-head mixing, K range, relative quantisation error, and perplexity degradation decrease as support increases. These results show that optimality for a structured surrogate need not reduce quantisation error when the surrogate and mixing support are misaligned with the quantiser's scale-setting statistic.

</details>


### [164] [Reduced Matrix Multiplication: Input-Adaptive Matrix-Product Reduction for LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13426)
*Zixuan Lan,Yanhong Li,Jiawei Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RMM is a training-free, input-adaptive inference method that reduces Transformer computational cost by selecting informative slices in matrix multiplications, providing smooth accuracy-efficiency trade-offs across various models and tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformer language models achieve strong performance but incur substantial inference cost due to repeated high-dimensional matrix multiplications, creating a need for efficient inference methods that don't require model retraining.

Method: Proposes Reduced Matrix Multiplication (RMM), which reduces Transformer matrix products by adaptively selecting informative slices along contraction dimensions during inference, without modifying model weights. Uses retention-ratio control for smooth accuracy-efficiency trade-off.

Result: RMM works across language models (1B to 70B parameters), showing reduction tolerance depends on model family, task, component, and retention ratio. It remains robust across discriminative, autoregressive generation, and long-context settings, with attention computations being substantially more reducible than MLP components. Practical runtime gains demonstrated on NVIDIA A100.

Conclusion: RMM is a scalable direction for input-adaptive inference-time optimization that provides computational savings across various Transformer models while maintaining robustness across different tasks and contexts.

Abstract: Transformer-based language models achieve strong performance but incur substantial inference cost due to repeated high-dimensional matrix multiplications. We propose Reduced Matrix Multiplication (RMM), a training-free, input-adaptive inference method that reduces Transformer matrix products by selecting informative slices along their contraction dimensions, without modifying model weights. Under a simple retention-ratio control, RMM provides a smooth and predictable accuracy-efficiency trade-off. Across language models ranging from 1B to 70B parameters, we find that reduction tolerance depends on the model family, task, component, and retention ratio, although it often improves with model scale. Under moderate reduction, RMM remains robust across the evaluated discriminative, autoregressive generation, and long-context settings. We further show that the same principle extends to multimodal vision-language inference. Mechanistic ablations reveal a structural asymmetry within Transformers: attention-side computations are substantially more reducible than MLP components. Finally, wall-clock benchmarks with custom kernels on an NVIDIA A100 show that these computational savings can translate into practical runtime gains, especially at longer sequence lengths. Together, these results position RMM as a scalable direction for input-adaptive inference-time optimization.

</details>


### [165] [Symmetry-Breaking De Novo Crystal Generation via Markovian Jump Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13457)
*Van Khoa Nguyen,Alexandros Kalousis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel diffusion-based framework called Symmetry-breaking Crystal Diffusion (SbCD) generates complete crystallographic specifications by reversing from lowest-symmetry priors through Markovian jump-diffusion processes, outperforming symmetry-preserving methods on benchmark datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing generative models for crystals struggle to produce complete crystallographic specifications and fail to capture global symmetry and structural dependencies. Current approaches only generate crystals up to site symmetries and rely on empirical sampling of space groups, lacking a principled way to handle symmetry relationships in the generative process.

Method: Proposes Symmetry-breaking Crystal Diffusion (SbCD), a diffusion-based framework inspired by spontaneous symmetry breaking in physics. Uses a Markovian jump-diffusion process to model symmetry-breaking dynamics, enabling traversal between different space groups. The method generates full structure specifications by reversing from the lowest-symmetry priors, explicitly incorporating inter-space-group transitions into the generative process.

Result: In de novo generation experiments on MP20 and MPTS-52 datasets, SbCD outperforms its symmetry-preserving counterpart by a substantial margin, demonstrating superior performance in generating crystalline materials.

Conclusion: SbCD offers a promising, physically motivated approach to generative modeling of crystalline materials by explicitly handling symmetry transitions, providing a principled alternative to existing methods that rely on empirical space group sampling.

Abstract: Generating crystals has recently attracted significant interest due to their broad applications in materials science. However, existing generative models struggle to produce complete crystallographic specifications, limiting their ability to capture global symmetry and structural dependencies. In particular, current state-of-the-art approaches generate crystals only up to site symmetries and rely on sampling space groups from empirical distributions during generation. Inspired by \emph{spontaneous symmetry breaking} in physics, where crystals break symmetries under external conditions, we propose a novel diffusion-based framework that generates full structure specifications by reversing from the lowest-symmetry priors. Our method leverages a Markovian jump-diffusion process to model these symmetry-breaking dynamics, enabling it to traverse different space groups in a physically motivated manner. Our model, dubbed \emph{Symmetry-breaking Crystal Diffusion} (SbCD), introduces a principled approach to explicitly incorporate inter-space-group transitions into the generative process. In de novo generation experiments on MP20 and MPTS-52, SbCD outperforms its symmetry-preserving counterpart by a substantial margin, offering a promising perspective for generative modeling of crystalline materials.

</details>


### [166] [Doubly Robust Estimation of Causal Effect on CVR with Targeted Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13461)
*Jiayi Dan,Bo Li,Lu Deng,Yong Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A new doubly robust causal effect estimator for chain-structured outcomes like post-click conversion rate (CVR) that addresses sample selection bias through semiparametric theory with better convergence rates and practical stability via targeted regularization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Directly applying existing causal inference methods to clicked samples for CVR estimation introduces sample selection bias and increased variance due to excluding non-click data. Current CVR prediction methods using "ideal loss" don't guarantee unbiasedness of the final estimator, creating a need for robust causal estimation methods.

Method: Developed a new doubly robust causal effect estimator using semiparametric theory for chain-structured outcomes like CVR. The method achieves faster convergence rates than nuisance parameter estimation and is robust with flexible nonparametric estimators. Introduced a framework based on targeted regularization for improved numerical stability and practical applicability.

Result: Extensive experiments on synthetic and real-world data demonstrate the method's effectiveness and robustness. Naive combination of loss debiasing with standard causal estimators underperforms compared to their new method, highlighting the necessity of specialized estimators for CVR-style objectives with solid theoretical guarantees.

Conclusion: The proposed doubly robust estimator provides a theoretically sound solution to CVR causal effect estimation, overcoming sample selection bias issues while maintaining practical applicability through targeted regularization, offering superior performance over existing approaches.

Abstract: Post-click conversion rate (CVR) is a key metric in various scenarios including e-commerce and advertising, reflecting the efficiency and user experience in the second stage of the conversion process. Estimating the causal effect on CVR is therefore of great practical importance. However, directly applying existing causal inference methods to clicked samples introduces sample selection bias and increased variance due to the exclusion of non-click data. Recent studies on CVR prediction introduce "ideal loss", which optimizes model parameters using an unbiased estimate of the loss over the full sample. Nevertheless, there is no guarantee that unbiasedness of the loss implies unbiasedness of the final estimator.
  We revisit this challenge from the perspective of semiparametric theory. Specifically, we develop a new doubly robust causal effect estimator for chain-structured outcomes such as CVR, and derive its theoretical properties in detail. It achieves a faster convergence rate compared to nuisance parameters estimation and is therefore more robust when using flexible nonparametric estimators, including neural networks. Based on these theoretical findings, we further design a framework based on targeted regularization to improve numerical stability and practical applicability.
  Extensive experiments on synthetic and real-world data demonstrate the effectiveness and robustness of our method. In addition, we find that naively combining loss debiasing with standard causal estimators underperforms our method, highlighting the necessity of developing the new estimator tailored to this CVR-style objective with solid theoretical guarantees.

</details>


### [167] [Concept Drift Detection and Adaptive Retraining of Malware Classification Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13465)
*Christofer Washington Berruz Chungata,Martin Jurecek,Katerina Potika,William B. Andreopoulos,Mark Stamp*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper analyzes concept drift detection techniques for malware classification, comparing OCSVM, MK-Means, and MMD approaches, showing drift-aware retraining maintains accuracy while reducing computational overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine learning models for malware detection suffer performance degradation from concept drift as attackers constantly modify malware. There's a need for automated concept drift detection to enable efficient model retraining only when necessary.

Method: Compared three concept drift detection techniques: novel OCSVM approach, MK-Means, and MMD. Tested on four ML models (MLP, Random Forest, SVM, XGBoost) across three scenarios: static (no retraining), periodic (constant retraining), and drift-aware (retraining only when drift detected). Used Pareto Front analysis for accuracy vs. efficiency tradeoffs.

Result: All three drift detection techniques achieved classification accuracy comparable to periodic retraining while requiring significantly fewer model retrainings. OCSVM-based drift detection generally outperformed MK-Means and MMD approaches.

Conclusion: Concept drift in malware classification can be accurately detected, enabling drift-aware retraining that maintains accuracy while improving training efficiency. OCSVM shows particular promise for this application.

Abstract: Concept drift refers to changes over time in the statistical properties of data, as compared to the data that was used to train a learning model. Machine learning models for malware detection or classification are particularly susceptible to performance degradation caused by concept drift, as attackers constantly modify existing malware. In this chapter, we analyze two machine learning-based approaches to automated concept drift detection-a novel approach based on One-Class Support Vector Machines (OCSVM) and a previously-studied technique based on Minibatch K-Means (MK-Means). For comparison we also consider Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD), a statistical technique for detecting changes in multidimensional data. We conduct an extensive series of experiments comparing the effectiveness of four learning models, namely, Multilayer Perceptron, Random Forest, Support Vector Machines, and eXtreme Gradient Boosting. For each of these models, we consider three distinct scenarios: A static scenario where no model retraining occurs, a periodic scenario where models are constantly retrained irrespective of concept drift, and a drift-aware scenario where models are only retrained when concept drift is detected. Under the drift-aware scenario, we analyze the tradeoff between accuracy and training efficiency using Pareto Front analysis. We find that all three concept drift detection techniques achieve classification accuracy comparable to periodic retraining, while offering substantially greater efficiency in terms of the number of models that must be retrained. In addition, drift-aware retraining based on our OCSVM technique generally outperforms the MK-Means and MMD approaches. Overall, these results provide strong evidence that we can accurately detect concept drift in malware classification models.

</details>


### [168] [Active-Trace Complexity Bounds for Moreau--Yosida Unadjusted Langevin Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13467)
*Yuchen Xin,Zhihua Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MYULA (Moreau-Yosida unadjusted Langevin algorithm) achieves faster convergence for nonsmooth composite targets by controlling discretization error via reference active trace instead of global curvature bounds, improving complexity from $\widetilde{O}(\varepsilon^{-3})$ to $\widetilde{O}(\varepsilon^{-2})$ for structured penalties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Langevin algorithms for sampling from nonsmooth composite distributions typically suffer from poor complexity due to global curvature bounds. The paper aims to show that for structured penalties (piecewise-linear, lasso, group, TV), the actual discretization error can be much smaller than predicted by worst-case bounds.

Method: Uses MYULA with Moreau envelope smoothing to handle nonsmooth convex term $g$. Analyzes discretization error through reference active trace $B_{\mathrm{ref}}$ (average of trace of Hessian of Moreau envelope along heat substep) rather than global bound $d/\lambda$. Provides curvature-tube estimates for structured penalties.

Result: Establishes iteration complexity bound: $N \lesssim \frac{1}{m}[L_f + \frac{\tau_f+G^2+B_{\mathrm{ref}}}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{alg}}^2} + \frac{M_\lambda}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{alg}}}]$ for $W_2$ convergence to smoothed target. Shows Moreau-bias bound: $\sqrt{m}W_2(\pi_\lambda,\pi) \leq \frac{G^2\lambda}{4}$. For structured penalties, $B_{\mathrm{ref}}$ becomes independent of $\lambda$, improving complexity from $\widetilde{O}(\varepsilon^{-3})$ to $\widetilde{O}(\varepsilon^{-2})$.

Conclusion: MYULA achieves better complexity than predicted by worst-case bounds for structured nonsmooth penalties by exploiting local curvature properties. The reference active trace provides a more accurate error control mechanism, leading to $\widetilde{O}(\varepsilon^{-2})$ convergence for practical penalties like lasso and total variation.

Abstract: We study the Moreau--Yosida unadjusted Langevin algorithm (MYULA) for the nonsmooth composite target \[ π(dx)\propto \exp\{-f(x)-g(x)\}\,dx, \qquad x\in\mathbb R^d, \] where \(f\) is \(m\)-strongly convex with \(L_f\)-Lipschitz gradient and \(g\) is convex and \(G\)-Lipschitz. Let \(g_λ\) be the Moreau envelope of \(g\), \(π_λ\) the corresponding smoothed target, and \(a_λ=\operatorname{tr}H_λ\), where \(H_λ\) is the a.e./weak Hessian of \(g_λ\). We show that the leading MYULA discretization error is controlled by the reference active trace \(B_{\mathrm{ref}}\), the average of \(a_λ\) along the heat substep of one MYULA update started from \(π_λ\), rather than by the global curvature bound \(d/λ\). If \(M_λ\) is an a.e. upper bound for \(a_λ\), then, up to logarithmic factors, \[ N \lesssim \frac{1}{m} \left[ L_f + \frac{ τ_f+G^2+B_{\mathrm{ref}} }{ \varepsilon_{\mathrm{alg}}^2 } + \frac{M_λ}{\varepsilon_{\mathrm{alg}}} \right], \qquad τ_f:= \sup_x\operatorname{tr}\nabla^2 f(x), \] iterations suffice to ensure \(\sqrt m\,W_2(μ_N,π_λ)\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{alg}}\), where \(μ_N\) is the law of the \(N\)-th iterate and \(W_2\) is the quadratic Wasserstein distance. We also prove the Moreau-bias bound \[ \sqrt m\,W_2(π_λ,π) \leq \frac{G^2λ}{4}. \] Thus, choosing \(λ\asymp\varepsilon/G^2\) gives an end-to-end guarantee for \(π\). The universal estimate \(B_{\mathrm{ref}}\leq d/λ\) yields \(\widetilde O(\varepsilon^{-3})\) accuracy dependence. For the structured piecewise-linear, lasso-type, group, and total-variation penalties considered here, curvature--tube estimates make \(B_{\mathrm{ref}}\) independent of \(λ\), yielding \(\widetilde O(\varepsilon^{-2})\) for the same classical MYULA kernel.

</details>


### [169] [Synthetic Persona Pretraining: Alignment from Token Zero](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13482)
*Julian Minder,Viktor Moskvoretskii,Raghav Singhal,Difan Jiao,Andy Arditi,Shaobo Cui,Yiderigun Borjigin,Kartik Bali,Stefan Krsteski,Harsh Raj,Huu Nguyen,Jannik Brinkmann,Ashton Anderson,Roland Aydin,Robert West*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SPP integrates assistant persona and values directly during pretraining from the start rather than post-training, improving alignment robustness and constitution following while preserving capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI alignment approaches introduce assistant persona and values only after pretraining, creating superficial overlays rather than deeply rooted values, which makes subsequent misalignment easier.

Method: Synthetic Persona Pretraining (SPP): 1) Annotates pretraining documents with value-aligned first-person reflections from a normative constitution; 2) Pretrains via cross-entropy on both original documents and their reflections; 3) Post-trains on user-assistant dialogue to bind persona to assistant identity.

Result: SPP improves constitution following, jailbreak robustness, and reduces misalignment rate in OOD moral dilemmas while preserving capabilities. Early intervention is crucial - late SPP introduction yields weaker results. Benefits increase with pretraining budget and require persona binding.

Conclusion: Shaping values early during pretraining is critical for alignment, and SPP establishes effective pretraining-time persona interventions to deeply root desired values from the start rather than as superficial overlays.

Abstract: As language-model-based AI is increasingly deployed in autonomous settings, aligning its goals and values with those of humans becomes critical. Today, alignment, and the assistant identity itself, are typically introduced only after pretraining, once behavioral priors are already established. This can make values a thin overlay, rather than deeply rooted, and facilitate subsequent misalignment. Pursuing a different paradigm, we introduce Synthetic Persona Pretraining (SPP), which installs the desired assistant persona from token zero in pretraining. First, we annotate pretraining documents with value-aligned first-person reflections derived from a normative value constitution. Second, we pretrain via the standard cross-entropy loss on standard pretraining documents as well as their reflections, which installs the desired persona among a multitude of other personas. Finally, we post-train on user-assistant dialogue data, which binds this desired persona to the assistant identity, a process we call persona binding. By pretraining models up to 3B parameters on 500B tokens, we show that SPP improves constitution following and jailbreak robustness, and reduces the misalignment rate in out-of-distribution moral dilemmas, while preserving capabilities. Early intervention matters: compared with alignment from token zero, introducing SPP only at the end of pretraining yields weaker constitution adherence, does not shift value priorities, and leads to less aligned choices in dilemmas. This advantage depends on persona binding and, importantly, increases with pretraining budget. Overall, our results show that shaping values early is critical for alignment and establish pretraining-time persona interventions as an effective approach to do so.

</details>


### [170] [Sparse Orthogonal Regression Technique: A Spectral Framework for Equation Discovery, Approximation, and Integration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13504)
*Sabin Roman,Ljupco Todorovski,Saso Dzeroski*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SORT is a sparse spectral regression method for learning orthonormal-basis expansions from noisy, irregular data, enabling data-driven ODE discovery without explicit quadrature by using L1-regularized regression on coefficient expansions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods for data-driven discovery of ordinary differential equations (like symbolic regression, grammar-based discovery, and SINDy) can be brittle when finite libraries miss problem-specific nonlinearities. There's a need for a more stable approach that handles sparse sampling, noisy derivative estimates, and representation mismatch better.

Method: Sparse Orthogonal Regression Technique (SORT) uses L1-regularized regression to directly estimate expansion coefficients from observations in chosen orthogonal bases, avoiding explicit quadrature or analytic inner-product evaluation. Vector fields are represented as sparse coefficient expansions in orthonormal bases.

Result: SORT matches or improves upon library-based sparse-regression baselines when the basis is well-adapted, shows more stable degradation under sparse sampling and noisy derivatives, and reveals that dominant low-order coefficients persist as model order increases. It successfully recovers compact spectral representations that can guide searches for simpler analytic forms.

Conclusion: SORT provides a reusable intermediate representation that shifts the modeling problem from brittle term selection to basis design, making basis design an explicit part of scientific modeling. It supports system identification, approximation, and integration while offering stable performance under challenging data conditions.

Abstract: We develop the Sparse Orthogonal Regression Technique (SORT), a sparse spectral framework for learning orthonormal-basis expansions from noisy and irregularly sampled data. SORT estimates expansion coefficients directly from observations using L1-regularized regression, avoiding explicit quadrature or analytic inner-product evaluation. The central application is data-driven discovery of ordinary differential equations: vector fields are represented in chosen orthogonal bases and learned as sparse coefficient expansions. This provides a complementary route to symbolic regression, grammar-based discovery, and SINDy-style sparse identification by first recovering a compact spectral representation, which can later guide searches for simpler analytic forms. Across the dynamical-system experiments, SORT matches or improves upon library-based sparse-regression baselines when the basis is well adapted to the problem, and shows more stable degradation under sparse sampling, noisy derivative estimates, and representation mismatch. Specific examples illustrate why this representation is useful: if a finite library misses the problem-specific nonlinearity, the resulting model can fail. SORT is not immune to mismatch, but it shifts the problem away from brittle selection among generic terms to basis design adapted to the problem domain. The experiments also show that dominant low-order coefficients persist as model order increases, supporting order-consistent model growth. Beyond equation discovery, the same learned expansion supports nonlinear approximation and estimation of complex, high-dimensional integrals by coefficient readout. Overall, SORT provides a reusable intermediate representation for system identification, approximation, and integration, while making basis design an explicit part of the scientific modeling problem.

</details>


### [171] [Intern-S2-Preview: Scientific Agentic Foundation Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13505)
*Lei Bai,Jiaqi Cao,Chiyu Chen,Guanzhou Chen,Kai Chen,Guangran Cheng,Erfei Cui,Xuanlang Dai,Shengyuan Ding,Shangheng Du,Yanhui Duan,Yue Fan,Youqing Fang,Quan Gan,Yuanyuan Gao,Jiaye Ge,Lixin Gu,Yuzhe Gu,Qipeng Guo,Junjun He,Xin Hong,Ming Hu,Zhouqi Hua,Haian Huang,Junhao Huang,Zixian Huang,Minxi Jin,Lingkai Kong,Alexander Lam,Zehao Li,Zonglin Li,Tianhao Liang,Dahua Lin,Junyao Lin,Tianyang Lin,Zhouhan Lin,Jiangning Liu,Jin Liu,Kuikun Liu,Wenran Liu,Yifei Liu,Yuhong Liu,Yuhong Liu,Zhoumianze Liu,Ziyan Liu,Ziyu Liu,Haijun Lv,Han Lv,Chengqi Lyu,Le Ma,Ningsheng Ma,Zerun Ma,Haoyang Peng,Runyu Peng,Jifei Shan,Zixin Shang,Kou Shi,Xiang Shi,Qisheng Su,Xuerui Su,Hao Sun,Xiao Sun,Yanan Sun,Yu Sun,Huanze Tang,Yinghao Tang,Wenhui Tian,Zhongbo Tian,Bingli Wang,Haomin Wang,Jiarui Wang,Jingzhi Wang,Rui Wang,Xiquan Wang,Yi Wang,Zhecan Wang,Ziyi Wang,Zun Wang,Rubin Wei,Lianyi Wu,Wen Wu,Yue Wu,Yuhan Wu,Zhenyu Wu,Zijian Wu,Shuhao Xing,Jun Xu,Xingle Xu,Xuenan Xu,Xiangchao Yan,Ziang Yan,Bowen Yang,Danni Yang,Lin Yang,Zhiqi Yang,Qian Yao,Haochen Ye,Peng Ye,Jinhui Yin,Jiashuo Yu,Dingbo Yuan,Fei Yuan,Yuhang Zang,Bo Zhang,Chao Zhang,Chen Zhang,Hongjie Zhang,Junming Zhang,Wenlong Zhang,Wenwei Zhang,Yiming Zhang,Zhuo Zhang,Ziyang Zhang,Haiteng Zhao,Penghao Zhao,Yibo Zhao,Zhonghan Zhao,Zhihang Zhong,Bowen Zhou,Peiheng Zhou,Xin Zhou,Xinyu Zhou,Yunhua Zhou,Dongsheng Zhu,Yicheng Zou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Intern-S2-Preview is a series of scientific agentic foundation models designed for multimodal scientific reasoning, generation, and long-horizon tasks, featuring a 397B parameter model with specialized time series modules and memory-augmented extensions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific discovery increasingly requires AI systems that can reason over heterogeneous scientific evidence, interact with scientific tools/environments, and sustain progress across long task horizons. Current systems lack the multimodal understanding, reasoning capabilities, and task persistence needed for complex scientific workflows.

Method: The approach uses: 1) Scientific multimodal pre-training on rendered documents, image-text data, and scientific corpora; 2) Unified post-training pipeline including supervised fine-tuning, scalable multi-task RL, agentic RL (black/white-box), and on-policy distillation; 3) Practical techniques for stability/efficiency (partial rollout with off-policy correction, adaptive length regularization, etc.); 4) Architecture extensions: time series modeling for numerical forecasting and Memory Decoder for rapid specialization without modifying the frozen 397B backbone.

Result: Intern-S2-Preview-397B achieves competitive or leading results across scientific, multimodal, agentic, and general-purpose benchmarks. Time series modules improve scientific signal understanding/forecasting on SciTS. The separate Intern-MemDec-4B extension improves Biology-Instructions average score from 56.92 to 60.32 without modifying the frozen 397B backbone.

Conclusion: Intern-S2-Preview demonstrates effective scientific agentic foundation models that combine multimodal understanding, reasoning capabilities, and specialized extensions for scientific tasks. The separate memory-augmented approach enables rapid specialization while preserving the main model's capabilities, offering a scalable framework for scientific AI systems.

Abstract: Scientific discovery increasingly requires AI systems that can reason over scientific evidence of heterogeneous modalities, interact with scientific tools and environments, and sustain progress across long task horizons. We present Intern-S2-Preview, a series of scientific agentic foundation models designed to support multimodal scientific understanding, reasoning, generation, and long-horizon tasks. The training pipeline begins with scientific multimodal pre-training over rendered scientific documents, interleaved image-text data, and diverse scientific corpora. Starting from the pretrained checkpoint, we apply a unified post-training pipeline consisting of supervised fine-tuning, scalable multi-task reinforcement learning (RL), black- and white-box agentic RL, and on-policy distillation. This pipeline is supported by practical techniques that improve rollout and training stability and efficiency, including partial rollout with off-policy correction, adaptive length regularization, online speculative decoding, robust multi-task optimization, and trace-aware experience assembly for agentic tasks. At the architecture level, Intern-S2-Preview-397B extends time series modelling from efficient long-sequence understanding to numerical forecasting, while Memory Decoder is studied as a separate memory-augmented path for rapid scientific specialization without modifying the frozen 397B backbone. Evaluations across scientific, multimodal, agentic, and general-purpose benchmarks show that Intern-S2-Preview-397B achieves competitive or leading results in multiple settings. The time series modules improve scientific signal understanding and forecasting on SciTS, while the separate Intern-MemDec-4B extension improves the Biology-Instructions average score from 56.92 to 60.32 without modifying the frozen 397B backbone.

</details>


### [172] [Intervention-Aware Clinical World Model for Post-Op Outcome Forecasting in Cardiology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13518)
*Yunsung Chung,Yingshuo Liu,Abboud F. Hassan,Han Feng,Mary M. Maleckar,Nassir Marrouche,Jihun Hamm*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An intervention-aware clinical world model that represents patient recovery as evolving trajectories rather than one-step predictions, using structured latent states updated by time-ordered post-intervention events, applied to atrial fibrillation ablation with strong predictive performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
